Singular sets in noncollapsed Ricci flow limit spaces
Hanbing Fang, and, Yu Li

Abstract
In this paper, we study the singular set 𝒮 \mathcal{S} of a noncollapsed Ricci flow limit space, arising as the pointed Gromov–Hausdorff limit of a sequence of closed Ricci flows with uniformly bounded entropy. The singular set 𝒮 \mathcal{S} admits a natural stratification: 𝒮 0 ⊂ 𝒮 1 ⊂ ⋯ ⊂ 𝒮 n − 2 = 𝒮 , \mathcal{S}^{0}\subset\mathcal{S}^{1}\subset\cdots\subset\mathcal{S}^{n-2}=\mathcal{S}, where a point z ∈ 𝒮 k z\in\mathcal{S}^{k} if and only if no tangent flow at z z is ( k + 1 ) (k+1) -symmetric. In general, the Minkowski dimension of 𝒮 k \mathcal{S}^{k} with respect to the spacetime distance is at most k k . We show that the subset 𝒮 qc k ⊂ 𝒮 k \mathcal{S}^{k}_{\mathrm{qc}}\subset\mathcal{S}^{k} , consisting of points where some tangent flow is given by a standard cylinder or its quotient, is parabolic k k -rectifiable. In dimension four, we prove the stronger statement that each stratum 𝒮 k \mathcal{S}^{k} is parabolic k k -rectifiable for k ∈ { 0 , 1 , 2 } k\in\{0,1,2\} . Furthermore, we establish a sharp uniform ℋ 2 \mathscr{H}^{2} -volume bound for 𝒮 \mathcal{S} and show that, up to a set of ℋ 2 \mathscr{H}^{2} -measure zero, the tangent flow at any point in 𝒮 \mathcal{S} is backward unique. In addition, we derive L 1 L^{1} -curvature bounds for four-dimensional closed Ricci flows.



1
Introduction

Ricci flow, introduced by Richard Hamilton in the early 1980s, is defined by the evolution equation

\partial_{t}g(t)=-2\mathrm{Ric}(g(t)).

Since its inception, Ricci flow has proven to be a powerful tool in geometric analysis, enabling deep insights into the topology and geometry of manifolds.

This paper investigates the pointed Gromov–Hausdorff limit space
(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})
, obtained from a sequence of closed Ricci flows
\mathcal{X}^{i}=\{M_{i}^{n},(g_{i}(t))_{t\in\mathbb{I}^{++}}\}\in\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)
. As introduced in
[
FL25a
]
, the moduli space
\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)
consists of all
n
-dimensional closed Ricci flows defined on
\mathbb{I}^{++}:=[-T,0]
with entropy uniformly bounded below by
-Y
(see Definition
2.4
). Equipped with a suitable spacetime distance
d^{*}_{i}
(see Definition
2.10
), we have the convergence

\displaystyle(M_{i}\times\mathbb{I},d^{*}_{i},p_{i}^{*},\mathfrak{t}_{i})\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathrm{pGH}\hskip 8.19447pt}(Z,d_{Z},p_{\infty},\mathfrak{t}),

(1.1)

where
\mathbb{I}:=[-0.98T,0]
and
p_{i}^{*}\in M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}
. The limit space
(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})
is referred to as a
noncollapsed Ricci flow limit space
over
\mathbb{I}
. In
[
FL25a
]
, a weak compactness theorem for the moduli space
\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)
(see Theorem
2.11
) and a corresponding structure theory for
(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})
are developed. In particular, the limit space
(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})
is a
parabolic space
(see
[
FL25a
, Definition 3.22]
), meaning that it is a metric space endowed with a time-function
\mathfrak{t}:Z\to\mathbb{I}
satisfying
|\mathfrak{t}(x)-\mathfrak{t}(y)|\leq d_{Z}^{2}(x,y)
for any
x,y\in Z
.

The noncollapsed Ricci flow limit space
(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})
, when restricted to the time interval
\mathbb{I}^{-}:=(-0.98T,0]
, admits the following regular–singular decomposition:

\displaystyle Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}=\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}\sqcup\mathcal{S},

where
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
is a dense open subset of
Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
that carries the structure of a Ricci flow spacetime
(\mathcal{R},\mathfrak{t},\partial_{\mathfrak{t}},g^{Z})
. Furthermore, the convergence in (
1.1
) can be improved to be

(M_{i}\times\mathbb{I},d^{*}_{i},p_{i}^{*},\mathfrak{t}_{i})\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\hat{C}^{\infty}\hskip 8.19447pt}(Z,d_{Z},p_{\infty},\mathfrak{t}).

Roughly speaking,
\hat{C}^{\infty}
means that the convergence is smooth on the regular part
\mathcal{R}
(see Theorem
2.12
and Notation
2.14
for more details). In addition, it is shown in
[
FL25a
, Theorem 1.13]
that the singular set
\mathcal{S}
has Minkowski dimension at most
n-2
.

Understanding the structure of the singular set
\mathcal{S}
is a central problem in the analysis of Ricci flow limit spaces. For any point
z\in\mathcal{S}
, a useful method for probing the local geometry near
z
is blow-up analysis. Specifically, one considers the pointed Gromov–Hausdorff limit, known as a
tangent flow
, of the rescaled spaces
(Z,r_{j}^{-1}d_{Z},z,r_{j}^{-2}(\mathfrak{t}-\mathfrak{t}(z)))
as
r_{j}\searrow 0
. It was shown in
[
FL25a
, Theorem 1.9]
that any such tangent flow is a
Ricci shrinker space
(see Definition
2.15
). Roughly speaking, a Ricci shrinker space is self-similar for
t\in(-\infty,0)
and satisfies the Ricci shrinker equation on its regular part. Moreover, the singular set of each negative time slice in a Ricci shrinker space has Minkowski codimension at least four.

By analyzing the symmetry properties of tangent flows, one obtains a natural stratification of the singular set:

\mathcal{S}^{0}\subset\mathcal{S}^{1}\subset\cdots\subset\mathcal{S}^{n-2}=\mathcal{S},

where a point
z\in\mathcal{S}^{k}
if and only if no tangent flow at
z
is
(k+1)
-symmetric (see Definition
2.18
). Our first main result shows that the Minkowski dimension of each stratum
\mathcal{S}^{k}
is at most
k
.

Theorem 1.1
.

For any
k\in\{0,1,\ldots,n-2\}
, the Minkowski dimension of
\mathcal{S}^{k}
with respect to
d_{Z}
satisfies

\displaystyle\dim_{\mathscr{M}}\mathcal{S}^{k}\leq k.

The proof of Theorem
1.1
appears in Corollary
4.24
, after the necessary technical preparations are established. We also derive quantitative estimates for the singular strata, giving a parabolic analog of the results in
[
CN13
]
.

Although Theorem
1.1
provides a sharp dimension bound, it does not by itself convey detailed geometric information about
\mathcal{S}^{k}
. While blow-up analysis effectively captures the local behavior of singularities, it does not, by itself, describe the global structure of the singular set
\mathcal{S}
. The optimal structural expectation is that each stratum
\mathcal{S}^{k}
resembles a
k
-dimensional smooth manifold in a measure-theoretic or geometric sense. In this paper, we focus on a particularly significant subset
\mathcal{S}^{k}_{\mathrm{qc}}
of
\mathcal{S}^{k}
, consisting of points at which some tangent flow is given by a standard cylinder or its finite quotient. For the precise definition, see Definition
5.25
.

It was shown in
[
FL25b
, Theorem 8.11]
that for any point
z\in\mathcal{S}^{k}_{\mathrm{qc}}
, the tangent flow at
z
is unique. The importance of this subset is highlighted by the decomposition of the top stratum
\mathcal{S}^{n-2}\setminus\mathcal{S}^{n-3}
, which admits the disjoint union:

{\@index{\@indexphrase{$\mathcal{S}^{n-2}_{\mathrm{F}}$}}}\mathcal{S}^{n-2}\setminus\mathcal{S}^{n-3}=\left(\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{qc}}^{n-2}\setminus\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{qc}}^{n-3}\right)\sqcup\mathcal{S}^{n-2}_{\mathrm{F}},

(1.2)

where the set
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{qc}}^{n-2}\setminus\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{qc}}^{n-3}
consists of points
z
whose tangent flow, restricted to time-slice
-1
, is isometric to the standard
\mathbb{R}^{n-2}\times S^{2}
or
\mathbb{R}^{n-2}\times\mathbb{RP}^{2}
. In contrast, any point
z\in\mathcal{S}^{n-2}_{\mathrm{F}}
admits at least one tangent flow given by a static flat cone of the form
\mathbb{R}^{n-4}\times(\mathbb{R}^{4}/\Gamma)\times\mathbb{R}
, where
\Gamma\leqslant\mathrm{O}(4)
acts freely on
S^{3}
.

Moreover, in the four-dimensional case, the classification of three-dimensional Ricci shrinkers (see
[
Ham93
,
Per02
,
Nab10
,
NW08
,
CCZ08
]
) implies that

\mathcal{S}^{1}\setminus\mathcal{S}^{0}=\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{qc}}^{1}\setminus\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{qc}}^{0},

(1.3)

meaning that the entire first stratum consists of quotient cylindrical-type singularities. These results underscore the central role of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{qc}}^{k}
in the structure theory of the singular set.

Before stating the structure theorem for
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{qc}}^{k}
, we first introduce the notion of parabolic
k
-rectifiability. Here,
\mathscr{H}^{k}
denotes the
k
-dimensional Hausdorff measure with respect to
d_{Z}
.

Definition 1.2

(Parabolic
k
-rectifiability)
.

A parabolic space
(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})
is said to be
horizontally parabolic
k
-rectifiable
if, for any
\epsilon>0
, there exists a countable collection of
\mathscr{H}^{k}
-measurable subsets
Z_{i}\subset Z
such that

\mathscr{H}^{k}\left(Z\setminus\bigcup_{i}Z_{i}\right)=0,

and for each
i
, there exists a bi-Lipschitz map
\phi_{i}:Z_{i}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
, where
\mathbb{R}^{k}
is equipped with the standard Euclidean distance, and the time-function
\mathfrak{t}
satisfies

\sqrt{|\mathfrak{t}(x)-\mathfrak{t}(y)|}\leq\epsilon d_{Z}(x,y)\quad\text{for all}\quad x,y\in Z_{i}.

(1.4)

Similarly,
(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})
is said to be
vertically parabolic
k
-rectifiable
if there exists a countable collection of
\mathscr{H}^{k}
-measurable subsets
Z_{i}\subset Z
such that

\mathscr{H}^{k}\left(Z\setminus\bigcup_{i}Z_{i}\right)=0,

and for each
i
, there exists a
time-preserving
, bi-Lipschitz map
\phi_{i}:Z_{i}\to\mathbb{R}^{k-2}\times\mathbb{R}
, where
\mathbb{R}^{k-2}\times\mathbb{R}
is equipped with the standard parabolic distance and the time function is the projection onto the last
\mathbb{R}
.

In general, the space
(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})
is said to be
parabolic
k
-rectifiable
if it can be written as a countable union of subspaces, each of which is horizontally or vertically parabolic
k
-rectifiable.

We are now ready to state our second main result, whose proof will be given in Theorems
5.22
and
5.32
.

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})
be a noncollapsed Ricci flow limit space arising as the pointed Gromov–Hausdorff limit of a sequence in
\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)
. Then, for any
k\in\{0,1,\ldots,n-2\}
, the set
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{qc}}^{k}
is horizontally parabolic
k
-rectifiable with respect to the
d_{Z}
-distance.

The notion of rectifiability plays an important role in geometric measure theory, with wide-ranging applications in geometric analysis and metric geometry. In the classical Euclidean setting, a set
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is
k
-rectifiable if it can be covered, up to a set of Hausdorff
k
-measure zero, by countably many images of Lipschitz maps from subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{k}
into
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Intuitively, this means that
E
can be approximated almost everywhere by pieces of
k
-dimensional
C^{1}
submanifolds. A detailed and systematic exposition of rectifiable sets in the Euclidean setting can be found in
[
Fed69
, Chapter 3]
.

For a general metric space
(X,d)
, the definition is analogous:
X
is
k
-rectifiable if it can be covered, up to a set of Hausdorff
k
-measure zero, by countably many Lipschitz images of subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{k}
into
X
. The concept, formalized by Federer and others in the mid-20th century, serves as a cornerstone for the modern theory of currents, minimal surfaces, and the study of singular sets arising in variational problems.

In the theory of harmonic maps, rectifiability provides a precise description of the structure of singularities. Simon
[
Sim95
]
proved that the singular set of an energy-minimizing harmonic map from an
n
-dimensional manifold is
(n-3)
-rectifiable. Lin
[
Lin99
]
extended this to stationary harmonic maps, showing that their singular sets are
(n-2)
-rectifiable. These foundational results were later refined by Naber and Valtorta
[
NV17
]
, who developed quantitative stratification techniques to show that each singular
k
-stratum is
k
-rectifiable.

In the context of noncollapsed Ricci limit spaces—the Gromov–Hausdorff limits of Riemannian manifolds with uniform lower Ricci curvature bounds and noncollapsing conditions—Cheeger, Jiang, and Naber
[
CJN21
]
established that the
k
-th stratum of the singular set, consisting of all points at which no tangent cone splits off an
\mathbb{R}^{k+1}
, is
k
-rectifiable. Notably, in settings with only a lower Ricci curvature bound, rectifiability is essentially the best one can hope for—the singular set may fail to be manifold-like and may even resemble a Cantor set; see
[
LN20
]
for such examples.

In the setting of mean curvature flow, which is closely related to the present work, Colding and Minicozzi
[
CMI16
]
established a foundational result concerning the structure of the singular set. Specifically, for a mean curvature flow
\{M_{t}\}
of hypersurface in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
with generic singularities—that is, tangent flows at singular points are multiplicity-one shrinking cylinders—they proved that the
k
-th stratum
\mathcal{S}^{k}\setminus\mathcal{S}^{k-1}
can be written as countably many bi-Lipschitz images of subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{k}
.

In fact, the result in
[
CMI16
]
goes further: each such Lipschitz image is shown to be
2
-Hölder with vanishing constant (see
[
CMI16
, Equation (4.16)]
). Consequently, each stratum
\mathcal{S}^{k}
is parabolic
k
-rectifiable in the
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\times\mathbb{R}
equipped with the standard parabolic metric, in the sense of
[
Mat22
, Definition 1.2]
. Building on this, one can further show that
\mathcal{S}^{k}\setminus\mathcal{S}^{k-1}
is locally contained in a
k
-dimensional
C^{1}
-submanifold (see also the recent work
[
SWX25
]
of Sun–Wang–Xue for the improved regularity). The definition of parabolic rectifiability introduced in Definition
1.2
is in part motivated by these results and concepts, adapted to the Ricci flow setting.

Next, we turn to the four-dimensional case, where a more refined structural result for the singular set can be established. Our third main theorem is as follows:

Theorem 1.4
.

Let
(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})
be a noncollapsed Ricci flow limit space arising as the pointed Gromov–Hausdorff limit of a sequence in
\mathcal{M}(4,Y,T)
. Then the following statements hold.

(i)

For each
k\in\{0,1,2\}
, the stratum
\mathcal{S}^{k}
is parabolic
k
-rectifiable.

(ii)

Each connected component of
\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\mathrm{qc}}
is contained in a single time slice.

(iii)

For
\mathscr{H}^{2}
-a.e.
x\in\mathcal{S}
, the tangent flow at
x
is backward unique.

Part (i) of Theorem
1.4
builds on Theorem
1.3
. As observed in the decompositions (
1.2
) and (
1.3
), it suffices to analyze the rectifiability of
\mathcal{S}^{0}
and
\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\mathrm{F}}
. In Proposition
6.1
, we show that
\mathcal{S}^{0}
is a countable set, and hence trivially parabolic
0
-rectifiable. In Theorem
6.22
, we prove that
\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\mathrm{F}}
is vertically parabolic
2
-rectifiable. By construction, any point
z\in\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\mathrm{F}}
has one tangent flow given by
\mathbb{R}^{4}/\Gamma\times\mathbb{R}
, and thus
\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\mathrm{F}}
can be thought of as being contained in a vertical line, justifying the vertical rectifiability.

Part (ii) of the theorem holds in any dimension (see Corollary
5.33
). Since
\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\mathrm{qc}}
is horizontally parabolic
2
-rectifiable by Theorem
1.3
, its time projection into
\mathbb{R}
has zero Lebesgue measure. Therefore, any connected component must be entirely contained in a single time slice. A similar result in the setting of mean curvature flow was established by Colding and Minicozzi in
[
CMI16
, Theorem 1.2]
.

Finally, part (iii) follows from the fact that tangent flows at points in
\mathcal{S}^{k}_{\mathrm{qc}}
are unique (see
[
FL25b
, Theorem 8.11]
), and that
\mathscr{H}^{2}
-a.e.
x\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}
, the negative part of the tangent flow at
x
is unique and given by
\mathbb{R}^{4}/\Gamma\times\mathbb{R}_{-}
. This will be proved in Corollary
6.23
.

Motivated by the results in the four-dimensional case, we propose the following conjecture in general dimension. If true, it would imply—when combined with Theorem
1.3
—that the entire singular set
\mathcal{S}
is parabolic
(n-2)
-rectifiable.

Conjecture 1.5
.

Let
(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})
be a noncollapsed Ricci flow limit space arising as the pointed Gromov–Hausdorff limit of a sequence in
\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)
. Then the set
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{F}}^{n-2}
is vertically parabolic
(n-2)
-rectifiable with respect to the
d_{Z}
-distance. Moreover, for
\mathscr{H}^{n-2}
-a.e.
x\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{F}}^{n-2}
, the tangent flow at
x
is backward unique, and its negative part is given by
\mathbb{R}^{n-4}\times(\mathbb{R}^{4}/\Gamma)\times\mathbb{R}_{-}
.

Next, in the four-dimensional setting, we provide a sharp volume estimate for the singular set.

Theorem 1.6
.

Let
(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})
be a noncollapsed Ricci flow limit space arising as the pointed Gromov–Hausdorff limit of a sequence in
\mathcal{M}(4,Y,T)
. Then for any
z_{0}\in Z
with
\mathfrak{t}(z_{0})-2r_{0}^{2}\in\mathbb{I}^{-}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}^{2}\left(\mathcal{S}\bigcap B^{*}(z_{0},r_{0})\right)\leq C(Y)r_{0}^{6}.

Here,
B^{*}
denotes a metric ball in
Z
. In fact, we prove a more refined estimate than that stated in Theorem
1.6
. Recall that in
[
FL25a
]
, we introduced the quantitative singular strata
\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,k}_{r_{1},r_{2}}
, inspired by the framework in
[
CN13
]
. More precisely, a point
z\in\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,k}_{r_{1},r_{2}}
if and only if
\mathfrak{t}(z)-\epsilon^{-1}r_{2}^{2}\in\mathbb{I}^{-}
and for all
r\in[r_{1},r_{2}]
,
z
is not
(k+1,\epsilon,r)
-symmetric (see Definition
2.4
). A basic identity follows from the definition: for any
L\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{k}=\bigcup_{\epsilon\in(0,L^{-1})}\bigcap_{0<r<\epsilon L}\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,k}_{r,\epsilon L}.

It was shown in
[
FL25a
, Theorem 1.12]
that for any
r\in(0,\epsilon)
,

\displaystyle\left\lvert B^{*}_{r}\left(\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,n-2}_{rr_{0},\epsilon r_{0}}\right)\bigcap B^{*}(z_{0},r_{0})\right\rvert\leq C(n,Y,\epsilon)r^{4-\epsilon}r_{0}^{n+2}

provided that
\mathfrak{t}(z_{0})-2r_{0}^{2}\in\mathbb{I}^{-}
. Moreover, for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\left\lvert B^{*}_{r}\left(\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,n-2}_{rr_{0},\epsilon r_{0}}\right)\bigcap B^{*}(z_{0},r_{0})\bigcap Z_{t}\right\rvert_{t}\leq C(n,Y,\epsilon)r^{2-\epsilon}r_{0}^{n}.

In this paper, we sharpen these estimates in the four-dimensional case.

Theorem 1.7
.

Under the same assumptions of Theorem
1.6
, for
k\in\{1,2\}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\lvert B^{*}_{r}\left(\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,k}_{rr_{0},\epsilon r_{0}}\right)\bigcap B^{*}(z_{0},r_{0})\right\rvert\leq C(Y,\epsilon)r^{6-k}r_{0}^{6}

for any
r\in(0,\epsilon)
. In particular, we have

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}^{k}\left(\bigcap_{0<r<\epsilon}\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,k}_{rr_{0},\epsilon r_{0}}\bigcap B^{*}(z_{0},r_{0})\right)\leq C(Y,\epsilon)r_{0}^{6}.

Moreover, for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
r\in(0,\epsilon)
,

\displaystyle\left\lvert B^{*}_{r}\left(\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,k}_{rr_{0},\epsilon r_{0}}\right)\bigcap B^{*}(z_{0},r_{0})\bigcap Z_{t}\right\rvert_{t}\leq C(Y,\epsilon)r^{4-k}r_{0}^{4}.

The proof of Theorem
1.7
will be provided in Theorems
6.24
,
6.25
and
6.27
. The volume estimate in Theorem
1.6
follows immediately from Theorem
1.7
, since

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}\bigcap B^{*}(z_{0},r_{0})=\bigcap_{0<r<\epsilon}\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,2}_{rr_{0},\epsilon r_{0}}\bigcap B^{*}(z_{0},r_{0})

for a sufficiently small
\epsilon=\epsilon(Y)
.

Note that similar sharp and uniform volume estimates for the singular set have been obtained in other geometric flow settings. In the context of mean curvature flow starting from a mean-convex closed hypersurface, such an estimate was proved in
[
FL25c
, Theorem 1.2]
. In the setting of noncollapsed Ricci limit spaces, a corresponding result was established in
[
CJN21
, Theorem 1.7]
.

As an application of the methods developed for singularity analysis, we also obtain the following
L^{1}
-curvature bounds for four-dimensional closed Ricci flows. Here,
r_{\mathrm{Rm}}
denotes the curvature radius; see
[
FL25a
, Definition 2.10]
.

Theorem 1.8
.

Let
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{4},(g(t))_{t\in[-T,0)}\}
be a four-dimensional closed Ricci flow with
T<\infty
, where
t=0
is the first singular time. Then there exists a constant
C
, depending only on the flow, such that for any
t\in[-T,0)
,

\displaystyle\int_{M}|\mathrm{Rm}|\,\mathrm{d}V_{g(t)}\leq\int_{M}r^{-2}_{\mathrm{Rm}}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g(t)}\leq C.

The proof of Theorem
1.8
will be given in Theorem
6.31
. Moreover, we obtain integral estimates for
\nabla^{k}\mathrm{Rm}
for all
k\geq 1
; see Theorem
6.32
.

In
[
FL25a
, Theorem 9.1]
, it is shown that for any
n
-dimensional closed Ricci flow
\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in[-T,0)}\}
, one has the uniform bound:

\displaystyle\int_{M}|\mathrm{Rm}|^{1-\epsilon}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g(t)}\leq C_{\epsilon}

for any
t\in[-T,0)
and every
\epsilon>0
, where
C_{\epsilon}
depends only on the flow and
\epsilon
. Theorem
1.8
refines this in dimension four and may be viewed as a parabolic analogue of the
L^{2}
-curvature bounds established in
[
JN21
]
. Related
L^{2}
-curvature estimates for four-dimensional closed Ricci flows under a bounded scalar curvature assumption appear in
[
BZ17
,
Sim20
]
. See also the recent work
[
GL25
]
for
L^{1}
-curvature bounds for closed Ricci flow under the Type I assumption.

By taking the product of a three-dimensional closed Ricci flow with a flat circle
S^{1}
and applying Theorem
1.8
, we obtain the corresponding
L^{1}
-curvature bounds in dimension three. These bounds, together with
[
Top05
, Theorem 2.4]
, yield the following result—often referred to as
Perelman’s bounded diameter conjecture
(see
[
Per02
, Section 13.2]
):

Theorem 1.9
.

Let
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{3},(g(t))_{t\in[-T,0)}\}
be a three-dimensional closed Ricci flow. Then there exists a constant
C
depending on the Ricci flow such that

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[-T,0)}\mathrm{diam}_{g(t)}(M)\leq C.

For comparison, Gianniotis
[
Gia25a
]
established this conclusion under the additional Type I curvature assumption. For related results in mean curvature flow, see
[
GH20
,
HJ25
]
.

Some Ingredients in the Proofs of the Main Results

In the following, we outline several key ideas and techniques that play a central role in the proofs of our main results.

The Reifenberg Method

Originally developed in the 1960s by Edward Reifenberg to address the Plateau problem for minimal surfaces (see
[
Rei60
]
), the Reifenberg method has since become a useful tool in geometric measure theory, with far-reaching applications in rectifiability, regularity theory, and the analysis of metric spaces.

In
[
Rei60
]
, Reifenberg considered closed subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
that are well approximated by affine
k
-planes at every scale and location. For a set
S\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
containing the origin, the flatness of
S
near a point
x
at scale
r>0
is quantified by

\displaystyle\beta(x,r):=r^{-1}\inf_{\Gamma}d_{\mathrm{H}}\left(S\cap B_{r}(x),\Gamma\cap B_{r}(x)\right)

(1.5)

where
d_{\mathrm{H}}
denotes the Hausdorff distance, and the infimum is taken over all
k
-dimensional affine subspaces
\Gamma
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

The classical Reifenberg’s theorem asserts that if
\beta(x,r)<\delta
for every
x\in S\cap B_{1}(\vec{0}^{n})
and all
r\in(0,1)
, where
\delta
is a sufficiently small constant, then there exists a bi-Hölder homeomorphism
\phi:S\cap B_{1/2}(\vec{0}^{n})\to B_{1/2}(\vec{0}^{k})\subset\mathbb{R}^{k}
. In particular, this establishes a form of topological regularity for sets under quantitative flatness assumptions.

In general, the bi-Hölder homeomorphism
\phi
in Reifenberg’s theorem cannot be improved to a
bi-Lipschitz
map. A classical counterexample illustrating this limitation is given by the snowflake construction (see
[
Nab20
, Example 3.3]
). However, if the approximation by affine
k
-planes improves sufficiently fast across scales, then a bi-Lipschitz parametrization becomes possible.

Indeed, Toro
[
Tor95
]
proved that if the
square-summability
condition on the
\beta
-numbers holds:

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\sup_{x\in S\cap B_{1}(\vec{0}^{n})}\beta(x,2^{-i})^{2}<\delta,

(1.6)

for a sufficiently small constant
\delta>0
, then there exists a bi-Lipschitz homeomorphism
\phi:S\cap B_{1/2}(\vec{0}^{n})\to B_{1/2}(\vec{0}^{k})\subset\mathbb{R}^{k}
.

Reifenberg’s theorem can be extended to complete metric spaces; see
[
CC97
, Theorem A.1.2]
. Roughly speaking, for a metric space
(X,d)
, one defines a scale-invariant flatness quantity using the Gromov–Hausdorff distance:

\displaystyle\beta^{\prime}(x,r):=r^{-1}d_{\mathrm{GH}}\left(B_{r}(x),B_{r}(\vec{0}^{n})\right)

(1.7)

for every
x\in X
and
r>0
. If
\beta^{\prime}(x,r)
is uniformly small for all
x
and
r
in a neighborhood, then one obtains a local bi-Hölder map
\phi:X\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Using this result, Cheeger and Colding proved that the regular part of a noncollapsed Ricci limit space admits a manifold structure. Furthermore, if a summability condition on
\beta^{\prime}
holds, the bi-Hölder map can be upgraded to a bi-Lipschitz map; see
[
GV25
, Theorem 2.7]
.

In the setting of a noncollapsed Ricci flow limit space
(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})
, applying the Reifenberg method to the set
\mathcal{S}^{k}_{\mathrm{qc}}
requires constructing a suitable flatness quantity, analogous to (
1.5
) or (
1.7
). For illustrative purposes, we restrict attention to the subset
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{c}}^{k}\setminus\mathcal{S}^{k-1}_{\mathrm{c}}\subset\mathcal{S}^{k}_{\mathrm{qc}}
(see Definition
2.28
), consisting of points at which one (and hence every) tangent flow is isometric to the model space
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{k}
(see Subsection
2.3
), whose time slice at
-1
is the standard cylinder
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times S^{n-k}
.

Intuitively, for any point
z_{0}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{c}}^{k}\setminus\mathcal{S}^{k-1}_{\mathrm{c}}
, the local geometry around
z_{0}
nearly splits off an
\mathbb{R}^{k}
, and the singular set
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{c}}^{k}\setminus\mathcal{S}^{k-1}_{\mathrm{c}}
near
z_{0}
behaves approximately like a horizontal
k
-plane. To quantify this almost
k
-splitting, we consider a
(k,\epsilon,r)
-splitting map
\vec{u}=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{k}):Z_{(\mathfrak{t}(z_{0})-10r^{2},\mathfrak{t}(z_{0})]}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
at
z_{0}
(see Definitions
2.22
and
2.23
).

But how does one construct such an approximate splitting map at
z_{0}
? To this end, we introduce the following notion of almost self-similarity:

Definition 1.10

(
(\delta,r)
-selfsimilar)
.

A point
z\in Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
is called
(\delta,r)
-selfsimilar
if
\mathfrak{t}(z)-\delta^{-1}r^{2}\in\mathbb{I}^{-}
and

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{z}(\delta r^{2})-\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{z}(\delta^{-1}r^{2})\leq\delta.

Here, we use the
modified pointed entropy

\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{z}(\tau)
, rather than the conventional pointed entropy
\mathcal{W}_{z}(\tau)
, for technical reasons. Although the two agree for almost every
\tau
, the modified version is better suited for analysis: it is nonincreasing in
\tau
and more accurately controls the spacetime integral of the Ricci shrinker operator (see Lemma
4.5
). In fact, in dimension four they agree identically; see Proposition
6.30
.

A point
z
being
(\delta,r)
-selfsimilar roughly means that, at scale
r
, the geometry around
z
in
Z
is close to that of a Ricci shrinker space. As shown in
[
FL25a
, Appendix D]
, the set of selfsimilar points in a Ricci shrinker space forms the so-called
spine
, which characterizes the degree of symmetry of the shrinker. For instance, if two selfsimilar points lie on distinct time slices, then the Ricci shrinker must be a static or quasi-static cone; see
[
FL25a
, Lemma D.5]
. In Theorem
3.6
, we establish a quantitative version of this result, yielding an integral estimate of
|\mathrm{Ric}|^{2}
in spacetime, which is crucial for constructing almost splitting maps.

We now introduce the concept of entropy pinching:

Definition 1.11

(Strongly entropy pinching)
.

For
k\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
,
\alpha\in(0,1)
,
\delta>0
and
r>0
, the
(k,\alpha,\delta,r)
-entropy pinching at
z_{0}
is defined as

\mathfrak{S}_{r}^{k,\alpha,\delta}(z_{0}):=\inf\sum_{i=0}^{k}\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{z_{i}}(r^{2}/40)-\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{z_{i}}(40r^{2})\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

where the infimum is taken for all sets
\{z_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq k}
which are strongly
(k,\alpha,\delta,r)
-independent at
z_{0}
; see Definition
4.7
. Roughly speaking, a collection
\{z_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq k}
is strongly
(k,\alpha,\delta,r)
-independent at
z_{0}
if the points are almost selfsimilar and well-distributed around
z_{0}
in a way as if they generate a
k
-dimensional space.

A key feature of the definition of
\mathfrak{S}_{r}^{k,\alpha,\delta}(z_{0})
is the
exponent
1/2
, which is critical for obtaining sharp estimates. With this quantity, we construct a sharp splitting map
\vec{u}
at
z_{0}
, whose spacetime integral of
|\nabla^{2}u_{i}|^{2}
is controlled by
\mathfrak{S}_{r}^{k,\alpha,\delta}(z_{0})
; see Theorem
4.12
. Similar sharp splitting maps have been constructed previously: for Ricci limit spaces, see
[
CJN21
, Theorem 6.1]
; and for Type-I Ricci flows, see
[
Gia25b
, Theorem 8.1]
, where related entropy pinching quantities are formulated differently.

A fundamental question is whether a
(k,\epsilon,r)
-splitting map at
z_{0}
remains a
(k,\epsilon,r^{\prime})
-splitting map at
z_{0}
for smaller scales
r^{\prime}<r
. In general, this does not hold without additional assumptions. However, under an appropriate summability condition on
\mathfrak{S}_{r}^{k,\alpha,\delta}(z_{0})
, the splitting property can be propagated to smaller scales.

In Theorem
4.16
, we prove a general nondegeneration result for almost splitting maps, assuming the following summability condition:

\sum_{\bar{r}\leq r_{j}=2^{-j}\leq 1}\mathfrak{S}^{k,\alpha,\epsilon}_{rr_{j}}(z_{0})\ll 1,

(1.8)

where
\bar{r}
is a scale such that, for all
s\in[\bar{r}r,r]
, the point
z_{0}
is
(\delta,s)
-selfsimilar and
(k,\delta,s)
-splitting but not
(k+1,\eta,s)
-splitting, where
\delta\leq\delta(n,Y,\eta,\alpha,\epsilon)
.

This summability condition can be viewed as a pointwise analog of the Reifenberg summability condition in (
1.6
). The proof of Theorem
4.16
follows a strategy similar to that of
[
CJN21
, Theorem 8.1]
, though the settings and technical execution differ substantially. The key steps involve establishing a Hessian decay estimate for the limiting heat flow (see Theorem
4.14
) and then using the sharp splitting map construction to derive the desired nondegeneration result. These results are first proven in the setting of closed Ricci flows, and then extended to the noncollapsed Ricci flow limit space via a limiting argument.

Construction of Cylindrical Neck Regions

To prove the horizontally parabolic
k
-rectifiability of
\mathcal{S}^{k}_{\mathrm{c}}
, we aim to decompose
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{c}}^{k}\setminus\mathcal{S}^{k-1}_{\mathrm{c}}
into countably many subsets, each of which admits a bi-Lipschitz map into
\mathbb{R}^{k}
. To achieve this, we introduce the concept of a
(k,\delta,\mathfrak{c},r)
-cylindrical neck region
(see Definition
5.9
), analogous to the definition introduced by the present authors in the mean curvature flow setting
[
FL25c
, Definition 4.1]
. Related notions also appear in the study of Ricci limit spaces, such as
[
JN21
, Definition 3.1]
and
[
CJN21
, Definition 2.4]
.

In Proposition
5.11
, we show that for any point
z_{0}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{c}}^{k}\setminus\mathcal{S}^{k-1}_{\mathrm{c}}
, there exists a sufficiently small
r_{0}>0
such that

\left(\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{c}}^{k}\setminus\mathcal{S}^{k-1}_{\mathrm{c}}\right)\bigcap B^{*}(z_{0},r_{0})\subset\mathscr{C}_{0},

(1.9)

where
\mathscr{N}=B^{*}(z_{0},2r_{0})\setminus B^{*}_{r_{x}}(\mathscr{C})
is a
(k,\delta,\mathfrak{c},r_{0})
-cylindrical neck region. The key fact used to establish (
1.9
) is that any
z_{0}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{c}}^{k}\setminus\mathcal{S}^{k-1}_{\mathrm{c}}
is
(k,\epsilon,r)
-cylindrical for all sufficiently small
r>0
, as shown in Proposition
5.8
. This relies crucially on the uniqueness of cylindrical singularities established in
[
FL25b
]
.

Given (
1.9
), it suffices to prove the rectifiability of
\mathscr{C}_{0}
for any general
(k,\delta,\mathfrak{c},r)
-cylindrical neck region
\mathscr{N}=B^{*}(z,2r)\setminus B_{r_{x}}^{*}(\mathscr{C})
. On this region, we define the
packing measure

\mu
as:

\mu:=\sum_{x\in\mathscr{C}^{+}}r_{x}^{k}\delta_{x}+\mathscr{H}^{k}|_{\mathscr{C}_{0}},

where
\mathscr{H}^{k}
is the
k
-dimensional Hausdorff measure with respect to
d_{Z}
. We show in Proposition
5.16
that if
\mu
satisfies the Ahlfors regularity condition:

\displaystyle D^{-1}s^{k}\leq\mu(B^{*}(x,s))\leq Ds^{k}

(1.10)

for any
x\in\mathscr{C}
and
r_{x}\leq s\leq r-d_{Z}(x,z)/2
, with some constant
D>1
, then one can construct a map
\vec{u}:\mathscr{C}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
that is bi-Lipschitz away from a set of small measure. A standard covering argument then implies that
\mathscr{C}_{0}
is horizontally parabolic
k
-rectifiable. Note that the conclusion (
1.4
) follows from the rapid clearing-out property (see Lemma
5.2
).

To guarantee the nondegeneracy of the map
\vec{u}
, we verify the summability condition:

\sum_{r_{x}\leq r_{i}=2^{-i}\leq 2^{-5}r}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.08334pt\hskip-2.08334pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\displaystyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-2.08334pt\hskip-2.77774pt\raisebox{-1.65277pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\displaystyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.77774pt\hskip 2.08334pt}\kern-7.49994pt}{\hbox{\hskip 2.08334pt\hskip-2.08334pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\textstyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-2.08334pt\hskip-2.77774pt\raisebox{-1.65277pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\textstyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.77774pt\hskip 2.08334pt}\kern-7.49994pt}{\hbox{\hskip 1.72743pt\hskip-1.72743pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\scriptstyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-1.72743pt\hskip-2.45831pt\raisebox{-1.15695pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.13998pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.13998pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.45831pt\hskip 1.72743pt}\kern-6.78813pt}{\hbox{\hskip 1.57988pt\hskip-1.57988pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-1.57988pt\hskip-2.29166pt\raisebox{-0.82639pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.09999pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.09999pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.29166pt\hskip 1.57988pt}\kern-6.49301pt}\int_{B^{*}(x,r_{i})}\left|\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{y}(r_{i}^{2}/40)-\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{y}(40r_{i}^{2})\right|^{1/2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)\leq\epsilon

(1.11)

for most
x\in\mathscr{C}
, which in turn ensures the summability condition (
1.8
) at
x=z_{0}
. A key estimate used to derive (
1.11
) is that for any
y\in\mathscr{C}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{r_{y}\leq r_{i}=2^{-i}\leq 2^{-5}r}\left\lvert\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{y}(r_{i}^{2}/40)-\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{y}(40r_{i}^{2})\right\rvert^{1/2}\leq\Psi(\delta),

where
\Psi(\delta)\to 0
as
\delta\to 0
. This summability inequality is a consequence of a discrete Lojasiewicz inequality, originally established for closed Ricci flows in
[
FL25b
, Theorem 1.3, Corollary 1.4]
, where it underpinned the strong uniqueness result. Here we generalize this inequality to noncollapsed Ricci flow limit spaces; see Proposition
5.1
.

Finally, in Theorem
5.21
, we establish the Ahlfors regularity estimate (
1.10
) with constant
D=D(n,Y,\mathfrak{c})
. The proof of this theorem is technically intricate and combines an inductive covering argument, a limiting process, and a geometric transformation theorem proved in Theorem
A.11
.

Neck Decomposition in Dimension Four

The study of quotient cylindrical singularities and the construction of quotient cylindrical neck regions are not sufficient to analyze general singularities. In dimension
4
, however, most singularities are modeled either on a quotient cylinder or on a flat cone of the form

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(\Gamma)=\mathbb{R}^{4}/\Gamma\times\mathbb{R},

where
\Gamma\leqslant\mathrm{O}(4)
is a nontrivial finite group acting freely on
S^{3}
.

The quantitative analysis of this model space presents certain difficulties. First, even if one tangent flow at a point is
\mathbb{R}^{4}/\Gamma\times\mathbb{R}
, other tangent flows at the same point need not coincide. Second, a region that is quantitatively close to
\mathbb{R}^{4}/\Gamma\times\mathbb{R}
at small scales may, on larger scales, resemble a quasi-static cone
\mathbb{R}^{4}/\Gamma\times(-\infty,a]
for some
a\in[0,\infty)
. Roughly speaking, this indicates that the model
\mathbb{R}^{4}/\Gamma\times\mathbb{R}
is not quantitatively stable.

To address this, we consider a more stable model:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{0}(\Gamma)=\mathbb{R}^{4}/\Gamma\times\mathbb{R}_{-},

which captures only the negative-time behavior. In this setting, we introduce the notion of a
(\delta,\mathfrak{c},r)
–flat neck region

\mathscr{N}
(see Definition
6.5
), defined analogously to a
(k,\delta,\mathfrak{c},r)
–cylindrical neck region, but with the model space replaced by
\mathcal{F}^{0}(\Gamma)
. For any
(\delta,\mathfrak{c},r)
–flat neck regions, we prove Ahlfors regularity in Proposition
6.7
, and further show in Lemma
6.6
that their centers
\mathscr{C}
are vertically parabolic
2
-rectifiable. By Definition
1.2
, this is equivalent to stating that the time function
\mathfrak{t}|_{\mathscr{C}}:\mathscr{C}\to\mathbb{R}
is bi-Lipschitz, where
\mathbb{R}
is equipped with the parabolic metric.

Another key fact is that quotient cylindrical neck regions and flat neck regions are mutually exclusive. In other words, there is no need to consider neck regions of mixed type—regions that resemble a quotient cylinder at one scale but
\mathbb{R}^{4}/\Gamma\times\mathbb{R}_{-}
(on the negative part) at another. Such a scenario is ruled out by the quantitative uniqueness of quotient cylinders; see Proposition
5.28
.

We now state the neck decomposition theorem in dimension four. For simplicity, we only state the case
k=2
in Theorem
6.9
.

Theorem 1.12

(Neck decomposition theorem)
.

Let
(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})
be a noncollapsed Ricci flow limit space arising as the pointed Gromov–Hausdorff limit of a sequence in
\mathcal{M}(4,Y,T)
. For any constants
\delta>0
and
\eta>0
, if
\zeta\leq\zeta(Y,\delta,\eta)
, then the following holds.

Given
z_{0}\in Z
with
\mathfrak{t}(z_{0})-2\zeta^{-2}r_{0}^{2}\in\mathbb{I}^{-}
, we have the decomposition
:

\displaystyle B^{*}(z_{0},r_{0})\subset\bigcup_{a}\big(\mathscr{N}^{\prime}_{a}\bigcap B^{*}(x_{a},r_{a})\big)\bigcup\bigcup_{b}B^{*}(x_{b},r_{b})\bigcup S^{2,\delta,\eta},

\displaystyle S^{2,\delta,\eta}\subset\bigcup_{a}\big(\mathscr{C}_{0,a}\bigcap B^{*}(x_{a},r_{a})\big)\bigcup\tilde{S}^{2,\delta,\eta},

with the following properties
:

(a)

For each
a
,
\mathscr{N}_{a}=B^{*}(x_{a},2r_{a})\setminus B^{*}_{r_{x}}(\mathscr{C}_{a})
is either a
(2,\delta,\mathfrak{c},r_{a})
-quotient cylindrical neck region or a
(\delta,\mathfrak{c},r_{a})
-flat neck region, where
\mathfrak{c}=\mathfrak{c}(Y)
. In the former case, we set
\mathscr{N}_{a}^{\prime}=\mathscr{N}_{a}
;
in the latter,
\mathscr{N}_{a}^{\prime}
denotes the modified
\delta
-region associated with
\mathscr{N}_{a}
.

(b)

For each
b
, there exists a point in
B^{*}(x_{b},2r_{b})
which is
(3,\eta,r_{b})
-symmetric.

(c)

The following content estimates hold
:

\displaystyle\sum_{a}r_{a}^{2}+\sum_{b}r_{b}^{2}+\mathscr{H}^{2}(S^{2,\delta,\eta})\leq C(Y)r_{0}^{2}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathscr{H}^{2}(\tilde{S}^{2,\delta,\eta})=0.

The definition of the modified
\delta
-region is given in Definition
6.18
. The motivation for introducing it is as follows. Roughly speaking, if one has constructed a quotient cylindrical neck region, then any point away from the center locally resembles
\mathbb{R}^{4}\times\mathbb{R}
and should therefore be covered by a
b
-ball
B^{*}(x_{b},r_{b})
as above. By contrast, if one has constructed a flat neck region, then any point in this region carries information only on its negative part, while the model space on the positive part remains a priori unclear. Nevertheless, in a quasi-static cone, one already knows that any point with time coordinate exceeding the arrival time must have larger pointed entropy. Thus, to cover the positive part of a flat neck region, one needs to introduce another collection of balls, each of which has larger pointed entropy. One can then perform a further decomposition on these new balls and repeat the process finitely many times. Consequently, we are led to modify the definition of flat neck regions so as to exclude the newly introduced balls.

The proof of Theorem
1.12
is technically involved. Related ideas can be found in the literature (see, e.g.,
[
CJN21
]
), but our argument must handle the quasi-static cone
\mathbb{R}^{4}/\Gamma\times(-\infty,a]
and the resulting complications near the boundary
\mathbb{R}^{4}/\Gamma\times\{a\}
.

As a consequence of Theorem
1.12
, we deduce that
\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\mathrm{F}}
is contained in the centers of countably many flat neck regions. Hence,
\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\mathrm{F}}
is vertically parabolic
2
-rectifiable. Moreover, Theorem
1.4
(iii) follows directly from Theorem
1.12
. The volume estimates in Theorem
1.7
also arise from this decomposition, and the
L^{1}
-curvature bounds in Theorem
1.8
are likewise derived from the neck decomposition.

Organization of the Paper

The paper is organized as follows.

•

Section
2
introduces the basic conventions and foundational results for Ricci flows. We also review key results on Ricci flow limit spaces from
[
FL25a
]
, and describe our model spaces—cylinders—along with their elementary properties.

•

Section
3
focuses on almost splitting maps in the setting of closed Ricci flows. We construct sharp splitting maps controlled by entropy pinching, establish a Hessian decay estimate for heat flows, and prove a covering lemma for independent points.

•

Section
4
extends the main results of the previous section to noncollapsed Ricci flow limit spaces. We first introduce a modified version of the pointed
\mathcal{W}
-entropy and prove its basic properties. We then extend the construction of sharp splitting maps and the Hessian decay estimate to the limit space. Finally, by generalizing the covering lemma, we obtain the proof of Theorem
1.1
.

•

Section
5
is devoted to the parabolic rectifiability of
\mathcal{S}^{k}_{\mathrm{c}}
via the construction of cylindrical neck regions. We also prove Ahlfors regularity for the associated packing measure and generalize the results to the case of quotient cylinders.

•

Section
6
focuses on the four-dimensional case. It begins with the countability of
\mathcal{S}^{0}
. We then introduce flat neck regions, establish their Ahlfors regularity and the rectifiability of their centers, and present the proof of the neck decomposition theorem, with applications to the rectifiability of
\mathcal{S}^{k}
, sharp volume estimates of
\mathcal{S}
, and
L^{1}
-curvature bounds for closed four-dimensional Ricci flows.

•

Appendix
A
contains the proof of a geometric transformation theorem for almost splitting maps, which plays an important role in establishing the Ahlfors regularity of cylindrical neck regions.

•

Finally, we include a list of notations for reference.

: Hanbing Fang would like to thank his advisor, Prof. Xiuxiong Chen, for his encouragement and support. Hanbing Fang is supported by the Simons Foundation. Yu Li is supported by YSBR-001, NSFC-12201597, NSFC-12522105 and research funds from the University of Science and Technology of China and the Chinese Academy of Sciences.

2
Preliminaries

2.1
Basic conventions and results for Ricci flows

Throughout this paper, we consider a closed Ricci flow solution
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in I}\}
, where
M
is an
n
dimensional closed manifold,
I\subset\mathbb{R}
is a closed interval, and
(g(t))_{t\in I}
is a family of smooth metrics on
M
satisfying for all
t\in I
the Ricci flow equation:

\partial_{t}g(t)=-2\mathrm{Ric}(g(t)).

Following the notation in
[
FL25a
]
, we use
x^{*}\in\mathcal{X}
to denote a spacetime point
x^{*}\in M\times I
, and define
\mathfrak{t}(x^{*})
to be its time component. We denote by
d_{t}
the distance function and by
\mathrm{d}V_{g(t)}
the volume form induced by the metric
g(t)
. For
x^{*}=(x,t)\in\mathcal{X}
, we write
B_{t}(x,r)
for the geodesic ball centered at
x
with radius
r
at time
t
. The Riemannian curvature, Ricci curvature, and scalar curvature are denoted by
\mathrm{Rm}
,
\mathrm{Ric}
, and
\mathrm{R}
, respectively, with the time parameter omitted when there is no ambiguity.

For the smooth closed Ricci flow
\mathcal{X}
, we set
K(x,t;y,s)
to be the heat kernel, which is determined by:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}&\square K(\cdot,\cdot;y,s)=0,\\
&\square^{*}K(x,t;\cdot,\cdot)=0,\\
&\lim_{t\searrow s}K(\cdot,t;y,s)=\delta_{y},\\
&\lim_{s\nearrow t}K(x,t;\cdot,s)=\delta_{x}.\end{cases}

where
\square:=\partial_{t}-\Delta
and
\square^{*}:=-\partial_{t}-\Delta+\mathrm{R}
.

Definition 2.1
.

The
conjugate heat kernel measure

\nu_{x^{*};s}
based at
x^{*}=(x,t)
is defined as

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\nu_{x^{*};s}=\mathrm{d}\nu_{x,t;s}:=K(x,t;\cdot,s)\,\mathrm{d}V_{g(s)}.

It is clear that
\nu_{x^{*};s}
is a probability measure on
M
. If we set

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\nu_{x^{*};s}=(4\pi(t-s))^{-n/2}e^{-f_{x^{*}}(\cdot,s)}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g(s)},

then the function
f_{x^{*}}
is called the
potential function
at
x^{*}
which satisfies:

-\partial_{s}f_{x^{*}}=\Delta f_{x^{*}}-|\nabla f_{x^{*}}|^{2}+\mathrm{R}-\frac{n}{2(t-s)}.

Next, we recall the definitions of Nash entropy and
\mathcal{W}
-entropy based at a spacetime point
x^{*}
.

Definition 2.2
.

The
Nash entropy
based at
x^{*}\in\mathcal{X}
is defined by

\mathcal{N}_{x^{*}}(\tau):=\int_{M}f_{x^{*}}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x^{*};\mathfrak{t}(x^{*})-\tau}-\frac{n}{2},

for
\tau>0
with
\mathfrak{t}(x^{*})-\tau\in I
, where
f_{x^{*}}
is the potential function at
x^{*}
.
Moreover, the
\mathcal{W}
-entropy based at
x^{*}
is defined by

\mathcal{W}_{x^{*}}(\tau):=\int_{M}\tau(2\Delta f_{x^{*}}-|\nabla f_{x^{*}}|^{2}+\mathrm{R})+f_{x^{*}}-n\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x^{*};\mathfrak{t}(x^{*})-\tau}.

(2.1)

The following proposition gives basic properties of Nash entropy (see
[
Bam20a
, Section 5]
):

Proposition 2.3
.

For any
x^{*}\in\mathcal{X}
with
\mathfrak{t}(x^{*})-\tau\in I
and
\mathrm{R}(\cdot,\mathfrak{t}(x^{*})-\tau)\geq R_{\min}
, we have the following inequalities:

(i)

\displaystyle-\frac{n}{2\tau}+R_{\min}\leq\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}\mathcal{N}_{x^{*}}(\tau)\leq 0;

(ii)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}\left(\tau\mathcal{N}_{x^{*}}(\tau)\right)=\mathcal{W}_{x^{*}}(\tau)\leq 0;

(iii)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}\tau^{2}}\left(\tau\mathcal{N}_{x^{*}}(\tau)\right)=-2\tau\int_{M}\left\lvert\mathrm{Ric}+\nabla^{2}f_{x^{*}}-\frac{1}{2\tau}g\right\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x^{*};\mathfrak{t}(x^{*})-\tau}\leq 0
.

As in
[
FL25a
]
, we have the following definition.

Definition 2.4
.

A closed Ricci flow
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in I}\}
is said to have entropy bounded below by
-Y
at
x^{*}\in\mathcal{X}
if

\displaystyle\inf_{\tau>0}\mathcal{N}_{x^{*}}(\tau)\geq-Y,

(2.2)

where the infimum is taken over all
\tau>0
whenever the Nash entropy
\mathcal{N}_{x^{*}}(\tau)
is well-defined.

Moreover, we say that the Ricci flow
\mathcal{X}
has entropy bounded below by
-Y
if (
2.2
) holds for all
x^{*}\in\mathcal{X}
.

We have the following result from
[
Bam20a
, Propositions 3.12, 3.13]
. Here, the definition of
H
-center can be found in
[
FL25a
, Definition 2.13]
. Note that by
[
Bam20a
, Corollary 3.8]
, an
H_{n}
-center of
x_{0}^{*}
, where
H_{n}:=(n-1)\pi^{2}/4+2
, must exist for any
t<\mathfrak{t}(x_{0}^{*})
.

Proposition 2.5
.

Any two
H
-centers
(z_{1},t)
and
(z_{2},t)
of
x_{0}^{*}
satisfy
d_{t}(z_{1},z_{2})\leq 2\sqrt{H(\mathfrak{t}(x_{0}^{*})-t)}
. Moreover, if
(z,t)
is an
H
-center of
x_{0}^{*}\in\mathcal{X}
, then for any
L>0
, we have

\displaystyle\nu_{x^{*}_{0};t}\left(B_{t}\left(z,\sqrt{LH(\mathfrak{t}(x_{0}^{*})-t)}\right)\right)\geq 1-L^{-1}.

For later applications, we need the following
L^{p}
-Poincaré inequality, proved by
[
HN14
, Theorem 1.10]
and
[
Bam20a
, Theorem 11.1]
.

Theorem 2.6

(Poincaré inequality)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in I}\}
be a closed Ricci flow with
x_{0}^{*}=(x_{0},t_{0})\in\mathcal{X}
. Suppose
\tau>0
with
t_{0}-\tau\in I
, and
h\in C^{1}(M)
with
\int_{M}h\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x_{0}^{*};t_{0}-\tau}=0
. Then for any
p\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\int_{M}|h|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x_{0}^{*};t_{0}-\tau}\leq C(p)\tau^{p/2}\int_{M}|\nabla h|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x_{0}^{*};t_{0}-\tau}.

Here, we can choose
C(1)=\sqrt{\pi}
and
C(2)=2
.

Also, the following integral estimates from
[
Bam20b
, Proposition 6.2]
will be frequently used.

Proposition 2.7
.

There exists
\bar{\theta}=\bar{\theta}(n)>0
such that the following holds. Let
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in I}\}
be a smooth closed Ricci flow. Assume
x_{0}^{*}=(x_{0},t_{0})\in\mathcal{X}
with
[t_{0}-2r^{2},t_{0}]\subset I
and set
\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}=\mathrm{d}\nu_{x_{0},t_{0};t}=(4\pi\tau)^{-n/2}e^{-f}\mathrm{d}V_{g(t)}
, where
\tau=t_{0}-t
. Assume
\mathcal{N}_{x_{0}^{*}}(2r^{2})\geq-Y
for some
r>0
, then for any
0<\chi\leq 1/2
and
\theta\in[0,\bar{\theta}]
,

\displaystyle\int_{-r^{2}}^{-\chi r^{2}}\int_{M}\left(\tau|\mathrm{Ric}|^{2}+\tau|\nabla^{2}f|^{2}+|\nabla f|^{2}+\tau|\nabla f|^{4}+\tau^{-1}e^{\theta f}+\tau^{-1}\right)e^{\theta f}\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq C(n,Y)|\log\chi|,

\displaystyle\int_{M}\left(\tau|\mathrm{R}|+\tau|\Delta f|+\tau|\nabla f|^{2}+e^{\theta f}+1\right)e^{\theta f}\mathrm{d}\nu_{-r^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C(n,Y).

We conclude this section with the following weighted Bianchi identity, whose proof can be found in
[
FL25b
, Lemma 2.5]
. Here, we use
div
to denote the divergence operator and
\mathrm{div}_{f}:=e^{f}\circ\mathrm{div}\circ e^{-f}
the weighted divergence operator.

Lemma 2.8
.

Given a spacetime point
x_{0}^{*}\in\mathcal{X}
with
f=f_{x_{0}^{*}}
and
\tau=\mathfrak{t}(x_{0}^{*})-t
, the following weighted Bianchi identity holds:

\nabla\left(\tau(2\Delta f-|\nabla f|^{2}+\mathrm{R})+f-n\right)=2\mathrm{div}_{f}\left(\tau\mathrm{Ric}+\nabla^{2}(\tau f)-\frac{g}{2}\right).

2.2
Noncollapsed Ricci flow limit spaces

In this subsection, we review the construction of noncollapsed Ricci flow limit spaces and their key properties, as developed in
[
FL25a
]
.

As in
[
FL25a
]
, we consider the moduli space
\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)
of closed Ricci flows defined as follows:

Definition 2.9

(Moduli space)
.

For fixed constants
T\in(0,+\infty]
and
Y>0
, the moduli space
\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)
consists of all
n
-dimensional closed Ricci flows
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in\mathbb{I}^{++}}\}
satisfying

(i)

\mathcal{X}
is defined on
\mathbb{I}^{++}:=[-T,0]
.

(ii)

\mathcal{X}
has entropy bounded below by
-Y
(cf. Definition
2.4
).

In addition, we set

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}^{+}:=[-0.99T,0],\quad\mathbb{I}:=[-0.98T,0],\quad\mathbb{I}^{-}:=(-0.98T,0].{\@index{\@indexphrase{$\mathbb{I}^{++},\mathbb{I}^{+},\mathbb{I},\mathbb{I}^{-}$}}}

As noted in
[
FL25a
]
, these intervals may generally be chosen as
[-(1-\sigma)T,0]
,
[-(1-2\sigma)T,0]
, and
(-(1-2\sigma)T,0]
, respectively, where
\sigma>0
is an arbitrarily small parameter. For simplicity, we fix
\sigma=0.01
in the present setting.

For any
\mathcal{X}\in\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)
, we have the following definition of the spacetime distance on
M\times\mathbb{I}^{+}
:

Definition 2.10
.

For any
x^{*}=(x,t),y^{*}=(y,s)\in M\times\mathbb{I}^{+}
with
s\leq t
, we define

\displaystyle d^{*}(x^{*},y^{*}):=\inf_{r\in[\sqrt{t-s},\sqrt{t+0.99T})}\left\{r\mid d_{W_{1}}^{t-r^{2}}(\nu_{x^{*};t-r^{2}},\nu_{y^{*};t-r^{2}})\leq\epsilon_{0}r\right\},

If no such
r
exists, we define
d^{*}(x^{*},y^{*}):=\epsilon_{0}^{-1}d_{W_{1}}^{-0.99T}(\nu_{x^{*};-0.99T},\nu_{y^{*};-0.99T})
.

Here,
\epsilon_{0}\in(0,1]
is the spacetime distance constant depending on
n
and
Y
(see
[
FL25a
, Definition 3.3]
). By
[
FL25a
, Lemma 3.7]
,
d^{*}
defines a distance function on
M\times\mathbb{I}^{+}
, which coincides with the standard topology on
M\times\mathbb{I}^{+}
(see
[
FL25a
, Corollary 3.11]
). The metric ball defined by
d^{*}
is denoted by
B^{*}
.

The following weak compactness theorem is proved in
[
FL25a
, Theorem 1.3]
.

Theorem 2.11

(Weak compactness)
.

Given any sequence
\mathcal{X}^{i}=\{M_{i}^{n},(g_{i}(t))_{t\in\mathbb{I}^{++}}\}\in\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)
with base points
p_{i}^{*}\in M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}

(
when
T=+\infty
, we additionally assume
\limsup_{i\to\infty}\mathfrak{t}_{i}(p_{i}^{*})>-\infty
)
, by taking a subsequence if necessary, we obtain the pointed Gromov-Hausdorff convergence

\displaystyle(M_{i}\times\mathbb{I},d^{*}_{i},p_{i}^{*},\mathfrak{t}_{i})\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathrm{pGH}\hskip 8.19447pt}(Z,d_{Z},p_{\infty},\mathfrak{t}),

(2.3)

where
d^{*}_{i}
denotes the restriction of the
d^{*}
-distance on
M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}
, and
\mathfrak{t}_{i}
is the standard time-function on
M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}
. The limit space
(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})
is a complete, separable, locally compact parabolic space.

The limit space
(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})
is referred to as a
noncollapsed Ricci flow limit space
over
\mathbb{I}
. For the distance
d_{Z}
, we write
B_{Z}^{*}
for the corresponding metric ball.
Z
contains a regular part
\mathcal{R}
, which is a dense open subset of
Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
(see
[
FL25a
, Corollary 5.7]
) and carries the structure of a Ricci flow spacetime
(\mathcal{R},\mathfrak{t},\partial_{\mathfrak{t}},g^{Z})
. On this regular part, the convergence described in Theorem
2.11
is smooth, in the following sense (cf.
[
FL25a
, Theorem 1.5]
):

Theorem 2.12

(Smooth convergence)
.

There exists an increasing sequence
U_{1}\subset U_{2}\subset\ldots\subset\mathcal{R}
of open subsets with
\bigcup_{i=1}^{\infty}U_{i}=\mathcal{R}
, open subsets
V_{i}\subset M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}
, time-preserving diffeomorphisms
\phi_{i}:U_{i}\to V_{i}
and a sequence
\epsilon_{i}\to 0
such that the following holds:

(a)

We have

\displaystyle\|\phi_{i}^{*}g^{i}-g^{Z}\|_{C^{[\epsilon_{i}^{-1}]}(U_{i})}

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon_{i},

\displaystyle\|\phi_{i}^{*}\partial_{\mathfrak{t}_{i}}-\partial_{\mathfrak{t}}\|_{C^{[\epsilon_{i}^{-1}]}(U_{i})}

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon_{i},

where
g^{i}
is the spacetime metric induced by
g_{i}(t)
, and
\partial_{\mathfrak{t}_{i}}
is the standard time vector field induced by
\mathfrak{t}_{i}
.

(b)

Let
y\in\mathcal{R}
and
y_{i}^{*}\in M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}
. Then
y_{i}^{*}\to y
in the Gromov-Hausdorff sense if and only if
y_{i}^{*}\in V_{i}
for large
i
and
\phi_{i}^{-1}(y_{i}^{*})\to y
in
\mathcal{R}
.

(c)

For
U_{i}^{(2)}=\{(x,y)\in U_{i}\times U_{i}\mid\mathfrak{t}(x)>\mathfrak{t}(y)+\epsilon_{i}\}
,
V_{i}^{(2)}=\{(x^{*},y^{*})\in V_{i}\times V_{i}\mid\mathfrak{t}_{i}(x^{*})>\mathfrak{t}_{i}(y^{*})+\epsilon_{i}\}
and
\phi_{i}^{(2)}:=(\phi_{i},\phi_{i}):U_{i}^{(2)}\to V_{i}^{(2)}
, we have

\displaystyle\|(\phi_{i}^{(2)})^{*}K^{i}-K_{Z}\|_{C^{[\epsilon_{i}^{-1}]}(U_{i}^{(2)})}\leq\epsilon_{i},

where
K^{i}
and
K_{Z}
denote the heat kernels on
(M_{i}\times\mathbb{I},g_{i}(t))
and
(\mathcal{R},g^{Z})
, respectively.

(d)

If
z_{i}^{*}\in M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}
converge to
z\in Z
in Gromov–Hausdorff sense, then

\displaystyle K^{i}(z_{i}^{*};\phi_{i}(\cdot))\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}}K_{Z}(z;\cdot)\quad\text{on}\quad\mathcal{R}_{(-\infty,t(z))}.

(e)

For each
t\in\mathbb{I}
, there are at most countable connected components of the time-slice
\mathcal{R}_{t}
.

For each
z\in Z
, we can assign a conjugate heat kernel measure
\mathrm{d}\nu_{z;s}:=K_{Z}(z;\cdot)\,\mathrm{d}V_{g^{Z}_{s}}
based at
z
for
s\leq\mathfrak{t}(z)
, which is a probability measure on
\mathcal{R}_{s}
. All these probability measures together satisfy the reproduction formula (cf.
[
FL25a
, Equation (5.5)]
).

For each
t\in\mathbb{I}^{-}
, one can define an extended distance function
d^{Z}_{t}(x,y):=\lim_{s\nearrow t}d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{R}_{s}}(\nu_{x;s},\nu_{y;s})\in[0,\infty]
for any
x,y\in Z_{t}
, where
d_{W_{1}}^{\mathcal{R}_{s}}
denotes the
W_{1}
-Wasserstein distance on
(\mathcal{R}_{s},g^{Z}_{s})
. It can be proved, see
[
FL25a
, Theorem 1.7]
, that
(Z_{t},d_{t}^{Z})
is a complete extended metric space. Moreover, for all but countably many times
t\in\mathbb{I}^{-}
,
d^{Z}_{t}=d_{g^{Z}_{t}}
on each connected component of
\mathcal{R}_{t}
. For more properties of
d^{Z}_{t}
, we refer readers to
[
FL25a
, Section 6]
.

Next, we recall the following definition and notation from
[
FL25a
, Definition 5.37, Notation 5.38]
.

Definition 2.13

(
\epsilon
-close)
.

Suppose
(Z,d_{Z},z,\mathfrak{t})
and
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
are two pointed noncollapsed Ricci flow limit spaces, with regular parts given by the Ricci flow spacetimes
(\mathcal{R},\mathfrak{t},\partial_{\mathfrak{t}},g^{Z})
and
(\mathcal{R}^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime},\partial_{\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}},g^{Z^{\prime}})
, respectively, such that
J
is a time interval.

We say that
(Z,d_{Z},z,\mathfrak{t})
is
\epsilon
-close
to
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})

over
J
if there exists an open set
U\subset\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{J}
and a smooth embedding
\phi:U\to\mathcal{R}_{J}
satisfying the following properties.

(a)

\phi
is time-preserving.

(b)

U\subset B^{*}_{Z^{\prime}}(z^{\prime},\epsilon^{-1})\bigcap\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{J}
and
U
is an
\epsilon
-net of
B^{*}_{Z^{\prime}}(z^{\prime},\epsilon^{-1})\bigcap Z^{\prime}_{J}
with respect to
d_{Z^{\prime}}
.

(c)

For any
x,y\in U
, we have

\displaystyle\left\lvert d_{Z}(\phi(x),\phi(y))-d_{Z^{\prime}}(x,y)\right\rvert\leq\epsilon.

(d)

The
\epsilon
-neighborhood of
\phi(U)
with respect to
d_{Z}
contains
B^{*}_{Z}(z,\epsilon^{-1}-\epsilon)\bigcap Z_{J}
.

(e)

There exists
x_{0}\in U
such that
d_{Z^{\prime}}(x_{0},z^{\prime})\leq\epsilon
and
d_{Z}(\phi(x_{0}),z)\leq\epsilon
.

(f)

On
U
, the following estimates hold:

\displaystyle\rVert\phi^{*}g^{Z}-g^{Z^{\prime}}\rVert_{C^{[\epsilon^{-1}]}(U)}+\rVert\phi^{*}\partial_{\mathfrak{t}}-\partial_{\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}}\rVert_{C^{[\epsilon^{-1}]}(U)}\leq\epsilon.

Note that
\phi
can be extended to a map
\tilde{\phi}:B^{*}_{Z^{\prime}}(z^{\prime},\epsilon^{-1})\bigcap Z^{\prime}_{J}\to Z_{J}
such that for any
x,y\in B^{*}_{Z^{\prime}}(z^{\prime},\epsilon^{-1})\bigcap Z^{\prime}_{J}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\lvert d_{Z}(\tilde{\phi}(x),\tilde{\phi}(y))-d_{Z^{\prime}}(x,y)\right\rvert\leq 3\epsilon.

We call the extension
\tilde{\phi}
an
\epsilon
-map
. In general,
\tilde{\phi}
is neither unique nor continuous, but it serves as a Gromov–Hausdorff approximation from
Z^{\prime}_{J}
to
Z_{J}
.

Notation 2.14
.

For a sequence of noncollapsed Ricci limit spaces
(Z_{i},d_{Z_{i}},z_{i},\mathfrak{t}_{i})
,
i\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
, we write

(Z_{i},d_{Z_{i}},z_{i},\mathfrak{t}_{i})\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\hat{C}^{\infty}\hskip 8.19447pt}(Z_{\infty},d_{Z_{\infty}},z_{\infty},\mathfrak{t}_{\infty}),

if there exists a sequence
\epsilon_{i}\to 0
such that
(Z_{i},d_{Z_{i}},z_{i},\mathfrak{t}_{i})
is
\epsilon_{i}
-close to
(Z_{\infty},d_{Z_{\infty}},z_{\infty},\mathfrak{t}_{\infty})
over
[-\epsilon_{i}^{-1},\epsilon_{i}^{-1}]
.

In particular, it is clear by Theorem
2.12
that the convergence (
2.3
) can be improved to be

(M_{i}\times\mathbb{I},d^{*}_{i},p_{i}^{*},\mathfrak{t}_{i})\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\hat{C}^{\infty}\hskip 8.19447pt}(Z,d_{Z},p_{\infty},\mathfrak{t}).

Now, we recall the following definitions.

Definition 2.15

(Ricci shrinker space)
.

A pointed parabolic metric space
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
with
\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}(z^{\prime})=0
is called an
n
-dimensional
Ricci shrinker space
with entropy bounded below by
-Y
if it satisfies
\mathbb{R}_{-}\subset\mathrm{image}(\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
and arises as the pointed Gromov–Hausdorff limit of a sequence of Ricci flows in
\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T_{i})
with
T_{i}\to+\infty

(
see
[
FL25a
, Remark 3.25]
)
. Moreover,
\mathcal{N}_{z^{\prime}}(\tau)
remains constant for all
\tau>0
.

For any Ricci shrinker space
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
, we call
(Z^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0]},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
its
negative part
.

Definition 2.16

(Tangent flow)
.

For any
z\in Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
, a
tangent flow
at
z
is a pointed Gromov-Hausdorff limit of
(Z,r_{j}^{-1}d_{Z},z,r_{j}^{-2}(\mathfrak{t}-\mathfrak{t}(z)))
for a sequence
r_{j}\searrow 0
.

It is clear that any tangent flow is a Ricci shrinker space. For Ricci shrinker spaces, we have (cf.
[
FL25a
, Theorem 1.9]
)

Theorem 2.17
.

Let
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
be a Ricci shrinker space so that its regular part is given by a Ricci flow spacetime
(\mathcal{R}^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime},\partial_{\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}},g^{Z^{\prime}}_{t})
. Then the following statements hold.

(a)

On
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0)}
, the following equation holds:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ric}(g^{Z^{\prime}})+\nabla^{2}f_{z^{\prime}}=\frac{g^{Z^{\prime}}}{2\tau},

where
f_{z^{\prime}}
is the potential function of
\nu_{z^{\prime};\cdot}
and
\tau(\cdot)=-\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}(\cdot)
.

(b)

For any
t<0
, the slice
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{t}
is connected. Moreover, the distance
d^{Z^{\prime}}_{t}
, when restricted on
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{t}
, coincides with the Riemannian distance induced by the metric
g^{Z^{\prime}}_{t}
.

(c)

Z^{\prime}_{(0,\infty)}=\emptyset
if
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
is
collapsed
(see
[
FL25a
, Definition 7.18]
).

(d)

For any
t<0
,
Z^{\prime}_{t}\setminus\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{t}
has Minkowski dimension at most
n-4
with respect to
d^{Z^{\prime}}_{t}
.

For tangent flows, we have the following rough classification.

Definition 2.18

(
k
-symmetric)
.

A Ricci shrinker space
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
is called
k
-symmetric
if one of the following holds:

(1)

(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
is
k
-splitting and is not a static cone.

(2)

(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
is a static cone that is
(k-2)
-splitting.

Here,
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
is
k
-splitting if the regular part
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
splits off an
\mathbb{R}^{k}
at
\mathfrak{t}=-1
(see
[
FL25a
, Definition 8.1, Proposition 8.2]
). Roughly speaking, a
static cone
is characterized by
\mathrm{image}(\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})=\mathbb{R}
and vanishing Ricci curvature on
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
. Case (1) above may include a
quasi-static cone
, which has vanishing Ricci curvature only on
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,t_{a}]}
for some constant
t_{a}\in[0,\infty)
called the
arrival time
, but not beyond. For precise definitions and related properties of static and quasi-static cones, see
[
FL25a
, Definition 7.17, Theorem 7.21, Corollary 7.22, Proposition 7.23]
.

On
Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
, we have the following regular-singular decomposition:

\displaystyle Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}=\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}\sqcup\mathcal{S},

where
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
denotes the restriction of
\mathcal{R}
on
\mathbb{I}^{-}
. It can be proved (see
[
FL25a
, Theorem 7.15]
) that a point
z
is a regular point if and only if any of its tangent flow is isometric to
(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R},d^{*}_{E,\epsilon_{0}},(\vec{0}^{n},0),\mathfrak{t})
or
(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}_{-},d^{*}_{E,\epsilon_{0}},(\vec{0}^{n},0),\mathfrak{t})
, where
d^{*}_{E,\epsilon_{0}}
denotes the induced
d^{*}
-distance on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}_{-}
, defined with respect to
\epsilon_{0}
. Equivalently,
z
is a regular point if and only if
\mathcal{N}_{z}(0)\geq-\epsilon_{n}
(see
[
FL25a
, Proposition 7.7]
).

The singular set
\mathcal{S}
admits a natural stratification:

\mathcal{S}^{0}\subset\mathcal{S}^{1}\subset\cdots\subset\mathcal{S}^{n+1}=\mathcal{S},

(2.4)

where a point
z\in\mathcal{S}^{k}
if and only if no tangent flow at
z
is
(k+1)
-symmetric. It can be proved, see
[
FL25a
, Theorems 1.10, 1.13]
) that

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}^{n-2},

and the Minkowski dimension of
\mathcal{S}
with respect to
d_{Z}
satisfies

\displaystyle\dim_{\mathscr{M}}\mathcal{S}\leq n-2.

Definition 2.19
.

A point
z\in Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
is called
(k,\epsilon,r)
-symmetric
if there exist a
k
-symmetric Ricci shrinker space
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
such that

\displaystyle(Z,r^{-1}d_{Z},z,r^{-2}(\mathfrak{t}-\mathfrak{t}(z)))\quad\text{is $\epsilon$-close to}\quad(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})\quad\text{over}\quad[-\epsilon^{-1},\epsilon^{-1}].

Furthermore, if
k\in\{n-3,n-2\}
, then the model space
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
cannot be a quasi-static cone. If
k\geq n-1
, then the model space
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
is isometric to
(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(-\infty,t_{a}],d^{*}_{E,\epsilon_{0}},(\vec{0},0),\mathfrak{t})
for some constant
t_{a}\in[0,+\infty]
.

Next, we recall the definition of the quantitative singular strata.

Definition 2.20
.

For
\epsilon>0
and
0<r_{1}<r_{2}<\infty
, the quantitative singular strata

\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,0}_{r_{1},r_{2}}\subset\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,1}_{r_{1},r_{2}}\subset\ldots\subset\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,n-2}_{r_{1},r_{2}}\subset Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}

are defined as follows:

z\in\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,k}_{r_{1},r_{2}}
if and only if
\mathfrak{t}(z)-\epsilon^{-1}r_{2}^{2}\in\mathbb{I}^{-}
and for all
r\in[r_{1},r_{2}]
,
z
is not
(k+1,\epsilon,r)
-symmetric.

The following identity is clear from the above definitions: for any
L>1
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{k}=\bigcup_{\epsilon\in(0,L^{-1})}\bigcap_{0<r<\epsilon L}\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,k}_{r,\epsilon L}.

Next, we recall the following definition from
[
FL25a
, Definition 10.5]
.

Definition 2.21
.

A point
z\in Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
is called
(k,\epsilon,r)
-splitting
if
\mathfrak{t}(z)-10r^{2}\in\mathbb{I}^{-}
and there exists a noncollapsed Ricci flow limit space such that its regular part
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{[-10,0]}
splits off an
\mathbb{R}^{k}
as a Ricci flow spacetime. Moreover,

\displaystyle(Z,r^{-1}d_{Z},z,r^{-2}(\mathfrak{t}-\mathfrak{t}(z)))\quad\text{is $\epsilon$-close to}\quad(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})\quad\text{over}\quad[-10,0].

We also have the following concept of almost splitting maps as in
[
FL25a
, Definition 10.1]
, which will be of crucial importance in proving the rectifiability of the singular set.

Definition 2.22

(
(k,\epsilon,r)
-splitting map)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in\mathbb{I}^{++}}\}\in\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)
and
x_{0}^{*}=(x_{0},t_{0})\in\mathcal{X},r>0
with
[t_{0}-10r^{2},t_{0}]\subset\mathbb{I}
.
A map
\vec{u}=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{k})
is called a
(k,\epsilon,r)
-splitting map at
x_{0}^{*}
if for all
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
, the following properties hold:

(i)

u_{i}(x^{*}_{0})=0
.

(ii)

\square u_{i}=0
on
M\times[t_{0}-10r^{2},t_{0}]
.

(iii)

\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}-10r^{2}}^{t_{0}-r^{2}/10}\int_{M}|\nabla^{2}u_{i}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\mathrm{d}t\leq\epsilon.

(iv)

\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}-10r^{2}}^{t_{0}-r^{2}/10}\int_{M}\langle\nabla u_{i},\nabla u_{j}\rangle-\delta_{ij}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\mathrm{d}t=0.

For the basic properties of almost splitting maps, we refer readers to
[
FL25a
, Section 10]
. On a Ricci flow limit space
(Z,d_{Z},p_{\infty},\mathfrak{t})
obtained in Theorem
2.11
, we can generalize the above definition.

Definition 2.23
.

A map
\vec{u}=(u_{1},\cdots,u_{k})
is called a
(k,\epsilon,r)
-splitting map at
z\in Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
if
\mathfrak{t}(z)-10r^{2}\in\mathbb{I}^{-}
, and
\vec{u}
is obtained as the limit of a sequence of
(k,\epsilon,r)
-splitting maps
\vec{u}^{i}=(u^{i}_{1},\ldots,u^{i}_{k})
at
z_{i}^{*}
with
z_{i}^{*}\to z
in the Gromov–Hausdorff sense. Note that
\vec{u}
is defined on
Z_{(\mathfrak{t}(z)-10r^{2},+\infty)}
by reproduction formula.

Note that by taking the limit, all properties for smooth almost splitting maps hold for almost splitting maps on
Z
. We also have the following definition.

Definition 2.24
.

A point
z\in Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
is
(\epsilon,r)
-static
if
\mathfrak{t}(z)-2r^{2}\in\mathbb{I}^{-}
and

\displaystyle r^{2}\int_{\mathfrak{t}(z)-2r^{2}}^{\mathfrak{t}(z)-r^{2}/2}\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t}}|\mathrm{Ric}_{g^{Z}}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{z;t}\mathrm{d}t\leq\epsilon.

We end this section with the following volume estimate from
[
FL25a
, Proposition 5.35]
, which will be frequently used.

Proposition 2.25
.

For any
x\in Z
and
r>0
with
\mathfrak{t}(x)-r^{2}\in\mathbb{I}^{-}
, we have

\displaystyle 0<c(n,Y)r^{n+2}\leq|B^{*}_{Z}(x,r)|\leq C(n)r^{n+2},

where
|\cdot|
denotes the volume in
Z
; see
[
FL25a
, Definition 5.33]
.

2.3
Cylindrical and almost cylindrical points

We consider the standard Ricci flow solution on the cylinder:

\displaystyle{\@index{\@indexphrase{$\mathcal{C}^{k}$}}}\mathcal{C}^{k}:=(\bar{M},(\bar{g}(t))_{t<0},(\bar{f}(t))_{t<0})=\left(\mathbb{R}^{k}\times S^{n-k},g_{E}\times|t|g_{S^{n-k}},\frac{|\vec{x}|^{2}}{4|t|}+\frac{n-k}{2}+\Theta_{n-k}\right),

where
g_{E}
is the Euclidean metric on
\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
g_{S^{n-k}}
is the round metric on
S^{n-k}
such that
\mathrm{Ric}(g_{S^{n-k}})=g_{S^{n-k}}/2
. The vector
\vec{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})
denotes the standard coordinate function on
\mathbb{R}^{k}
. The constant
\Theta_{n-k}
is chosen to ensure that for any
t<0

\displaystyle(4\pi|t|)^{-\frac{n}{2}}\int_{\mathcal{C}^{k}_{t}}e^{-\bar{f}(t)}\,\mathrm{d}V_{\bar{g}(t)}=1.

We denote by
d_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
the spacetime distance on
\mathcal{C}^{k}
as in Definition
2.10
, with respect to a spacetime distance constant
\epsilon_{0}
depending on
n
and
Y
. Here, we implicitly assume
\Theta_{n-k}\geq-Y
.

Then, we set the completion of
\mathcal{C}^{k}
under
d_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
by
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{k}
. It is straightforward to verify that the metric completion adds only the singular set
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\{0\}
, which is the spine (see
[
FL25a
, Definition D.4]
) of
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{k}
.

We then define the base point
p^{*}
as the limit of
(\bar{p},t)
as
t\nearrow 0
with respect to
d_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
, where
\bar{p}\in\bar{M}
is a minimum point of
\bar{f}(-1)
. It is clear that
p^{*}
is independent of the choice of
\bar{p}
. Moreover, for any
t<0
,

\displaystyle\nu_{p^{*};t}=(4\pi|t|)^{-\frac{n}{2}}e^{-\bar{f}(t)}\,\mathrm{d}V_{\bar{g}(t)}.

For later application, we need the following lemma from
[
FL25a
, Lemma 8.5]
.

Lemma 2.26
.

For any
x^{*},y^{*}\in\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{k}_{0}
, we have

\displaystyle 0<c(n)|\vec{x}-\vec{y}|\leq d_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}(x^{*},y^{*})\leq\epsilon_{0}^{-1}|\vec{x}-\vec{y}|,

where
\vec{x}
and
\vec{y}
are components of
x^{*}
and
y^{*}
in
\mathbb{R}^{k}
, respectively.

Next, we consider a general noncollapsed Ricci flow limit space
(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})
over
\mathbb{I}
, obtained as the limit of a sequence in
\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)
. Then, we have the following definition from
[
FL25b
, Definition 2.22]
.

Definition 2.27
.

A point
z\in Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
is called a
cylindrical point with respect to
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{k}
if a tangent flow at
z
(see Definition
2.16
) is isometric to
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{k}
.

Note that by
[
FL25b
, Theorem 2.23]
, the tangent flow at a cylindrical point is unique. Next, we introduce the following definitions.

Definition 2.28
.

Let
\mathcal{S}^{k}_{\mathrm{c}}
be the subset of
\mathcal{S}^{k}
consisting of all cylindrical points with respect to
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{l}
for some
l\leq k
.

Definition 2.29
.

Let
(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})
be a noncollapsed Ricci flow limit space arising as the pointed Gromov–Hausdorff limit of a sequence in
\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)
. A point
z\in Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
is called
(k,\epsilon,r)
-cylindrical
if
\mathfrak{t}(z)-\epsilon^{-1}r^{2}\in\mathbb{I}^{-}
and

\displaystyle(Z,r^{-1}d_{Z},z,r^{-2}(\mathfrak{t}-\mathfrak{t}(z)))\quad\text{is $\epsilon$-close to}\quad(\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{k},d^{*}_{\mathcal{C}},p^{*},\mathfrak{t})\quad\text{over}\quad[-\epsilon^{-1},\epsilon^{-1}].

Let
\tilde{\phi}
be an
\epsilon
-map from Definition
2.13
, which is from
B^{*}(p^{*},\epsilon^{-1})\cap\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{k}_{[-\epsilon^{-1},\epsilon^{-1}]}
to
Z_{[\mathfrak{t}(z)-\epsilon^{-1}r^{2},\mathfrak{t}(z)+\epsilon^{-1}r^{2}]}
, where
B^{*}(p^{*},\epsilon^{-1})
is the metric ball in
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{k}
with respect to
d_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
. Then, we define
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{z,r}:=\tilde{\phi}\left(B^{*}(p^{*},\epsilon^{-1})\cap\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{k}_{0}\right),{\@index{\@indexphrase{$\mathcal{L}_{z,r}$}}}

and say that
z
is
(k,\epsilon,r)
-cylindrical with respect to
\mathcal{L}_{z,r}
. Note that
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{k}_{0}
is exactly the spine of
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{k}
.

3
Almost splitting maps on Ricci flows

Throughout this section, we assume
\mathcal{X}\in\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)
(see Definition
2.9
) and set

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}^{++}:=[-T,0],\quad\mathbb{I}^{+}:=[-0.99T,0],\quad\mathbb{I}:=[-0.98T,0],\quad\mathbb{I}^{-}:=(-0.98T,0].

Moreover, the spacetime distance
d^{*}
on
M\times\mathbb{I}^{+}
is defined as in Definition
2.10
, and the corresponding metric balls are denoted by
B^{*}
.

3.1
Construction of auxiliary functions

In this subsection, we fix a spacetime point
x_{0}^{*}=(x_{0},t_{0})\in\mathcal{X}
and set

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}=\mathrm{d}\nu_{x^{*}_{0};t}=(4\pi\tau)^{-n/2}e^{-f}\mathrm{d}V_{g(t)},

where
\tau=t_{0}-t
and
f=f_{x_{0}^{*}}
. For simplicity, we define

\begin{cases}&w:=\tau(2\Delta f-|\nabla f|^{2}+\mathrm{R})+f-n,\\
&\mathcal{T}:=\tau\mathrm{Ric}+\nabla^{2}(\tau f)-\frac{g}{2},\\
&F:=\tau f.\end{cases}

Recall that Perelman’s differential Harnack inequality (see
[
Per02
, Section 9]
) states
w\leq 0
.

For constructing almost splitting maps, we need the following lemma, which is similar to
[
FL25b
, Proposition 6.7]
.

Lemma 3.1
.

Suppose that
[t_{0}-30r^{2},t_{0}]\subset\mathbb{I}
and
u_{0}:M\times[t_{0}-30r^{2},t_{0}]\to\mathbb{R}
is a function satisfying:

\displaystyle\square u_{0}=-\frac{n}{2}\quad\text{and}\quad u_{0}=F\quad\mathrm{at}\quad t=-30r^{2}.

Then, the following statements hold.

(i)

We have

\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}-30r^{2}}^{t_{0}-r^{2}/30}\int_{M}\left|\tau\mathrm{Ric}+\nabla^{2}u_{0}-\frac{g}{2}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\mathrm{d}t\leq 75r^{2}\left(\mathcal{W}_{x_{0}^{*}}(r^{2}/30)-\mathcal{W}_{x_{0}^{*}}(30r^{2})\right).

(ii)

For any
t\in[t_{0}-30r^{2},t_{0}-r^{2}/30]
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{M}\left|\nabla(u_{0}-F)\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\leq 30r^{2}\left(\mathcal{W}_{x_{0}^{*}}(r^{2}/30)-\mathcal{W}_{x_{0}^{*}}(30r^{2})\right).

(iii)

For
0<\theta\leq\theta(n)
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[t_{0}-10r^{2},t_{0}-r^{2}/10]}\int_{M}|\nabla u_{0}|^{2}e^{\theta f}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}+\int_{t_{0}-10r^{2}}^{t_{0}-r^{2}/10}\int_{M}|\nabla^{2}u_{0}|^{2}e^{\theta f}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\mathrm{d}t\leq C(n,Y)r^{2}.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we assume
t_{0}=0
and
r=1
.

It is clear from
\partial_{t}f=-\Delta f+|\nabla f|^{2}-\mathrm{R}+\frac{n}{2\tau}
that

\displaystyle\square F=-w-\frac{n}{2}.

(3.1)

Set
u:=u_{0}-F
. Then by (
3.1
), the following evolution equation holds:

\displaystyle\square u=w\quad\text{and}\quad u=0\quad\mathrm{at}\quad t=-30.

By the weighted Bianchi identity (see Lemma
2.8
), we calculate

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\int_{M}\left|\nabla u\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}

\displaystyle=\int_{M}\square\left|\nabla u\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}

\displaystyle=2\int_{M}\langle\nabla\square u,\nabla u\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}-2\int_{M}\left|\nabla^{2}u\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}

\displaystyle=2\int_{M}\langle\nabla w,\nabla u\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}-2\int_{M}\left|\nabla^{2}u\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}

\displaystyle=4\int_{M}\langle\mathrm{div}_{f}\mathcal{T},\nabla u\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}-2\int_{M}\left|\nabla^{2}u\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}.

Using integration by parts and integrating in time, we obtain that for any
t_{1}\in[-30,0]
,

\displaystyle 2\int_{-30}^{t_{1}}\int_{M}\left|\nabla^{2}u\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=-4\int_{-30}^{t_{1}}\int_{M}\langle\mathcal{T},\nabla^{2}u\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\mathrm{d}t-\int_{M}\left|\nabla u\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t_{1}}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{-30}^{t_{1}}\int_{M}4\left|\mathcal{T}\right|^{2}+\left|\nabla^{2}u\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\mathrm{d}t

(3.2)

and thus we get

\int_{-30}^{-1/30}\int_{M}\left|\nabla^{2}u\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\mathrm{d}t\leq 4\int_{-30}^{-1/30}\int_{M}\left|\mathcal{T}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\mathrm{d}t.

(3.3)

Note that by Proposition
2.3
(iii),

\mathcal{W}_{x_{0}^{*}}(1/30)-\mathcal{W}_{x_{0}^{*}}(30)=2\int_{-30}^{-1/30}\int_{M}\tau^{-1}\left|\mathcal{T}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\mathrm{d}t\geq\frac{1}{15}\int_{-30}^{-1/30}\int_{M}\left|\mathcal{T}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\mathrm{d}t.

Combining this identity with (
3.3
) and using the definition of
u
and
\mathcal{T}
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{-30}^{-1/30}\int_{M}\left|\tau\mathrm{Ric}+\nabla^{2}u_{0}-\frac{g}{2}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\mathrm{d}t\leq 5\int_{-30}^{-1/30}\int_{M}\left|\mathcal{T}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\mathrm{d}t\leq 75\left(\mathcal{W}_{x_{0}^{*}}(1/30)-\mathcal{W}_{x_{0}^{*}}(30)\right).

Moreover, by (
3.1
), for any
t_{1}\in[-30,-1/30]
,

\displaystyle\int_{M}\left|\nabla u\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t_{1}}

\displaystyle\leq-4\int_{-30}^{t_{1}}\int_{M}\langle\mathcal{T},\nabla^{2}u\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\mathrm{d}t-2\int_{-30}^{t_{1}}\int_{M}|\nabla^{2}u|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq 2\int_{-30}^{-1/30}\int_{M}|\mathcal{T}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\mathrm{d}t\leq 30\left(\mathcal{W}_{x_{0}^{*}}(1/30)-\mathcal{W}_{x_{0}^{*}}(30)\right).

Thus,
u_{0}
satisfies properties (i) and (ii) above. Next, we focus on (iii).

Since
\square|\nabla u_{0}|^{2}=-2|\nabla^{2}u_{0}|^{2}\leq 0
, it follows from the hypercontractivity (see
[
Bam20a
, Theorem 12.1]
) that for any
t\in[-10,-1/10]
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{M}|\nabla u_{0}|^{4}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\leq\left(\int_{M}|\nabla u_{0}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{-30}\right)^{2}=\left(\int_{M}|\nabla F|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{-30}\right)^{2}\leq C(n,Y),

(3.4)

where we used Proposition
2.7
for the last inequality. Then, it follows from Proposition
2.7
that

\displaystyle\int_{M}|\nabla u_{0}|^{2}e^{\theta f}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\leq\left(\int_{M}|\nabla u_{0}|^{4}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{M}e^{2\theta f}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq C(n,Y)

for any
t\in[-10,-1/10]
. In particular, by Proposition
2.7
again, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[-10,-1/10]}\int_{M}|\nabla u|^{2}e^{\theta f}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\leq C(n,Y).

(3.5)

Moreover, we obtain by Proposition
2.7
and (
3.4
),

\displaystyle\int_{-10}^{-1/10}\int_{M}|\nabla u|^{4}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\mathrm{d}t\leq C(n,Y)+C\int_{-10}^{-1/10}\int_{M}|\nabla F|^{4}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\mathrm{d}t\leq C(n,Y).

(3.6)

For any small
\theta>0
, we compute

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\int_{M}\left|\nabla u\right|^{2}e^{\theta f}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}=\int_{M}\square\left(\left|\nabla u\right|^{2}e^{\theta f}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{M}-2|\nabla^{2}u|^{2}e^{\theta f}+4\langle\mathrm{div}_{f}\mathcal{T},\nabla u\rangle e^{\theta f}+|\nabla u|^{2}\square(e^{\theta f})-2\theta\langle\nabla|\nabla u|^{2},\nabla f\rangle e^{\theta f}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{M}-2|\nabla^{2}u|^{2}e^{\theta f}-4\langle\mathcal{T},\nabla^{2}u\rangle e^{\theta f}-4\theta\langle\mathcal{T},du\otimes df\rangle e^{\theta f}+|\nabla u|^{2}\square(e^{\theta f})-2\theta\langle\nabla|\nabla u|^{2},\nabla f\rangle e^{\theta f}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}
