r_{\mathrm{Rm}}(x)\leq 3r
for all sufficiently large
l
. Thus, by using
[
FL25a
, Theorem 1.12 (b)]
, we obtain

\displaystyle\iint_{B^{*}(x_{0,l}^{*};L/3)\cap M_{l}\times[-10,-1/10]\setminus\phi_{l}\left(\mathrm{supp}(\eta_{r,L})\right)}\left\lvert\langle\nabla h^{l}_{i},\nabla h^{l}_{j}\rangle\right\rvert\,\mathrm{d}\nu^{l}_{t}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C(n,Y)\left(\iint_{B^{*}(x_{0,l}^{*};L/3)\cap M_{l}\times[-10,-1/10]\setminus\phi_{l}\left(\mathrm{supp}(\eta_{r,L})\right)}1\,\mathrm{d}\nu^{l}_{t}\mathrm{d}t\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq C(n,L,Y)r.

(4.11)

Combining (
4.2
) and (
4.2
), we obtain

\displaystyle\iint_{M_{l}\times[-10,-1/10]\setminus B_{r,L}^{l}}\left\lvert\langle\nabla h^{l}_{i},\nabla h^{l}_{j}\rangle\right\rvert\,\mathrm{d}\nu^{l}_{t}\mathrm{d}t\leq C(n,Y)\Psi(L^{-1})+C(n,L,Y)r.

(4.12)

Similarly to (
4.2
), for sufficiently large
l
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\iint_{B_{r,L}^{l}}\left\lvert\langle\nabla h^{l}_{i},\nabla h^{l}_{j}\rangle\right\rvert\left(1-\eta_{r,L}\circ\phi_{l}^{-1}\right)\,\,\mathrm{d}\nu^{l}_{t}\mathrm{d}t\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(\iint_{B_{r,L}^{l}}|\nabla h^{l}_{i}|^{2}|\nabla h^{l}_{j}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu^{l}_{t}\mathrm{d}t\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\iint_{B_{r,L}^{l}}\left(1-\eta_{r,L}\circ\phi_{l}^{-1}\right)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu^{l}_{t}\mathrm{d}t\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq C(n,L,Y)r,

(4.13)

where the last inequality holds by the same reason as in (
4.2
).

Thus, we can first choose a large
L
and then a small
r
so that all integrals (
4.12
) and (
4.2
) are as small as we want. Combining this fact with (
4.9
), we obtain
a_{ij}^{l}\xrightarrow[l\to\infty]{}a_{ij}
. The convergence of eigenvalues follows directly.
∎

As a corollary of Lemma
4.9
by using Proposition
4.3
, we have

Corollary 4.10
.

For any
\epsilon>0
,
\{x_{i,l}^{*}\}_{1\leq i\leq k}
is a set of
(k,(1-\epsilon)\alpha,(1+\epsilon)\delta,r)
-independent points at
x_{0,l}^{*}
, provided that
l
is sufficiently large.

Next, we give the following characterization of the existence of a strongly
(k,\alpha,\delta,r)
-independent set at a point.

Lemma 4.11
.

Suppose
z_{0}\in Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
is
(\delta,r)
-selfsimilar and
(k,\delta,r)
-splitting. For any
\epsilon>0
, if
\delta\leq\delta(n,Y,\epsilon)
, then there exists a strongly
(k,1/4-\epsilon,\epsilon,r)
-independent set at
z_{0}
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we assume
r=1
and
\mathfrak{t}(z_{0})=0
.

Suppose that the conclusion fails. Then there exists a sequence of noncollapsed Ricci flow limit spaces
Z^{l}
over
\mathbb{I}_{l}
, obtained as the limit of a sequence of closed Ricci flows in
\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T_{l})
, where
\mathbb{I}_{l}=[-0.98T_{l},0]
. Moreover, there exists
z_{0,l}\in Z^{l}_{0}
that is
(l^{-2},1)
-selfsimilar and
(k,l^{-2},1)
-splitting. However, there exists no strongly
(k,1/4-\epsilon,\epsilon,r)
-independent set at
z_{0,l}
for some
\epsilon>0
.

By passing to a subsequence, we obtain the convergence

\displaystyle(Z^{l},d_{Z^{l}},z_{0,l},\mathfrak{t}_{l})\xrightarrow[l\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\hat{C}^{\infty}\hskip 8.19447pt}(Z,d_{Z},z_{0},\mathfrak{t}),

where
(Z,d_{Z},z_{0},\mathfrak{t})
is a Ricci shrinker space. Moreover, for the regular part
(\mathcal{R},\mathfrak{t},\partial_{\mathfrak{t}},g^{Z})
of
Z
,
\mathcal{R}_{t}
is connected for any
t<0
, and the Ricci shrinker equation is satisfied on
\mathcal{R}_{(-\infty,0)}
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ric}(g^{Z})+\nabla^{2}f_{z}=\frac{g^{Z}}{2\tau},

where
\tau=-\mathfrak{t}
. By our assumption,
\mathcal{R}
splits off an
\mathbb{R}^{k}
and hence it follows from
[
FL25a
, Proposition 8.4]
that there exists an isometry
\boldsymbol{\phi}^{s}
for
s\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
on
Z_{(-\infty,0]}
. Now, we set
z_{i}=\boldsymbol{\phi}^{e_{i}}(z_{0})
, where
(e_{1},\ldots,e_{k})
is the standard basis of
\mathbb{R}^{k}
. Then, by
[
FL25a
, Proposition 8.6]
, we have

\displaystyle\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.08334pt\hskip-2.08334pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\displaystyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-2.08334pt\hskip-2.77774pt\raisebox{-1.65277pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\displaystyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.77774pt\hskip 2.08334pt}\kern-7.49994pt}{\hbox{\hskip 2.08334pt\hskip-2.08334pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\textstyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-2.08334pt\hskip-2.77774pt\raisebox{-1.65277pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\textstyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.77774pt\hskip 2.08334pt}\kern-7.49994pt}{\hbox{\hskip 1.72743pt\hskip-1.72743pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\scriptstyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-1.72743pt\hskip-2.45831pt\raisebox{-1.15695pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.13998pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.13998pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.45831pt\hskip 1.72743pt}\kern-6.78813pt}{\hbox{\hskip 1.57988pt\hskip-1.57988pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-1.57988pt\hskip-2.29166pt\raisebox{-0.82639pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.09999pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.09999pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.29166pt\hskip 1.57988pt}\kern-6.49301pt}\int_{-10}^{-1/10}\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t}}\langle\nabla h_{i},\nabla h_{j}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{z;t}\mathrm{d}t=\frac{\delta_{ij}}{4},

(4.14)

for
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
, where
h_{i}=\tau(f_{z_{i}}-f_{z_{0}})
.

We choose
z_{i,l}\in Z^{l}
converging to
z_{i}
. On the one hand, it follows from Proposition
4.3
that
z_{i,l}
is
(\epsilon,1)
-selfsimilar for any
0\leq i\leq l
. On the other hand, by using Proposition
2.7
, it follows from the same argument as in the proof of Lemma
4.9
and (
4.14
) that

\displaystyle\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.08334pt\hskip-2.08334pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\displaystyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-2.08334pt\hskip-2.77774pt\raisebox{-1.65277pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\displaystyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.77774pt\hskip 2.08334pt}\kern-7.49994pt}{\hbox{\hskip 2.08334pt\hskip-2.08334pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\textstyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-2.08334pt\hskip-2.77774pt\raisebox{-1.65277pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\textstyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.77774pt\hskip 2.08334pt}\kern-7.49994pt}{\hbox{\hskip 1.72743pt\hskip-1.72743pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\scriptstyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-1.72743pt\hskip-2.45831pt\raisebox{-1.15695pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.13998pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.13998pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.45831pt\hskip 1.72743pt}\kern-6.78813pt}{\hbox{\hskip 1.57988pt\hskip-1.57988pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-1.57988pt\hskip-2.29166pt\raisebox{-0.82639pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.09999pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.09999pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.29166pt\hskip 1.57988pt}\kern-6.49301pt}\int_{-10}^{-1/10}\int_{\mathcal{R}^{l}_{t}}\langle\nabla h^{l}_{i},\nabla h^{l}_{j}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{z_{0,l};t}\mathrm{d}t\xrightarrow[l\to\infty]{}\frac{\delta_{ij}}{4},

where
\mathcal{R}^{l}
is the regular part of
Z^{l}
, and
h^{l}_{i}=\tau(f_{z_{i,l}}-f_{z_{0,l}})
. Thus, it is clear that
\{z_{i,l}\}_{1\leq i\leq k}
is a strongly
(k,1/4-\epsilon,\epsilon,1)
-independent set at
z_{0,l}
, which contradicts our assumption.

Consequently, the proof is complete.
∎

By taking the limit of
(k,\epsilon,r)
-splitting maps at
x_{0}^{*}
and taking the limit of
\mathcal{W}
-entropy together with Definition
4.4
, the following counterpart of Corollary
3.10
still holds on Ricci flow limit spaces.

Theorem 4.12

(Sharp splitting map on Ricci flow limit spaces)
.

Let
(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})
be a Ricci flow limit space with
z_{0}\in Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
such that
z_{0}
is
(\delta,r)
-selfsimilar and
(k,\delta,r)
-splitting. For any
\epsilon>0
and
\alpha\in(0,1/5)
, If
\epsilon\leq\epsilon(n,Y,\alpha)
and
\delta\leq\delta(n,Y,\epsilon)
, then there exists a
(k,\epsilon^{\prime},r)
-splitting map
\vec{u}=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{k}):Z_{(\mathfrak{t}(z_{0})-10r^{2},\mathfrak{t}(z_{0})]}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
at
z_{0}
, where

\displaystyle\epsilon^{\prime}=C(n,Y,\alpha)\mathfrak{S}_{r}^{k,\alpha,\epsilon}(z_{0}).

Proof.

Since
\delta\leq\delta(n,Y,\epsilon)
, it follows from Lemma
4.11
that there exist points
\{z_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq k}
which are strongly
(k,\alpha,\epsilon,r)
-independent points at
z_{0}
such that

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{k}\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{z_{i}}(r^{2}/40)-\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{z_{i}}(40r^{2})\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq 2\mathfrak{S}_{r}^{k,\alpha,\epsilon}(z_{0}).

Assume that
x_{i,l}^{*}\in\mathcal{X}^{l}
converge to
z_{i}
for
0\leq i\leq k
. By Corollary
4.10
, we know that
\{x_{i,l}^{*}\}_{1\leq i\leq k}
are
(k,\alpha/2,2\epsilon,r)
-independent at
x_{0,l}^{*}
, for sufficiently large
l
. By Proposition
3.8
, if
\epsilon\leq\epsilon(n,Y,\alpha)
, we can find a
(k,\epsilon_{l},r)
-splitting map
\vec{u}^{l}=(u^{l}_{1},\ldots,u^{l}_{k}):M_{l}\times[\mathfrak{t}_{l}(x_{0,l}^{*})-10r^{2},\mathfrak{t}_{l}(x_{0,l}^{*})]\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
at
x_{0,l}^{*}
, where

\displaystyle\epsilon_{l}=C(n,Y,\alpha)\sum_{i=0}^{k}\left(\mathcal{W}_{x_{i,l}^{*}}(r^{2}/30)-\mathcal{W}_{x_{i,l}^{*}}(30r^{2})\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

In particular, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k}\int^{\mathfrak{t}_{l}(x_{0,l}^{*})-r^{2}/10}_{\mathfrak{t}_{l}(x_{0,l}^{*})-10r^{2}}\int_{M_{l}}\left|\nabla^{2}u^{l}_{i}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x_{0,l}^{*};t}\mathrm{d}t\leq C(n,Y,\alpha)\sum_{i=0}^{k}\left(\mathcal{W}_{x_{i,l}^{*}}(r^{2}/30)-\mathcal{W}_{x_{i,l}^{*}}(30r^{2})\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(4.15)

Taking the limit for
l\to\infty
in (
4.15
) and using Proposition
4.3
and Definition
4.4
, we conclude that
\vec{u}^{l}
converge to a
(k,\epsilon^{\prime},r)
-splitting map
\vec{u}=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{k})
at
z_{0}
, where

\displaystyle\epsilon^{\prime}=C(n,Y,\alpha)\sum_{i=0}^{k}\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{z_{i}}(r^{2}/40)-\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{z_{i}}(40r^{2})\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}=C(n,Y,\alpha)\mathfrak{S}_{r}^{k,\alpha,\epsilon}(z_{0}).

This completes the proof. ∎

Next, we have the following definition.

Definition 4.13

(Limiting heat flow)
.

Given a positive constant
L
, a map
h:Z_{(\mathfrak{t}(z)-L,\mathfrak{t}(z)]}\to\mathbb{R}
is called a
limiting heat flow at
z\in Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
if
h|_{\mathcal{R}_{(\mathfrak{t}(z)-L,\mathfrak{t}(z)]}}
is a smooth limit of a sequence
h^{l}:M_{l}\times[\mathfrak{t}_{l}(x_{l}^{*})-L,\mathfrak{t}_{l}(x_{l}^{*})]\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\square h^{l}=0
and

\displaystyle\int_{M_{l}}|\nabla h^{l}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x_{l}^{*};\mathfrak{t}_{l}(x_{l}^{*})-L}\leq C

for a sequence
x_{l}^{*}\in\mathcal{X}^{l}
converging to
z
and a constant
C<\infty
. In particular, any component of a
(k,\epsilon,r)
-splitting map is a limiting heat flow.

Theorem 4.14

(Hessian decay of limiting heat flows)
.

Let
\eta>0
be a fixed constant. Suppose that
z\in Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
is
(\delta,r)
-selfsimilar and is not
(k+1,\eta,r)
-splitting.

Let
\vec{u}=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{k}):Z_{(\mathfrak{t}(z)-10r^{2},\mathfrak{t}(z))}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
be a
(k,\delta,r)
-splitting map at
z
, and
h:Z_{(\mathfrak{t}(z)-10r^{2},\mathfrak{t}(z))}\to\mathbb{R}
be a limiting heat flow. Define

\displaystyle v:=h-\sum_{i=1}^{k}b_{i}u_{i},\quad where\quad b_{i}:=\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.08334pt\hskip-2.08334pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\displaystyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-2.08334pt\hskip-2.77774pt\raisebox{-1.65277pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\displaystyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.77774pt\hskip 2.08334pt}\kern-7.49994pt}{\hbox{\hskip 2.08334pt\hskip-2.08334pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\textstyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-2.08334pt\hskip-2.77774pt\raisebox{-1.65277pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\textstyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.77774pt\hskip 2.08334pt}\kern-7.49994pt}{\hbox{\hskip 1.72743pt\hskip-1.72743pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\scriptstyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-1.72743pt\hskip-2.45831pt\raisebox{-1.15695pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.13998pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.13998pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.45831pt\hskip 1.72743pt}\kern-6.78813pt}{\hbox{\hskip 1.57988pt\hskip-1.57988pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-1.57988pt\hskip-2.29166pt\raisebox{-0.82639pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.09999pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.09999pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.29166pt\hskip 1.57988pt}\kern-6.49301pt}\int_{\mathfrak{t}(z)-8r^{2}}^{\mathfrak{t}(z)-8r^{2}/10}\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t}}\langle\nabla u,\nabla u_{i}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{z;t}\mathrm{d}t.

Then there exists a constant
\theta=\theta(n,Y,\eta)\in(0,1)
such that the following holds: if
\delta\leq\delta(n,Y,\eta)
, then

\int_{\mathfrak{t}(z)-2r^{2}}^{\mathfrak{t}(z)-2r^{2}/10}\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t}}\left|\nabla^{2}v\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{z;t}\mathrm{d}t\leq\theta\int_{\mathfrak{t}(z)-8r^{2}}^{\mathfrak{t}(z)-8r^{2}/10}\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t}}\left|\nabla^{2}v\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{z;t}\mathrm{d}t.

Proof.

By Definition
2.23
,
\vec{u}
is obtained as the limit of
\vec{u}^{l}=(u^{l}_{1},\ldots,u^{l}_{k})
, which is a
(k,\delta,r)
-splitting map at
x_{l}^{*}\in\mathcal{X}^{l}
, where
x_{l}^{*}
converge to
z
.

Moreover, by Definition
4.13
, there exist
h^{l}:M_{l}\times[\mathfrak{t}_{l}(x_{l}^{*})-10r^{2},\mathfrak{t}_{l}(x_{l}^{*})]\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying

\displaystyle\square h^{l}=0,\quad\int_{M_{l}}\left|\nabla h^{l}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x_{l}^{*};\mathfrak{t}_{l}(x_{l}^{*})-10r^{2}}\leq C_{0},\quad h^{l}\xrightarrow[l\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447ptC_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\infty}(\mathcal{R})\hskip 8.19447pt}h.

Define
v^{l}
and
b^{l}_{i}
by

\displaystyle v^{l}:=h^{l}-\sum_{i=1}^{k}b_{i}^{l}u_{i}^{l},\quad where\quad b_{i}^{l}:=\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.08334pt\hskip-2.08334pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\displaystyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-2.08334pt\hskip-2.77774pt\raisebox{-1.65277pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\displaystyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.77774pt\hskip 2.08334pt}\kern-7.49994pt}{\hbox{\hskip 2.08334pt\hskip-2.08334pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\textstyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-2.08334pt\hskip-2.77774pt\raisebox{-1.65277pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\textstyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.77774pt\hskip 2.08334pt}\kern-7.49994pt}{\hbox{\hskip 1.72743pt\hskip-1.72743pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\scriptstyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-1.72743pt\hskip-2.45831pt\raisebox{-1.15695pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.13998pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.13998pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.45831pt\hskip 1.72743pt}\kern-6.78813pt}{\hbox{\hskip 1.57988pt\hskip-1.57988pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-1.57988pt\hskip-2.29166pt\raisebox{-0.82639pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.09999pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.09999pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.29166pt\hskip 1.57988pt}\kern-6.49301pt}\int_{\mathfrak{t}_{l}(x_{l}^{*})-8r^{2}}^{\mathfrak{t}_{l}(x_{l}^{*})-8r^{2}/10}\int_{M_{l}}\langle\nabla h^{l},\nabla u_{i}^{l}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x_{l}^{*};t}\mathrm{d}t.

Then we can argue as in the proof of Lemma
4.9
to conclude that

\displaystyle b_{i}^{l}\xrightarrow[l\to\infty]{}b_{i},\quad v^{l}\xrightarrow[l\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447ptC_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\infty}(\mathcal{R})\hskip 8.19447pt}v.

By a similar argument as in the proof of Claim
3.13
, we have, for any
t\geq\mathfrak{t}(z)-9r^{2}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{l\to\infty}\int_{M_{l}}\left|\nabla v^{l}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu^{l}_{t}=\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t}}|\nabla v|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{z;t}.

In addition, the same proof leading to Claim
3.15
yields that for any
\mathfrak{t}(z)-9r^{2}\leq t_{1}<t_{2}<\mathfrak{t}(z)
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t_{1}}}|\nabla v|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{z;t_{1}}-\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t_{2}}}|\nabla v|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{z;t_{2}}=2\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t}}\left|\nabla^{2}v\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{z;t}\mathrm{d}t.

Combining all the above convergence results, the conclusion follows from Theorem
3.12
.
∎

4.3
Nondegeneration theorem

In this subsection, we first investigate the behavior of an almost splitting map when restricted to a smaller scale.

Theorem 4.15
.

Given constants
\eta>0
,
\alpha\in(0,1/5)
,
\epsilon>0
and
\epsilon^{\prime}>0
, suppose
z_{0}\in Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
with
t_{0}:=\mathfrak{t}(z_{0})
satisfies that for any
s\in[\bar{r}r,r]
,
z_{0}
is
(\delta,s)
-selfsimilar and
(k,\delta,s)
-splitting but not
(k+1,\eta,s)
-splitting.
Let
\vec{u}=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{k}):Z_{(t_{0}-10r^{2},t_{0}]}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
be a
(k,\epsilon^{\prime},r)
-splitting map at
z_{0}
. If
\epsilon\leq\epsilon(n,Y,\alpha)
and
\delta\leq\delta(n,Y,\eta,\alpha,\epsilon)
, then we can find
\beta=\beta(n,Y,\eta)>0
such that for any
s\in[\bar{r},1]
, the following Hessian estimate holds:

\sum_{i=1}^{k}\int_{t_{0}-10s^{2}r^{2}}^{t_{0}-s^{2}r^{2}/10}\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t}}\left|\nabla^{2}u_{i}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{z_{0};t}\mathrm{d}t\leq C(n,Y,\alpha)\left(\sum_{s\leq r_{j}=2^{-j}\leq 1}\left(\frac{s}{r_{j}}\right)^{\beta}\mathfrak{S}_{r_{j}r}^{k,\alpha,\epsilon}(z)+\epsilon^{\prime}s^{\beta}\right).

(4.16)

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we assume
t_{0}=0
and
r=1
.

By Theorem
4.12
, for any
j
with
r_{j}\in[s,1]
, we can find a
(k,\epsilon_{j},r_{j})
-splitting map
\vec{v}^{j}=(v_{1}^{j},\ldots,v_{k}^{j})
based at
z_{0}
which satisfies

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k}\int_{-10r_{j}^{2}}^{-r_{j}^{2}/10}\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t}}\left|\nabla^{2}v_{i}^{j}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{z_{0};t}\mathrm{d}t\leq\epsilon_{j}:=C(n,Y,\alpha)\mathfrak{S}^{k,\alpha,\epsilon}_{r_{j}}(z_{0}).

For any
j
, we define
\vec{u}^{j}=(u^{j}_{1},\ldots,u^{j}_{k})
by

\displaystyle u^{j}_{i}:=u_{i}-\sum_{l=1}^{k}b^{j}_{i,l}v^{j}_{l},\quad\mathrm{where}\quad b^{j}_{i,l}:=\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.08334pt\hskip-2.08334pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\displaystyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-2.08334pt\hskip-2.77774pt\raisebox{-1.65277pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\displaystyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.77774pt\hskip 2.08334pt}\kern-7.49994pt}{\hbox{\hskip 2.08334pt\hskip-2.08334pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\textstyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-2.08334pt\hskip-2.77774pt\raisebox{-1.65277pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\textstyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.77774pt\hskip 2.08334pt}\kern-7.49994pt}{\hbox{\hskip 1.72743pt\hskip-1.72743pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\scriptstyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-1.72743pt\hskip-2.45831pt\raisebox{-1.15695pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.13998pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.13998pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.45831pt\hskip 1.72743pt}\kern-6.78813pt}{\hbox{\hskip 1.57988pt\hskip-1.57988pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-1.57988pt\hskip-2.29166pt\raisebox{-0.82639pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.09999pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.09999pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.29166pt\hskip 1.57988pt}\kern-6.49301pt}\int_{-8r_{j}^{2}}^{-8r_{j}^{2}/10}\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t}}\langle\nabla u_{i},\nabla v^{j}_{l}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{z_{0};t}\mathrm{d}t.

Since
\vec{v}^{j}
and
\vec{u}
are almost splitting maps, we have by
[
FL25a
, Proposition 10.2]
,

\displaystyle|b^{j}_{i,l}|^{2}\leq C\left(\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.08334pt\hskip-2.08334pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\displaystyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-2.08334pt\hskip-2.77774pt\raisebox{-1.65277pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\displaystyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.77774pt\hskip 2.08334pt}\kern-7.49994pt}{\hbox{\hskip 2.08334pt\hskip-2.08334pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\textstyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-2.08334pt\hskip-2.77774pt\raisebox{-1.65277pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\textstyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.77774pt\hskip 2.08334pt}\kern-7.49994pt}{\hbox{\hskip 1.72743pt\hskip-1.72743pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\scriptstyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-1.72743pt\hskip-2.45831pt\raisebox{-1.15695pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.13998pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.13998pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.45831pt\hskip 1.72743pt}\kern-6.78813pt}{\hbox{\hskip 1.57988pt\hskip-1.57988pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-1.57988pt\hskip-2.29166pt\raisebox{-0.82639pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.09999pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.09999pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.29166pt\hskip 1.57988pt}\kern-6.49301pt}\int_{-8r_{j}^{2}}^{-8r_{j}^{2}/10}\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t}}|\nabla u_{i}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{z_{0};t}\mathrm{d}t\right)\left(\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.08334pt\hskip-2.08334pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\displaystyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-2.08334pt\hskip-2.77774pt\raisebox{-1.65277pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\displaystyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.77774pt\hskip 2.08334pt}\kern-7.49994pt}{\hbox{\hskip 2.08334pt\hskip-2.08334pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\textstyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-2.08334pt\hskip-2.77774pt\raisebox{-1.65277pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\textstyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.77774pt\hskip 2.08334pt}\kern-7.49994pt}{\hbox{\hskip 1.72743pt\hskip-1.72743pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\scriptstyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-1.72743pt\hskip-2.45831pt\raisebox{-1.15695pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.13998pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.13998pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.45831pt\hskip 1.72743pt}\kern-6.78813pt}{\hbox{\hskip 1.57988pt\hskip-1.57988pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-1.57988pt\hskip-2.29166pt\raisebox{-0.82639pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.09999pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.09999pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.29166pt\hskip 1.57988pt}\kern-6.49301pt}\int_{-8r_{j}^{2}}^{-8r_{j}^{2}/10}\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t}}|\nabla v_{l}^{j}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{z_{0};t}\mathrm{d}t\right)\leq C

where
C
is a universal constant.

Since
\vec{v}^{j}
is a
(k,\epsilon_{j},r_{j})
-splitting map and
\delta\leq\delta(n,Y,\eta,\alpha,\epsilon)
, it follows from Theorem
4.14
, we obtain for
i\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
,

\displaystyle\int_{-2r_{j}^{2}}^{-2r_{j}^{2}/10}\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t}}\left|\nabla^{2}u_{i}^{j}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{z_{0};t}\mathrm{d}t\leq\theta\int_{-8r_{j}^{2}}^{-8r_{j}^{2}/10}\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t}}\left|\nabla^{2}u_{i}^{j}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{z_{0};t}\mathrm{d}t.

Then we have

\displaystyle\int_{-2r_{j}^{2}}^{-2r_{j}^{2}/10}\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t}}\left|\nabla^{2}u_{i}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{z_{0};t}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{-2r_{j}^{2}}^{-2r_{j}^{2}/10}\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t}}\left|\nabla^{2}u_{i}^{j}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{z_{0};t}\mathrm{d}t+C\sum_{l=1}^{k}\int_{-2r_{j}^{2}}^{-2r_{j}^{2}/10}\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t}}\left|\nabla^{2}v_{l}^{j}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{z_{0};t}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\theta\int_{-8r_{j}^{2}}^{-8r_{j}^{2}/10}\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t}}\left|\nabla^{2}u_{i}^{j}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{z_{0};t}\mathrm{d}t+C\sum_{l=1}^{k}\int_{-2r_{j}^{2}}^{-2r_{j}^{2}/10}\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t}}\left|\nabla^{2}v_{l}^{j}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{z_{0};t}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\theta\int_{-8r_{j}^{2}}^{-8r_{j}^{2}/10}\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t}}\left|\nabla^{2}u_{i}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{z_{0};t}\mathrm{d}t+C\left(\sum_{l=1}^{k}\int_{-8r_{j}^{2}}^{-8r_{j}^{2}/10}\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t}}\left|\nabla^{2}v_{l}^{j}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{z_{0};t}\mathrm{d}t+\sum_{l=1}^{k}\int_{-2r_{j}^{2}}^{-2r_{j}^{2}/10}\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t}}\left|\nabla^{2}v_{l}^{j}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{z_{0};t}\mathrm{d}t\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\theta\int_{-8r_{j}^{2}}^{-8r_{j}^{2}/10}\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t}}\left|\nabla^{2}u_{i}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{z_{0};t}\mathrm{d}t+C(n,Y,\alpha)\mathfrak{S}_{r_{j}}^{k,\alpha,\epsilon}(z_{0}).

(4.17)

We can rewrite (
4.3
) as

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k}\int_{-8r_{j+1}^{2}}^{-8r_{j+1}^{2}/10}\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t}}\left|\nabla^{2}u_{i}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{z_{0};t}\mathrm{d}t\leq\theta\sum_{i=1}^{k}\int_{-8r_{j}^{2}}^{-8r_{j}^{2}/10}\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t}}\left|\nabla^{2}u_{i}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{z_{0};t}\mathrm{d}t+C(n,Y,\alpha)\mathfrak{S}_{r_{j}}^{k,\alpha,\epsilon}(z_{0}).

(4.18)

Iterating (
4.18
), it follows that

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k}\int_{-8r_{j}^{2}}^{-8r_{j}^{2}/10}\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t}}\left|\nabla^{2}u_{i}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{z_{0};t}\mathrm{d}t\leq\theta^{j}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\int_{-8r_{0}^{2}}^{-8r_{0}^{2}/10}\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t}}\left|\nabla^{2}u_{i}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{z_{0};t}\mathrm{d}t+C(n,Y,\alpha)\sum_{l=0}^{j-1}\theta^{j-1-l}\mathfrak{S}_{r_{l}}^{k,\alpha,\epsilon}(z_{0}).

(4.19)

Note that
r_{0}=1
and thus

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k}\int_{-8r_{0}^{2}}^{-8r_{0}^{2}/10}\int_{M}\left|\nabla^{2}u_{i}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{z_{0};t}\mathrm{d}t\leq k\epsilon^{\prime}.

(4.20)

Choosing
\beta
such that
2^{-\beta}=\theta
, we then obtain from (
4.19
) and (
4.20
) that for any
s\in[\bar{r},1]
,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k}\int_{-10s^{2}}^{-s^{2}/10}\int_{M}\left|\nabla^{2}u_{i}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{z_{0};t}\mathrm{d}t\leq C\epsilon^{\prime}s^{\beta}+C(n,Y,\alpha)\sum_{s\leq r_{j}\leq 1}\left(\frac{s}{r_{j}}\right)^{\beta}\mathfrak{S}_{r_{j}}^{k,\alpha,\epsilon}(z_{0}).

This gives (
4.16
) and thus finishes the proof.
∎

Theorem 4.16

(Nondegeneration for almost splitting maps)
.

Given constants
\eta>0
,
\alpha\in(0,1/5)
,
\epsilon>0
and
\epsilon^{\prime}>0
, suppose
z_{0}\in Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
with
t_{0}:=\mathfrak{t}(z_{0})
satisfies for any
s\in[\bar{r}r,r]
,
z_{0}
is
(\delta,s)
-selfsimilar and
(k,\delta,s)
-splitting but not
(k+1,\eta,s)
-splitting.

Let
\vec{u}=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{k}):Z_{(t_{0}-10r^{2},t_{0}]}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
be a
(k,\epsilon^{\prime},r)
-splitting map at
z
. If
\epsilon\leq\epsilon(n,Y,\alpha)
,
\delta\leq\delta(n,Y,\eta,\alpha,\epsilon)
,
\epsilon^{\prime}\leq\epsilon^{\prime}(n,Y,\eta,\alpha)
and

\sum_{\bar{r}\leq r_{j}=2^{-j}\leq 1}\mathfrak{S}^{k,\alpha,\epsilon}_{rr_{j}}(z_{0})<\epsilon^{\prime},

then for all
s\in[\bar{r},1]
, there exist a constant
C=C(n,Y,\eta,\alpha)>0
and a matrix
T_{s}
with
\|T_{s}-\mathrm{Id}\|\leq C\epsilon^{\prime}
such that the map
\vec{u}_{s}:=T_{s}\vec{u}:M\times(t_{0}-10s^{2}r^{2},t_{0}]\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
is a
(k,C\epsilon^{\prime},sr)
-splitting map at
z_{0}
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we assume
t_{0}=0
and
r=1
. We only need to prove the theorem for
s=r_{j}=2^{-j}
and the general case is similar. Also, we use the same notations as in Theorem
4.15
. Then it follows that

\displaystyle\sum_{a=1}^{k}\int_{-10r_{j}^{2}}^{-r_{j}^{2}/10}\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t}}\left|\nabla^{2}u_{a}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{z_{0};t}\mathrm{d}t\leq C(n,Y,\alpha)\left(\sum_{i=0}^{j}2^{(i-j)\beta}\mathfrak{S}_{r_{i}}^{k,\alpha,\epsilon}(z_{0})+2^{-j\beta}\epsilon^{\prime}\right).

(4.21)

Claim 4.17
.

For any
t^{\prime}\in[-10r_{j}^{2},-r_{j}^{2}/10]
,

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq a,b\leq k}\left\lvert\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t^{\prime}}}\langle\nabla u_{a},\nabla u_{b}\rangle-\delta_{ab}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{z_{0};t^{\prime}}\right\rvert\leq C(n,Y,\eta,\alpha)\epsilon^{\prime}.

Note that for any
t\in[-10r_{0}^{2},-r_{0}^{2}/10]
, it follows from
[
FL25a
, Proposition 10.2]
that

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq a,b\leq k}\left\lvert\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t}}\langle\nabla u_{a},\nabla u_{b}\rangle-\delta_{ab}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{z_{0};t}\right\rvert\leq C(n)\epsilon^{\prime}.

(4.22)

Then by (
4.21
) and the same argument in obtaining Claim
3.15
, we have
that for any
t^{\prime}\in[-10r_{i}^{2},-r_{i}^{2}/10]
and
a,b\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
,

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t^{\prime}}}\langle\nabla u_{a},\nabla u_{b}\rangle-\delta_{ab}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{z_{0};t^{\prime}}-\int_{\mathcal{R}_{-10r_{i}^{2}}}\langle\nabla u_{a},\nabla u_{b}\rangle-\delta_{ab}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{z_{0};-10r_{i}^{2}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\int_{-10r_{i}^{2}}^{-r_{i}^{2}/10}\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t}}\left|\nabla^{2}u_{a}\right|\left|\nabla^{2}u_{b}\right|\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{z_{0};t}\mathrm{d}t\leq C(n,Y,\alpha)\left(\sum_{l=0}^{i}2^{(l-i)\beta}\mathfrak{S}_{r_{l}}^{k,\alpha,\epsilon}(z_{0})+2^{-i\beta}\epsilon^{\prime}\right).

(4.23)

Iterating (
4.3
) and using (
4.22
), it follows that for any
t^{\prime}\in[-10r_{j}^{2},-r_{j}^{2}/10]
and
a,b\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
,

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t^{\prime}}}\langle\nabla u_{a},\nabla u_{b}\rangle-\delta_{ab}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{z_{0};t^{\prime}}\right|\leq C(n)\epsilon^{\prime}+C(n,Y,\alpha)\sum_{i=0}^{j}\left(\sum_{l=0}^{i}2^{(l-i)\beta}\mathfrak{S}_{r_{l}}^{k,\alpha,\epsilon}(z_{0})+2^{-i\beta}\epsilon^{\prime}\right).

(4.24)

Note that
\beta=\beta(n,Y,\eta)>0
, thus we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{j}2^{-i\beta}\epsilon^{\prime}\leq C(n,Y,\eta)\epsilon^{\prime}.

(4.25)

On the other hand, by assumption, it holds that

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{j}\sum_{l=0}^{i}2^{(l-i)\beta}\mathfrak{S}_{r_{l}}^{k,\alpha,\epsilon}(z_{0})=

\displaystyle\sum_{l=0}^{j}\sum_{i=l}^{j}2^{(l-i)\beta}\mathfrak{S}_{r_{l}}^{k,\alpha,\epsilon}(z_{0})\leq C(n,Y,\eta)\sum_{l=0}^{j}\mathfrak{S}_{r_{l}}^{k,\alpha,\epsilon}(z_{0})\leq C(n,Y,\eta)\epsilon^{\prime}.

(4.26)

Plugging (
4.25
) and (
4.26
) into (
4.24
), we conclude that for any
t^{\prime}\in[-10r_{j}^{2},-r_{j}^{2}/10]
and
a,b\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
,

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t^{\prime}}}\langle\nabla u_{a},\nabla u_{b}\rangle-\delta_{ab}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{z_{0};t^{\prime}}\right|\leq C(n,Y,\eta,\alpha)\epsilon^{\prime}.

This proves Claim
4.17
.

By Claim
4.17
, we can find a
k\times k
matrix
T_{r_{j}}
with
\|T_{r_{j}}-\mathrm{Id}\|\leq C(n,Y,\eta,\alpha)\epsilon^{\prime}
such that the map
\vec{u}_{r_{j}}:=T_{r_{j}}\vec{u}=(u_{r_{j},1},\ldots,u_{r_{j},k}):Z_{[-10s_{j}^{2},0]}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
satisfies

\displaystyle\int_{-10r_{j}^{2}}^{-r_{j}^{2}/10}\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t}}\langle\nabla u_{r_{j},a},\nabla u_{r_{j},b}\rangle-\delta_{ab}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{z_{0};t}\mathrm{d}t=0.

Moreover, using (
4.21
), we have

\displaystyle\sum_{a=1}^{k}\int_{-10r_{j}^{2}}^{-r_{j}^{2}/10}\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t}}\left|\nabla^{2}u_{r_{j},a}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{z_{0};t}\mathrm{d}t\leq C(n,Y,\eta,\alpha)\epsilon^{\prime}.

Thus,
\vec{u}_{r_{j}}
is a
(k,C\epsilon^{\prime},r_{j})
-splitting map at
z_{0}
, where
C=C(n,Y,\eta,\alpha)
. This finishes the proof.
∎

We end this section with the following covering result, which is a direct consequence of Lemma
3.18
by taking the limit.

Lemma 4.18
.

Suppose that
z_{0}\in Z
is
(\delta,r)
-selfsimilar. Let
S
be the subset consisting of all
(\delta,r)
-selfsimilar points in
B_{Z}^{*}(z_{0},r)\bigcap Z_{[\mathfrak{t}(z_{0})-\alpha^{2}r^{2}/L^{2},\mathfrak{t}(z_{0})+\alpha^{2}r^{2}/L^{2}]}
, where
L=L(n,Y)>1
. Assume that there exists no strongly
(k,\alpha,\delta,r)
-independent points in
S
at
z_{0}
. If
\delta\leq\delta(n,Y,\alpha)
, then we can find
\{z_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq N}\subset S
with
N\leq C(n,Y)\alpha^{1-k}
and

\displaystyle S\subset\bigcup_{i=0}^{N}B_{Z}^{*}(z_{i},\alpha r).

4.4
Minkowski dimension and quantitative estimates of singular strata

In this subsection, we consider
(k,\epsilon,r)
-symmetric points (see Definition
2.19
) in
Z
and investigate the
k
-th stratum
\mathcal{S}^{k}
.

First, we prove the following static estimate on
Z
.

Lemma 4.19
.

There exists a constant
C=C(n,Y)>0
such that the following holds for any
\epsilon>0
and
\beta>0
: if
x,y\in Z
are
(\delta,r)
-selfsimilar, with
d_{Z}(x,y)\leq r
and
|\mathfrak{t}(x)-\mathfrak{t}(y)|\geq\beta r^{2}
, then both
x
and
y
are
(C\delta^{\frac{1}{2}}\beta^{-2},r)
-static (see Definition
2.24
).

Proof.

The result follows directly from Theorem
3.6
, Proposition
4.3
, and a standard limiting argument.
∎

The following result follows immediately from Definition
2.24
, Proposition
3.11
, and a standard limiting argument. For (ii), we also need
[
Bam20b
, Claim 22.7]
.

Proposition 4.20
.

For any
\epsilon>0
, if
\delta\leq\delta(n,Y,\epsilon)
, then the following statements hold:

(i)

If
z\in Z
is
(\delta,r)
-selfsimilar and there exists a
(k,\delta,r)
-splitting map at
z
, then
z
is
(k,\epsilon,s)
-symmetric for any
s\in[\epsilon r,\epsilon^{-1}r]
.

(ii)

If
z\in Z
is both
(\delta,r)
-selfsimilar and
(\delta,r)
-static, then
z
is
(\epsilon,s)
-static for any
s\in[\epsilon r,\epsilon^{-1}r]
.

Next, we prove the covering result.

Lemma 4.21
.

Given
z_{0}\in Z
,
\epsilon>0
,
\beta>0
and
r>0
, let
S
be the subset consisting of all
(\delta,r)
-selfsimilar points in
B^{*}(z_{0},r)
which are not
(k+1,\epsilon,s)
-symmetric for any
s\in[\epsilon^{1/2}\beta r,r]
. If
\delta\leq\delta(n,Y,\epsilon,\beta)
, then we can find
\{z_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq N}\subset S
with
N\leq C(n,Y)\beta^{-k}
and

\displaystyle S\subset\bigcup_{i=1}^{N}B^{*}(z_{i},\beta r).

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we assume
r=1
. Let
\{z_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq N}\subset S
be a maximal
\beta
-separated set, i.e.,
d_{Z}(z_{i},z_{j})\geq\beta
for all
i\neq j
. Then it is clear that

\displaystyle S\subset\bigcup_{i=1}^{N}B^{*}(z_{i},\beta).

Suppose, for contradiction, that the desired conclusion fails. Then we assume

\displaystyle N\gg C(n,Y)\beta^{-k}.

(4.27)

We now consider two cases to derive a contradiction.

Case 1
: There exists
i\in[1,N]
such that
|\mathfrak{t}(z_{i})-\mathfrak{t}(z_{j})|\leq 3\beta^{2}
for any
j\neq i
.

Without loss of generality, we assume
i=1
. In this case, we claim that if
\delta\leq\delta(n,Y,\beta)
, there exists a strongly
(k+1,3L\beta,\delta,1)
-independent points in
S
at
z_{1}
, where
L=L(n,Y)
is the constant from Lemma
4.18
. Otherwise, by Lemma
4.18
(applied with
r=1
and
\alpha=3L\beta
), the set
S
can be covered by at most
C(n,Y)\beta^{-k}
balls of radius
3L\beta
, contradicting (
4.27
) if we further cover each ball with at most
C(n,Y)
balls of radius
\beta
.

Given the existence of such independent points, Theorem
4.12
implies that there exists a
(k+1,\zeta,1)
-splitting map at
z_{1}
, where
\zeta=C(n,Y,\beta)\delta^{1/2}
. Combining this fact with Proposition
4.20
(i), we conclude that
(Z,d_{Z},z_{1},\mathfrak{t})
is
\epsilon
-close (see Definition
2.13
) to a
(k+1)
-splitting Ricci shrinker space
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
over
[-\epsilon^{-1},\epsilon^{-1}]
if
\delta\leq\delta(n,Y,\beta,\epsilon)
, which is a contradiction.

Case 2
: There is no
i\in[1,N]
such that
|\mathfrak{t}(z_{i})-\mathfrak{t}(z_{j})|\leq 3\beta^{2}
for any
j\neq i
.

By the pigeonhole principle, there exists a subset
I\subset\{1,\ldots,N\}
with cardinality
|I|\gg C(n,Y)\beta^{2-k}
such that for any
i,j\in I
, we have
|\mathfrak{t}(z_{i})-\mathfrak{t}(z_{j})|\leq\beta^{2}
.

Without loss of generality, assume
I=\{1,\ldots,N^{\prime}\}
. By our assumption,
N^{\prime}<N
, and there exists
j>N^{\prime}
such that

\displaystyle|\mathfrak{t}(z_{i})-\mathfrak{t}(z_{j})|\geq 2\beta^{2}

for any
1\leq i\leq N^{\prime}
. Then, by Lemma
4.19
, each
z_{i}
and
z_{j}
are
(C\delta^{1/2}\beta^{-4},1)
-static for
1\leq i\leq N^{\prime}
. Arguing as in Case 1, we can then find a
(k-1,\zeta,2)
-splitting map at
z_{1}
, where
\zeta=C(n,Y,\beta)\delta^{1/2}
.

Thus, we conclude that
(Z,d_{Z},z_{1},\mathfrak{t})
is
\epsilon
-close to a
(k-1)
-splitting static or quasi-static cone
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
, if
\delta\leq\delta(n,Y,\beta,\epsilon)
. If
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
is a static cone, one can derive the contradiction as in Case 1. Therefore, we may assume that
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
is a quasi-static cone.

If
\mathfrak{t}(z_{1})<\mathfrak{t}(z_{j})
, since
d_{Z}(z_{1},z_{j})\leq 2
and
\mathfrak{t}(z_{1})\leq\mathfrak{t}(z_{j})-2\beta^{2}
, we conclude that
z_{1}
is
(k+1,\epsilon,\epsilon^{1/2}\beta)
-symmetric by Definition
2.19
, provided that
\delta\leq\delta(n,Y,\epsilon,\beta)
. Similarly, if
\mathfrak{t}(z_{j})<\mathfrak{t}(z_{1})
, then
z_{j}
is
(k+1,\epsilon,\epsilon^{1/2}\beta)
-symmetric.

In either case, we obtain a contradiction to (
4.27
). Therefore, the original statement holds.
∎

By Lemma
4.21
, we prove the following covering lemma for the quantitative singular strata, whose proof is based on
[
CN13
]
; see also
[
Bam20b
, Proposition 11.2]
. Note that in
[
FL25a
, Theorem 8.14]
, the conclusion has already been proved for
k=n-2
.

Proposition 4.22
.

Given
k\in\{0,1,\ldots,n-2\}
,
z_{0}\in Z
,
\epsilon>0
and
r>0
with
\mathfrak{t}(z_{0})-2r^{2}\in\mathbb{I}^{-}
, for any
\sigma\in(0,\epsilon)
, there exist
x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{N}\in B^{*}(z_{0},r)
with
N\leq C(n,Y,\epsilon)\sigma^{-k-\epsilon}
and

\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,k}_{\sigma r,\epsilon r}\bigcap B^{*}(z_{0},r)\subset\bigcup_{j=1}^{N}B^{*}(x_{j},\sigma r).

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we assume
r=1
. In the following proof, we will choose
\beta=\beta(n,Y,\epsilon)
. Once
\beta
is fixed, set
\delta=\delta(n,Y,\epsilon,\beta)
so that Lemma
4.21
holds.

We first prove that there exists
x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{N}\in B^{*}(z_{0},1)
with
N\leq C(n,Y,\epsilon)\sigma^{-k-\epsilon}
such that

\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,k}_{\epsilon^{1/2}\sigma,\epsilon}\bigcap B^{*}(z_{0},1)\subset\bigcup_{j=1}^{N}B^{*}(x_{j},\sigma).

(4.28)

Once (
4.28
) is established, we immediately obtain the covering

\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,k}_{\sigma,\epsilon}\bigcap B^{*}(z_{0},1)\subset\bigcup_{j=1}^{N}B^{*}(x_{j},\epsilon^{-1/2}\sigma).

By applying Proposition
2.25
, each ball
B^{*}(x_{j},\epsilon^{-1/2}\sigma)
can be further covered by at most
C(n,Y,\epsilon)
balls of radius
\sigma
. This completes the proof.

To prove (
4.28
), it suffices to prove the conclusion for
\sigma=\beta^{m}\epsilon
, where
m\in\mathbb{N}
. First, note that for any
x\in B^{*}(z_{0},r)
, the number of indices
j\in\mathbb{N}
such that
x
is not
(\delta,\beta^{j}\epsilon)
-selfsimilar is bounded by

Q=Q(Y,\delta,\beta,\epsilon)=Q(n,Y,\beta,\epsilon).

For any finite sequence
a_{0},a_{1},\ldots,a_{m-1}\in\{0,1\}
, define

\displaystyle E(a_{0},a_{1},\ldots,a_{m-1}):=\{x\in B^{*}(z_{0},1)\mid x\ \mathrm{is}\ (\delta,\beta^{j}\epsilon)-\mathrm{selfsimilar\ if\ and\ only\ if}\ a_{j}=0\}.

If
a_{0}+a_{1}+\ldots+a_{m-1}>Q
, then
E(a_{0},a_{1},\ldots,a_{m-1})=\emptyset
. Thus, there are at most
m^{Q}
such non-empty sets which together cover
B^{*}(z_{0},1)
.

Claim 4.23
.

There exists a constant
C_{1}=C_{1}(n,Y)
such that the following holds.

For any
j\in\{0,1,\ldots,m-1\}
, any
x\in B^{*}(z_{0},1)
, and any
a_{0},a_{1},\ldots,a_{m-1}\in\{0,1\}
, one can find
\{y_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq N^{\prime}}\subset\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,k}_{\epsilon^{1/2}\sigma,\epsilon}\bigcap E(a_{0},a_{1},\ldots,a_{m-1})\bigcap B^{*}(x,\beta^{j}\epsilon)
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,k}_{\epsilon^{1/2}\sigma,\epsilon}\bigcap E(a_{0},a_{1},\ldots,a_{m-1})\bigcap B^{*}(x,\beta^{j}\epsilon)\subset\bigcup_{i=1}^{N^{\prime}}B^{*}(y_{i},\beta^{j+1}\epsilon),

where
N^{\prime}\leq C_{1}\beta^{-k}
if
a_{j}=0
and
N^{\prime}\leq C_{1}\beta^{-n-2}
if
a_{j}=1
.

In fact, if
a_{j}=0
, applying Lemma
4.21
with
r=\beta^{j}\epsilon
yields the desired covering. If
a_{j}=1
, the covering follows directly from Proposition
2.25
. This proves the claim.

Note that
B^{*}(z_{0},1)
can be covered by at most
C(n,Y,\epsilon)
balls of radius
\epsilon
. Thus, by applying Claim
4.23
inductively,
\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,k}_{\epsilon^{1/2}\sigma,\epsilon}\bigcap E(a_{0},a_{1},\ldots,a_{m-1})
can be covered by at most

\displaystyle C(n,Y,\epsilon)(C_{1}\beta^{-n-2})^{1+\sum a_{j}}(C_{1}\beta^{-k})^{m-1-\sum a_{j}}

balls of the form
B^{*}(y,\sigma)
where
y\in B^{*}(z_{0},1)
. Therefore,
\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,k}_{\epsilon^{1/2}\sigma,\epsilon}\bigcap B^{*}(z_{0},1)
can be covered by at most

\displaystyle N\leq C(n,Y,\epsilon)m^{Q}(C_{1}\beta^{-n-2})^{1+Q}(C_{1}\beta^{-k})^{m-1}\leq C(n,Y,\beta,\epsilon)m^{Q}C_{1}^{m}\beta^{-mk}

such balls.

Now, we choose
\beta=\beta(n,Y,\epsilon)
such that
\beta^{-\epsilon/2}\geq C_{1}
. Then

\displaystyle N\leq C(n,Y,\epsilon)m^{Q}\beta^{-mk-m\epsilon/2}\leq C(n,Y,\epsilon)\beta^{-mk-m\epsilon}\leq C(n,Y,\epsilon)\sigma^{-k-\epsilon},

which completes the proof.
∎

Based on Proposition
4.22
, we obtain the following result by the same argument as in
[
FL25a
, Corollaries 8.15, 8.16, 8.17]
.

Corollary 4.24
.

Given
z_{0}\in Z
,
\epsilon>0
and
r>0
with
\mathfrak{t}(z_{0})-2r^{2}\in\mathbb{I}^{-}
, the following statements are true for any
k\in\{0,1,\ldots,n-2\}
.

(a)

For any
\delta\in(0,\epsilon)
,

\displaystyle\left\lvert B^{*}_{\delta r}\left(\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,k}_{\delta r,\epsilon r}\right)\cap B^{*}(z_{0},r)\right\rvert\leq C(n,Y,\epsilon)\delta^{n+2-k-\epsilon}r^{n+2}.

(b)

For any
\delta\in(0,\epsilon)
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\left\lvert B^{*}_{\delta r}\left(\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,k}_{\delta r,\epsilon r}\right)\cap B^{*}(z_{0},r)\cap Z_{t}\right\rvert_{t}\leq C(n,Y,\epsilon)\delta^{n-k-\epsilon}r^{n}.

(c)

The Minkowski dimension satisfies

\displaystyle\dim_{\mathscr{M}}\mathcal{S}^{k}\leq k.

5
Rectifiability of cylindrical singularities

In this section, we prove the rectifiability of cylindrical singularities in a noncollapsed Ricci flow limit space. Throughout, we fix a noncollapsed Ricci flow limit space
(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})
, which is obtained as a pointed Gromov-Hausdorff limit of a sequence in
\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)
. As before, we set

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}:=[-0.98T,0]\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{I}^{-}:=(-0.98T,0].

Moreover, the regular part is given by a Ricci flow spacetime
(\mathcal{R},\mathfrak{t},\partial_{\mathfrak{t}},g^{Z})
. For simplicity, we use
B^{*}(x,r)
instead of
B_{Z}^{*}(x,r)
for the metric balls in
Z
with respect to
d_{Z}
.

5.1
Discrete Lojasiewicz inequality for Ricci flow limit spaces

In this section, we first prove the summability result of the
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}
-entropy.

Proposition 5.1
.

For any
0<\epsilon\leq\epsilon(n,Y)
,
\alpha\in(1/4,1)
, and
\delta\leq\delta(n,Y,\epsilon,\alpha)
, the following holds. Suppose

\displaystyle\left|\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{z}(s_{1})-\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{z}(s_{2})\right|<\delta,

for
0<s_{1}<s_{2}
, and for any
s\in[s_{1},s_{2}]
,
z
is
(k,\delta,\sqrt{s})
-cylindrical (see Definition
2.29
). Then

\displaystyle\sum_{s_{1}\leq r_{j}=2^{-j}\leq s_{2}}\left|\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{z}(r_{j})-\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{z}(r_{j-1})\right|^{\alpha}<\epsilon.

Proof.

As in the proof of
[
FL25b
, Corollary 6.28]
, it suffices to show that for any
j
with
s_{1}\leq r_{j}\leq s_{2}
and any
\theta\in(1/3,1)
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{z}(r_{j})-\Theta_{n-k}\right|^{1+\theta}\leq C(n,Y)\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{z}(r_{j+1})-\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{z}(r_{j-1})\right),

(5.1)

provided that
\delta\leq\delta(n,Y,\theta)
.

Assume that
\mathcal{X}^{l}=\{M_{l}^{n},(g_{l}(t))_{t\in\mathbb{I}_{l}}\}\in\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)
with
z_{l}^{*}\in\mathcal{X}^{l}
satisfy

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{X}^{l},d_{l}^{*},z_{l}^{*},\mathfrak{t}_{l}\right)\xrightarrow[l\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\hat{C}^{\infty}\hskip 8.19447pt}\left(Z,d_{Z},z,\mathfrak{t}\right).

Then, by our assumption,
z_{l}^{*}
is
(k,2\delta,\sqrt{r_{j}})
-cylindrical for any sufficiently large
l
. By
[
FL25b
, Lemma 2.27]
and
[
FL25b
, Theorem 6.26]
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{W}_{z_{l}^{*}}(s)-\Theta_{n-k}\right|^{1+\theta}\leq C(n,Y)\left(\mathcal{W}_{z_{l}^{*}}(s/2)-\mathcal{W}_{z_{l}^{*}}(2s)\right).

for any
s\in[r_{j}/2,2r_{j}]
. Letting
l\to\infty
and using Proposition
4.3
, we conclude that for any
\epsilon^{\prime}>0
, there exists
s\in(r_{j}-\epsilon^{\prime},r_{j}]
with
s,s/2,2s\notin J^{z}
such that

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{W}_{z}(s)-\Theta_{n-k}\right|^{1+\theta}\leq C(n,Y)\left(\mathcal{W}_{z}(s/2)-\mathcal{W}_{z}(2s)\right).

Taking
\epsilon^{\prime}\to 0
, we obtain from Definition
4.4
that

\displaystyle\left|\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{z}(r_{j})-\Theta_{n-k}\right|^{1+\theta}\leq C(n,Y)\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{z}(r_{j+1})-\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{z}(r_{j-1})\right).

In other words, (
5.1
) holds, which completes the proof.
∎

5.2
Basic properties of almost cylindrical points

We begin with the following lemma, which shows that the entire flow is rapidly clearing out after an almost cylindrical singularity.

Lemma 5.2

(Rapid clearing out)
.

Suppose
z\in Z
is
(k,\delta,r)
-cylindrical (see Definition
2.29
). For any
\epsilon>0
, if
\delta\leq\delta(n,Y,\epsilon)
, we have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{t}^{-1}\big([\epsilon r^{2},\infty)\big)\bigcap B^{*}(z,\epsilon^{-1}r)=\emptyset.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we assume
r=1
. Suppose that the conclusion fails. Then we can find a sequence of Ricci flow limit spaces
(Z^{l},d_{Z^{l}},\mathfrak{t}_{l})
and
(k,l^{-2},1)
-cylindrical points
z_{l}\in Z^{l}
such that

\displaystyle y_{l}\in\mathfrak{t}_{l}^{-1}\big([\epsilon,\infty)\big)\bigcap B^{*}(z_{l},\epsilon^{-1}).

It follows from Definition
2.29
that

\displaystyle(Z^{l},d_{Z^{l}},z_{l},\mathfrak{t}_{l})\xrightarrow[l\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\hat{C}^{\infty}\hskip 8.19447pt}(\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{k},d_{\mathcal{C}}^{*},p^{*},\mathfrak{t}).

Moreover,
y_{l}\xrightarrow[l\to\infty]{}y_{\infty}\in\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{k}_{[\epsilon,\infty)}\cap B^{*}(p^{*},2\epsilon^{-1})
. However, it is clear that
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{k}_{[\epsilon,\infty)}
is empty, which gives the contradiction. This finishes the proof.
∎

Next, we prove

Proposition 5.3
.

Suppose
z\in Z
is
(k,\delta,r)
-cylindrical with respect to
\mathcal{L}_{z,r}
. For any
\epsilon>0
, if
\delta\leq\delta(n,Y,\epsilon)
, then the following conclusions hold.

(i)

For any
\tau\in[\epsilon r^{2},\epsilon^{-1}r^{2}]
,

\displaystyle\left\lvert\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{z}(\tau)-\Theta_{n-k}\right\rvert<\epsilon.

(ii)

Any point in
B^{*}(z,\epsilon^{-1}r)\cap B^{*}_{\delta r}\left(\mathcal{L}_{z,r}\right)
is
(k,\epsilon,s)
-cylindrical for any
s\in[\epsilon r,\epsilon^{-1}r]
.

(iii)

If
x\in B^{*}(z,\epsilon^{-1}r)\cap Z_{[-r^{2},r^{2}]}
is
(k,\delta,s)
-cylindrical with respect to
\mathcal{L}_{x,s}
for some
s\in[\epsilon r,\epsilon^{-1}r]
, then

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{H}}\left(\mathcal{L}_{x,s}\cap B^{*}(x,\epsilon^{-1}s),\mathcal{L}_{z,r}\cap B^{*}(x,\epsilon^{-1}s)\right)<\epsilon r,

where
d_{\mathrm{H}}
denotes the Hausdorff distance with respect to
d_{Z}
.

Proof.

We only prove (i), as (ii) and (iii) can be proved similarly by a limiting argument.

Without loss of generality, we assume that
\mathfrak{t}(z)=0
and
r=1
. Suppose the conclusion (i) fails. Then we can find a sequence of Ricci flow limit spaces
(Z^{l},d_{Z^{l}},\mathfrak{t}_{l})
, which is obtained as the limit of a sequence in
\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T_{l})
, and
z_{l}\in Z^{l}
that is
(k,l^{-2},1)
-cylindrical, but there exists
s_{l}\in[\epsilon,\epsilon^{-1}]
such that

\displaystyle\left\lvert\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{z_{l}}(s_{l})-\Theta_{n-k}\right\rvert\geq\epsilon.

(5.2)

By passing to a diagonal subsequence, we may assume that each
(Z^{l},d_{Z^{l}},\mathfrak{t}_{l})
is given by a closed Ricci flow. By our assumption, we have

\displaystyle(Z^{l},d_{Z^{l}},z_{l},\mathfrak{t}_{l})\xrightarrow[l\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\hat{C}^{\infty}\hskip 8.19447pt}(\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{k},d_{\mathcal{C}}^{*},p^{*},\mathfrak{t}).

Then it follows from Proposition
4.3
that for almost all
\tau>0
,

\displaystyle\lim_{l\to\infty}\mathcal{W}_{z_{l}}(\tau)=\mathcal{W}_{p^{*}}(\tau)=\Theta_{n-k}.

Thus, it follows from Definition
4.4
and the monotonicity that
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{z_{l}}(s_{l})\to\Theta_{n-k}
, which contradicts (
5.2
). In sum, the proof is complete.
∎

Next, we show

Proposition 5.4
.

Suppose
z\in Z
is
(k,\delta,r)
-cylindrical with respect to
\mathcal{L}_{z,r}
and
\vec{u}=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{k})
is a
(k,\delta,r)
-splitting map at
z
. For any
\epsilon>0
, if
\delta\leq\delta(n,Y,\epsilon)
, then the set

\displaystyle V_{z,\epsilon^{-1}r}:=\vec{u}\left(\mathcal{L}_{z,r}\cap B^{*}(z,\epsilon^{-1}r)\right)\subset\mathbb{R}^{k}

satisfies

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{H}}\left(V_{z,\epsilon^{-1}r}\cap B(\vec{0}^{k},\epsilon_{0}\epsilon^{-1}r),B(\vec{0}^{k},\epsilon_{0}\epsilon^{-1}r)\right)<\epsilon r,

where
B(\vec{0}^{k},\epsilon_{0}\epsilon^{-1}r)
denotes the ball in
\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
d_{\mathrm{H}}
denotes the Hausdorff distance with respect to the Euclidean metric, and
\epsilon_{0}
is the spacetime distance constant.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we assume that
\mathfrak{t}(z)=0
and
r=1
. Suppose the conclusion fails. Then we can find a sequence of Ricci flow limit spaces
(Z^{l},d_{Z^{l}},\mathfrak{t}_{l})
, which is obtained as the limit of a sequence in
\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T_{l})
, and
z_{l}\in Z^{l}
such that
z_{l}
is
(k,l^{-2},1)
-cylindrical with respect to
\mathcal{L}_{z_{l},1}
. Moreover, there exist a
(k,l^{-2},1)
-splitting map
\vec{u}^{l}=(u^{l}_{1},\ldots,u^{l}_{k})
at
z_{l}
.

By our assumption,

\displaystyle(Z^{l},d_{Z^{l}},z_{l},\mathfrak{t}_{l})\xrightarrow[l\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\hat{C}^{\infty}\hskip 8.19447pt}(\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{k},d_{\mathcal{C}}^{*},p^{*},\mathfrak{t}).

Moreover,
\mathcal{L}_{z_{l},1}
converge to
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{k}_{0}
. Passing to a subsequence if necessary, it follows that the maps
\vec{u}^{l}
converge to the standard coordinate functions
\vec{y}=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{k})
on
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{k}_{[-10,0]}
. This convergence is evident on
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{k}_{[-10,0)}
, and the convergence at time slice
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{k}_{0}
follows from the reproduction formula and
[
FL25a
, Theorem 5.20]
.

Therefore, we conclude from Lemma
2.26
that

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{H}}\left(V_{z_{l},\epsilon^{-1}}\cap B(\vec{0}^{k},\epsilon_{0}\epsilon^{-1}),B(\vec{0}^{k},\epsilon_{0}\epsilon^{-1})\right)\leq\Psi(l^{-1}),

which gives a contradiction.

In sum, the proof is complete.
∎

Next, we prove the converse conclusion regarding
[
FL25b
, Lemma 2.27]
.

Lemma 5.5
.

Suppose
z\in Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
is
(\delta,r)
-selfsimilar and there exists a diffeomorphism
\varphi
from
\{\bar{b}:=2\sqrt{\bar{f}(-1)}\leq\delta^{-1}\}\subset\bar{M}
onto a subset of
\mathcal{R}_{-r^{2}}
such that on
\{\bar{b}\leq\delta^{-1}\}
,

\displaystyle\|r^{-2}\varphi^{*}g^{Z}_{-r^{2}}-\bar{g}(-1)\|_{C^{[\delta^{-1}]}}+\|\varphi^{*}f_{z}(-r^{2})-\bar{f}(-1)\|_{C^{[\delta^{-1}]}}\leq\delta,

where
(\bar{g}(-1),\bar{f}(-1))
comes from
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{k}
(see Subsection
2.3
). For any
\epsilon>0
, if
\delta\leq\delta(n,Y,\epsilon)
, then
z
is
(k,\epsilon,s)
-cylindrical for any
s\in[\epsilon r,\epsilon^{-1}r]
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we assume
\mathfrak{t}(z)=0
and
r=1
. Suppose the conclusion fails. Then we can find a sequence of Ricci flow limit spaces
(Z^{l},d_{Z^{l}},\mathfrak{t}_{l})
, which is obtained as the limit of a sequence in
\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T_{l})
and
z_{l}\in Z^{l}
such that
z_{l}
is
(l^{-1},1)
-selfsimilar, and there exists a diffeomorphism
\varphi_{l}
from
\{\bar{b}\leq l\}
onto a subset of the regular part
\mathcal{R}^{l}_{-1}
of
Z^{l}
such that

\displaystyle\|\varphi_{l}^{*}g^{Z^{l}}_{-1}-\bar{g}(-1)\|_{C^{l}}+\|\varphi_{l}^{*}f_{z_{l}}(-1)-\bar{f}(-1)\|_{C^{l}}\leq l^{-1}.

(5.3)

However,
z_{l}
is not
(k,\epsilon,s_{l})
-cylindrical for some
s_{l}\in[\epsilon,\epsilon^{-1}]
.

By our assumption, we may assume

\displaystyle(Z^{l},d_{Z^{l}},z_{l},\mathfrak{t}_{l})\xrightarrow[l\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\hat{C}^{\infty}\hskip 8.19447pt}(Z,d_{Z},z,\mathfrak{t}),

for which
(Z,d_{Z},z,\mathfrak{t})
is a noncollapsed Ricci flow limit space over
(-\infty,0]
. Moreover, as in the proof of Lemma
3.3
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ric}(g^{Z})+\nabla^{2}f_{z}=\frac{g^{Z}}{2|\mathfrak{t}|}

holds on
\mathcal{R}_{(-\infty,0)}
, and
\mathcal{R}_{t}
is connected for
t<0
. By (
5.3
) and the self-similarity, we conclude that

\displaystyle Z_{(-\infty,0)}=\mathcal{R}_{(-\infty,0)}=\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{k}_{(-\infty,0)}.

Thus, it follows from
[
FL25a
, Theorem 7.25]
that
Z_{(0,\infty)}
is empty. In particular, we have
Z=\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{k}
. However, this implies that
z_{l}
is
(k,\epsilon,s_{l})
-cylindrical for sufficiently large
l
, contradicting our assumption.

In sum, the proof is complete.
∎

Next, we prove

Proposition 5.6
.

Let
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in\mathbb{I}^{++}}\}\in\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)
. Suppose
z^{*}\in M\times\mathbb{I}
is
(k,\delta,r)
-cylindrical and
\mathcal{W}_{z^{*}}(\delta\bar{r}^{2})-\mathcal{W}_{z^{*}}(\delta^{-1}r^{2})<\delta
for some
0<\bar{r}<r
. For any
\epsilon>0
, if
\delta\leq\delta(n,Y,\epsilon)
, then
z^{*}
is
(k,\epsilon,s)
-cylindrical for any
s\in[\bar{r},r]
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we assume
r=1
and
\mathfrak{t}(z^{*})=0
.

For each
s\in[\bar{r},1]
, define
\mathbf{r}_{A}(s)
as
\mathbf{r}_{A}
-radius (see
[
FL25b
, Definition 5.1 (A)]
) for the weighted Riemannian manifold
(M,s^{-2}g(-s^{2}),f_{z^{*}}(-s^{2}))
.

By Lemma
5.5
, there exists a constant
L_{1}=L_{1}(n,Y,\epsilon)>1
such that if
\mathbf{r}_{A}(s)\geq L_{1}
, then
z^{*}
is
(k,\epsilon,s)
-cylindrical.

Furthermore, by
[
FL25b
, Theorem 6.24]
, there exists
L_{2}=L_{2}(n,Y)>1
such that if
\mathbf{r}_{A}(s)\geq L_{2}
, then

\displaystyle\mathbf{r}_{A}(s)\geq\sqrt{-\log\left(\mathcal{W}_{z^{*}}(s^{2}/2)-\mathcal{W}_{z^{*}}(2s^{2})\right)}.

(5.4)

Now set
L=\max\{L_{1},L_{2}\}
. Since
z^{*}
is
(k,\delta,1)
-cylindrical,
[
FL25b
, Lemma 2.27]
implies that
\mathbf{r}_{A}(1)\geq 2L
, provided that
\delta
is sufficiently small.

Define
r^{\prime}
as the infimum in
[\bar{r},1]
such that
\mathbf{r}_{A}(s)\geq 2L
for any
s\in[r^{\prime},1]
. If
r^{\prime}>\bar{r}
, then by continuity we must have
\mathbf{r}_{A}(r^{\prime})=2L
. However, this contradicts (
5.4
) if
\delta\leq\delta(n,Y,\epsilon)
.

Thus,
\mathbf{r}_{A}(s)\geq 2L
for any
s\in[\bar{r},1]
, and hence
z^{*}
is
(k,\epsilon,s)
-cylindrical for all
s\in[\bar{r},1]
.
∎

Next, we have the following definition.

Definition 5.7
.

We say
z\in Z
is
uniformly
(k,\epsilon,r)
-cylindrical
if
z
is
(k,\epsilon,s)
-cylindrical for any
s\in(0,r]
.

By taking the limit and using Propositions
4.3
and
5.6
, we obtain the following result.

Proposition 5.8
.

Suppose
z\in Z
is
(k,\delta,r)
-cylindrical and
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{z}(\delta\bar{r}^{2})-\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{z}(\delta^{-1}r^{2})<\delta
for some
0<\bar{r}<r
. For any
\epsilon>0
, if
\delta\leq\delta(n,Y,\epsilon)
, then
z
is
(k,\epsilon,s)
-cylindrical for any
s\in[\bar{r},r]
. In particular, if
z\in\mathcal{S}^{k}_{\mathrm{c}}\setminus\mathcal{S}^{k-1}_{\mathrm{c}}
is
(k,\delta,r)
-cylindrical with
\delta\leq\delta(n,Y,\epsilon)
, then
z
is uniformly
(k,\epsilon,r)
-cylindrical.

5.3
Cylindrical neck regions

We introduce the following notion of a cylindrical neck region, adapted from
[
FL25c
, Definition 4.1]
with an additional parameter
\mathfrak{c}
that encodes finer quantitative information. Related neck-region notions for noncollapsed Ricci limit spaces appear in
[
JN21
, Definition 3.1]
and
[
CJN21
, Definition 2.4]
.

Definition 5.9

(Cylindrical neck region)
.

Given constants
\delta>0
,
\mathfrak{c}\in(0,10^{-10n})
,
r>0
and
z\in Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
with
\mathfrak{t}(z)-2\delta^{-1}r^{2}\in\mathbb{I}^{-}
, we call a subset
\mathscr{N}\subset B^{*}(z,2r)
a
(k,\delta,\mathfrak{c},r)
-cylindrical neck region
if
\mathscr{N}=B^{*}(z,2r)\setminus B_{r_{x}}^{*}(\mathscr{C})
, where
\mathscr{C}\subset B^{*}(z,2r)
is a nonempty closed subset with
r_{x}:\mathscr{C}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}:=[0,+\infty)
, satisfies:

(n1)
for any
x,y\in\mathscr{C}
,
d_{Z}(x,y)\geq\mathfrak{c}^{2}(r_{x}+r_{y});

(n2)
for all
x\in\mathscr{C}
,

\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{x}(\delta r^{2}_{x})-\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{x}(\delta^{-1}r^{2})<\delta;

(n3)
for each
x\in\mathscr{C}
and
\mathfrak{c}^{2}r_{x}\leq s\leq 2r
,
x
is
(k,\delta,s)
-cylindrical with respect to
\mathcal{L}_{x,s};

(n4)
for each
x\in\mathscr{C}
and
\mathfrak{c}^{-5}r_{x}\leq s\leq r-d_{Z}(x,z)/2
, we have
\mathcal{L}_{x,s}\cap B^{*}(x,s)\subset B^{*}_{\mathfrak{c}s}(\mathscr{C})
and
\mathscr{C}\bigcap B^{*}(x,s)\subset B^{*}_{\mathfrak{c}s}(\mathcal{L}_{x,s})
.

Here,
\mathscr{C}
is called the
center
of the neck region, and
r_{x}
is referred to as the
radius function
. We decompose
\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{C}_{0}\bigcup\mathscr{C}_{+}
, where
r_{x}>0
on
\mathscr{C}_{+}
and
r_{x}=0
on
\mathscr{C}_{0}
. In addition, we use the notation

\displaystyle B^{*}_{r_{x}}(\mathscr{C}):=\mathscr{C}_{0}\bigcup\bigcup_{x\in\mathscr{C}_{+}}B^{*}(x,r_{x}).

It is clear from
(n1)
above that
r_{x}:\mathscr{C}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
is a Lipschitz function with Lipschitz constant
\mathfrak{c}^{-2}
. Moreover, it follows from
(n2)
and Definition
4.6
that any
x\in\mathscr{C}
is
(\delta,s)
-selfsimilar for any
s\in[r_{x},r]
. In addition, it is easy to check that the restriction of a cylindrical neck region to any small ball centered at points in
\mathscr{C}
is still a cylindrical neck region.

The following result is immediate from Proposition
5.8
.

Lemma 5.10
.

Given
\zeta>0
and
z\in\mathcal{S}^{k}_{\mathrm{c}}\setminus\mathcal{S}^{k-1}_{\mathrm{c}}
, there exists a constant
r_{z}>0
such that any point in
\left(\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{c}}^{k}\setminus\mathcal{S}^{k-1}_{\mathrm{c}}\right)\bigcap B^{*}(z,r_{z})
is uniformly
(k,\zeta,r_{z})
-cylindrical.

Proof.

By our assumption and Proposition
5.8
, for any
\epsilon>0
, there exists a constant
r>0
such that
z
is uniformly
(k,\epsilon,r)
-cylindrical. By a limiting argument, given
\epsilon^{\prime}>0
, if
\epsilon\leq\epsilon(n,Y,\epsilon^{\prime})
, then there exists a small constant
r^{\prime}\in(0,r/2)
such that any
w\in B^{*}(z,r^{\prime})
is also
(k,\epsilon^{\prime},r/2)
-cylindrical. Then, it follows from Proposition
5.8
again that any
w\in\left(\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{c}}^{k}\setminus\mathcal{S}^{k-1}_{\mathrm{c}}\right)\bigcap B^{*}(z,r^{\prime})
is uniformly
(k,\zeta,r^{\prime})
-cylindrical, provided that
\epsilon^{\prime}\leq\epsilon^{\prime}(n,Y,\zeta)
. After choosing a small
\epsilon
, the proof is complete.
∎

Proposition 5.11
.

Given
\delta>0
and
\mathfrak{c}\in(0,10^{-10n})
, for any
x_{0}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{c}}^{k}\setminus\mathcal{S}^{k-1}_{\mathrm{c}}
, there exist a constant
r_{0}>0
and
\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{C}_{0}\bigcup\mathscr{C}_{+}\subset B^{*}(x_{0},2r_{0})
with
r_{x}:\mathscr{C}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
satisfying
r_{x}>0
on
\mathscr{C}_{+}
and
r_{x}=0
on
\mathscr{C}_{0}
, such that the following hold:

(i)

\mathscr{N}=B^{*}(x_{0},2r_{0})\setminus B^{*}_{r_{x}}(\mathscr{C})
is a
(k,\delta,\mathfrak{c},r_{0})
-cylindrical neck region.

(ii)

\left(\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{c}}^{k}\setminus\mathcal{S}^{k-1}_{\mathrm{c}}\right)\bigcap B^{*}(x_{0},r_{0})\subset\mathscr{C}_{0}
.

Proof.

In the proof, a ball
B^{*}(y,r)
is called a
good ball
if there exists
z\in B^{*}(y,r)
such that
z
is
(k,\zeta,r)
-cylindrical, where
\zeta
is a fixed constant to be determined. Otherwise, we call
B^{*}(y,r)
a
bad ball
. Without loss of generality, we assume
\delta\ll\mathfrak{c}
. Moreover, throughout the proof, the constants
\zeta
and
\epsilon
will be chosen so that

\displaystyle\zeta\ll\epsilon\ll\delta.

First, we determine
r_{0}
. Since
x_{0}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{c}}^{k}\setminus\mathcal{S}^{k-1}_{\mathrm{c}}
, it follows from Lemma
5.10
that we can find
r_{0}>0
so that any point in
\left(\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{c}}^{k}\setminus\mathcal{S}^{k-1}_{\mathrm{c}}\right)\bigcap B^{*}(x_{0},2r_{0})
is uniformly
(k,\zeta,2r_{0})
-cylindrical with
\mathfrak{t}(x_{0})-5\zeta^{-1}r_{0}^{2}\in\mathbb{I}^{-}
. In particular,
B^{*}(x_{0},r_{0})
is a good ball.

We assume that
x_{0}
itself is
(k,\zeta,r_{0})
-cylindrical with respect to
\mathcal{L}_{x_{0},r_{0}}
. Then it follows from Proposition
5.3
(iii) that any
(k,\zeta,r_{0})
-cylindrical point
z\in B^{*}(x_{0},2r_{0})
satisfies

\displaystyle z\in B^{*}(x_{0},2r_{0})\bigcap B^{*}_{\epsilon r_{0}}(\mathcal{L}_{x_{0},r_{0}}).

In particular, we have

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{c}}^{k}\setminus\mathcal{S}^{k-1}_{\mathrm{c}}\right)\bigcap B^{*}(x_{0},2r_{0})\subset B^{*}_{\epsilon r_{0}}\left(\mathcal{L}_{x_{0},r_{0}}\right)\bigcap B^{*}(x_{0},2r_{0}).

(5.5)

By Proposition
5.3
(i) (ii), we can choose a small
\epsilon
so that any point
z\in B^{*}_{\epsilon r_{0}}\left(\mathcal{L}_{x_{0},r_{0}}\right)\bigcap B^{*}(x_{0},2r_{0})
is
(k,\delta^{2},s)
-cylindrical for any
s\in[\delta r_{0},\delta^{-1}r_{0}]
and satisfies

\displaystyle\left|\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{z}(s^{2})-\Theta_{n-k}\right|\leq\delta^{2}.

(5.6)

Set
\gamma=\mathfrak{c}^{5}
and
L^{1}=\mathcal{L}_{x_{0},r_{0}}\bigcap B^{*}(x_{0},2r_{0})
. Then we choose a maximal
2\mathfrak{c}^{2}\gamma r_{0}
-separated set
\{y_{\alpha}\}\subset L^{1}
. In particular,
\left\{B^{*}(y_{\alpha},\mathfrak{c}^{2}\gamma r_{0})\right\}
are pairwise disjoint and

\displaystyle L^{1}\subset\bigcup_{\alpha}B^{*}(y_{\alpha},2\mathfrak{c}^{2}\gamma r_{0}).

We can re-index those balls
B^{*}(y_{\alpha},\gamma r_{0})
by good balls with index
g^{1}_{i}
and bad balls with index
b^{1}_{i}
and hence rewrite

\displaystyle B^{*}_{\epsilon r_{0}}\left(\mathcal{L}_{x_{0},r_{0}}\right)\bigcap B^{*}(x_{0},2r_{0})\subset\bigcup_{g^{1}_{i}}B^{*}(y_{g^{1}_{i}},\gamma r_{0})\bigcup\bigcup_{b^{1}_{i}}B^{*}(y_{b^{1}_{i}},\gamma r_{0}).

(5.7)

Then we set

\displaystyle\mathscr{N}^{1}:=B^{*}(x_{0},2r_{0})\setminus\left(\bigcup_{g^{1}_{i}}B^{*}(y_{g^{1}_{i}},\gamma r_{0})\bigcup\bigcup_{b^{1}_{i}}B^{*}(y_{b^{1}_{i}},\gamma r_{0})\right).

From our construction and (
5.6
), we know that
\mathscr{N}^{1}
is a
(k,\delta,\mathfrak{c},r_{0})
-cylindrical neck region with center
\bigcup_{i}\{y_{g^{1}_{i}},y_{b^{1}_{i}}\}
and radius function
r_{x}\equiv\gamma r_{0}
for
x\in\bigcup_{i}\{y_{g^{1}_{i}},y_{b^{1}_{i}}\}
. Indeed, condition
(n1)
follows from our construction.
(n2)
follows from (
5.6
). Moreover,
(n3)
and
(n4)
follow from our construction and Proposition
5.3
.

Moreover, by the definition of bad balls, there exists no
(k,\zeta,\gamma r_{0})
-cylindrical point in
\bigcup_{b_{i}^{1}}B^{*}(y_{b_{i}^{1}},\gamma r_{0})
. Thus, it follows from (
5.5
) and (
5.7
) that

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{c}}^{k}\setminus\mathcal{S}^{k-1}_{\mathrm{c}}\right)\bigcap B^{*}(x_{0},r_{0})\subset\bigcup_{g^{1}_{i}}B^{*}(y_{g_{i}^{1}},\gamma r_{0}).

Next, we use induction to construct a sequence of
(k,\delta,\mathfrak{c},r_{0})
-cylindrical neck regions
\mathscr{N}^{l}
iteratively.

Suppose that for some
l\geq 1
, we have constructed a
(k,\delta,\mathfrak{c},r_{0})
-cylindrical neck region

\displaystyle\mathscr{N}^{l}:=B^{*}(x_{0},2r_{0})\setminus\left(\bigcup_{g^{l}_{i}}B^{*}(y_{g_{i}^{l}},\gamma^{l}r_{0})\bigcup\bigcup_{1\leq j\leq l}\bigcup_{b_{i}^{j}}B^{*}(y_{b_{i}^{j}},\gamma^{j}r_{0})\right)

(5.8)

such that

•

each
B^{*}(y_{g_{i}^{l}},\gamma^{l}r_{0})
is a good ball, while each
B^{*}(y_{b_{i}^{j}},\gamma^{j}r_{0})
is a bad ball.

•

each
y_{g_{i}^{l}}
is
(k,\delta^{2},s)
-cylindrical for any
s\in[\delta\gamma^{l}r_{0},\delta^{-1}r_{0}]
, and satisfies

\displaystyle\left|\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{y_{g_{i}^{l}}}(s^{2})-\Theta_{n-k}\right|\leq\delta^{2};

•

each
y_{b_{i}^{j}}
(for
1\leq j\leq l
) is
(k,\delta^{2},s)
-cylindrical for any
s\in[\delta\gamma^{j}r_{0},\delta^{-1}r_{0}]
, and satisfies

\displaystyle\left|\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{y_{b_{i}^{j}}}(s^{2})-\Theta_{n-k}\right|\leq\delta^{2};

•

we have

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{c}}^{k}\setminus\mathcal{S}^{k-1}_{\mathrm{c}}\right)\bigcap B^{*}(x_{0},r_{0})\subset\bigcup_{g^{l}_{i}}B^{*}(y_{g_{i}^{l}},\gamma^{l}r_{0}).

(5.9)

For each good ball
B^{*}(y_{g_{i}^{l}},\gamma^{l}r_{0})
, we can find a
(k,\zeta,\gamma^{l}r_{0})
-cylindrical point
y_{g_{i}^{l}}^{\prime}\in B^{*}(y_{g_{i}^{l}},\gamma^{l}r_{0})
with respect to
\mathcal{L}_{y_{g_{i}^{l}}^{\prime},\gamma^{l}r_{0}}
. Then we have

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{c}}^{k}\setminus\mathcal{S}^{k-1}_{\mathrm{c}}\right)\bigcap B^{*}(y_{g_{i}^{l}},\gamma^{l}r_{0})\subset B^{*}_{\epsilon\gamma^{l}r_{0}}\left(\mathcal{L}_{y_{g_{i}^{l}}^{\prime},\gamma^{l}r_{0}}\right).

By the same argument as before, we conclude that any point
z\in B^{*}_{\epsilon\gamma^{l}r_{0}}\left(\mathcal{L}_{y_{g_{i}^{l}}^{\prime},\gamma^{l}r_{0}}\right)\bigcap B^{*}(y_{g_{i}^{l}}^{\prime},2\gamma^{l}r_{0})
is
(k,\delta^{2},s)
-cylindrical for any
s\in[\delta\gamma^{l}r_{0},\delta^{-1}\gamma^{l}r_{0}]
and satisfies

\displaystyle\left|\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{z}(s^{2})-\Theta_{n-k}\right|\leq\delta^{2}.

(5.10)

We set

\displaystyle L^{l+1}:=\bigcup_{g_{i}^{l}}\left(\mathcal{L}_{y_{g_{i}^{l}}^{\prime},\gamma^{l}r_{0}}\bigcap B^{*}(y_{g_{i}^{l}}^{\prime},2\gamma^{l}r_{0})\right)\bigcap B^{*}(x_{0},2r_{0})\setminus\left(\bigcup_{b_{i}^{l}}B^{*}(y_{b_{i}^{l}},2\mathfrak{c}^{2}\gamma^{l}r_{0})\right).

Then we choose a maximal
2\mathfrak{c}^{2}\gamma^{l+1}r_{0}
-separated set
\{x_{\alpha}\}\subset L^{l+1}
. In particular,

\displaystyle L^{l+1}\subset\bigcup_{\alpha}B^{*}(x_{\alpha},2\mathfrak{c}^{2}\gamma^{l+1}r_{0}).

From our construction,
\left\{B^{*}(x_{\alpha},\mathfrak{c}^{2}\gamma^{l+1}r_{0}),B^{*}(y_{b_{i}^{j}},\mathfrak{c}^{2}\gamma^{j}r_{0})\right\}_{\alpha,i,1\leq j\leq l}
are pairwise disjoint.

After re-indexing by good balls and bad balls, we define

\displaystyle\mathscr{N}^{l+1}:=B^{*}(x_{0},2r_{0})\setminus\left(\bigcup_{g^{l+1}_{i}}B^{*}(y_{g_{i}^{l+1}},\gamma^{l+1}r_{0})\bigcup\bigcup_{1\leq j\leq l+1}\bigcup_{b_{i}^{j}}B^{*}(y_{b_{i}^{j}},\gamma^{j}r_{0})\right).

For
z=y_{g_{i}^{l+1}}
or
y_{b_{i}^{l+1}}
, we claim that
z
is
(k,\delta^{2},s)
-cylindrical for any
s\in[\delta\gamma^{l}r_{0},\delta^{-1}r_{0}]
and satisfies

\displaystyle\left|\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{z}(s^{2})-\Theta_{n-k}\right|\leq\delta^{2}.

Indeed, if
s\in[\delta\gamma^{l}r_{0},\delta^{-1}\gamma^{l}r_{0}]
, this follows immediately from our construction and (
5.10
). In general, if
s\in[\delta\gamma^{j}r_{0},\delta^{-1}\gamma^{j}r_{0}]
for
1\leq j\leq l-1
, it follows from our construction that there exists a good ball
B^{*}(y_{g_{i}^{j}},\gamma^{j}r_{0})
so that
d_{Z}(z,y_{g_{i}^{j}})\leq 2\gamma^{j}r_{0}
and

\displaystyle d_{Z}(z,\mathcal{L}_{y^{\prime}_{g_{i}^{j}},\gamma^{j}r_{0}})\leq\sum_{m=j}^{l}\epsilon\gamma^{m}r_{0}\leq 2\epsilon\gamma^{j}r_{0},

where
y^{\prime}_{g_{i}^{j}}
is a
(k,\zeta,\gamma^{j}r_{0})
-cylindrical point in
B^{*}(y_{g_{i}^{j}},\gamma^{j}r_{0})
. Thus,
z
is
(k,\delta^{2},s)
-cylindrical with
\left|\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{z}(s^{2})-\Theta_{n-k}\right|\leq\delta^{2}
by Proposition
5.3
(i)(ii).

As before,
\mathscr{N}^{l+1}
is a
(k,\delta,\mathfrak{c},r_{0})
-cylindrical neck region with a center
\mathscr{C}^{l+1}
consisting of the centers of the above balls and the radius function chosen to be the corresponding radii of these balls.

Here,
(n1)
follows by construction, while
(n2)
and
(n3)
were established earlier. The second assertion of
(n4)
follows from our construction together with Proposition
5.3
(iii). Thus, the only remaining point is to verify the first assertion of
(n4)
. Specifically, we need to show that for
x=g_{i}^{l+1}
or
b_{i}^{l+1}
and

\displaystyle s\in[\mathfrak{c}^{-5}\gamma^{l+1}r_{0},r_{0}-d_{Z}(x,x_{0})/2]=[\gamma^{l}r_{0},r_{0}-d_{Z}(x,x_{0})/2],

the inclusion

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{x,s}\cap B^{*}(x,s)\subset B^{*}_{\mathfrak{c}s}(\mathscr{C}^{l+1})

holds. Without loss of generality, we may assume
s\in[\gamma^{l}r_{0},\gamma^{l-1}r_{0}]
. For the general case
s\in[\gamma^{j}r_{0},\gamma^{j-1}r_{0}]
for
1\leq j\leq l-1
, the same reasoning applies after selecting the comparison point from
\{y_{g_{i}^{l+1}},y_{b_{i}^{m}}\}_{j-1\leq m\leq l+1}
.

For any
w\in\mathcal{L}_{x,s}\cap B^{*}(x,s)
, one of the following alternatives occurs:

•

There exists another point
x_{i^{\prime}}=y_{g_{i^{\prime}}^{l+1}}
or
y_{b_{i^{\prime}}^{l+1}}
such that

\displaystyle d_{Z}(w,x_{i^{\prime}})\leq 4\mathfrak{c}^{2}\gamma^{l+1}r_{0}<\mathfrak{c}s;

•

or there exists a point
y_{b_{i^{\prime}}^{l}}
such that

\displaystyle d_{Z}(w,y_{b_{i^{\prime}}^{l}})\leq 2\mathfrak{c}^{2}\gamma^{l}r_{0}<\mathfrak{c}s.

Thus, in both cases, the first statement in
(n4)
is verified.

Now, it is clear from (
5.9
) that

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{c}}^{k}\setminus\mathcal{S}^{k-1}_{\mathrm{c}}\right)\bigcap B^{*}(x_{0},r_{0})\subset\bigcup_{g^{l+1}_{i}}B^{*}(y_{g_{i}^{l+1}},\gamma^{l+1}r_{0}).

(5.11)

We set
\mathcal{G}^{l}:=\bigcup_{g^{l}_{i}}\{y_{g_{i}^{l}}\}
. Then it is straightforward from our construction that
\mathcal{G}^{l+1}\subset B^{*}_{2\gamma^{l}r_{0}}(\mathcal{G}^{l})
. Then we define

\displaystyle\mathscr{C}_{0}:=\lim_{l\to\infty}\mathcal{G}^{l},

(5.12)

where the limit is the Hausdorff limit with respect to
d_{Z}
.

Letting
l\to\infty
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathscr{N}:=B^{*}(x_{0},2r_{0})\setminus\left(\mathscr{C}_{0}\bigcup\bigcup_{1\leq j<\infty}\bigcup_{b_{i}^{j}}B^{*}(y_{b_{i}^{j}},\gamma^{j}r_{0})\right),

is a
(k,\delta,\mathfrak{c},r_{0})
-cylindrical neck region with center
\mathscr{C}:=\mathscr{C}_{0}\bigcup\mathscr{C}_{+}
, where
\mathscr{C}_{+}=\bigcup_{b_{i}^{j}}\{y_{b^{j}_{i}}\}
, and radius function chosen to be the corresponding radii of these balls. By (
5.11
), we have

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{c}}^{k}\setminus\mathcal{S}^{k-1}_{\mathrm{c}}\right)\bigcap B^{*}(x_{0},r_{0})\subset\mathscr{C}_{0}.

This finishes the proof.
∎

Next, we define the packing measure associated with a cylindrical neck region as follows:

Definition 5.12

(Packing measure)
.

For a
(k,\delta,\mathfrak{c},r)
-cylindrical neck region
\mathscr{N}=B^{*}(z,2r)\setminus B_{r_{x}}^{*}(\mathscr{C})
, we define the
packing measure

\mu
as

\mu:=\sum_{x\in\mathscr{C}^{+}}r_{x}^{k}\delta_{x}+\mathscr{H}^{k}|_{\mathscr{C}_{0}},

where
\mathscr{H}^{k}
is the
k
-dimensional Hausdorff measure with respect to
d_{Z}
.

For the remainder of this subsection, we fix a
(k,\delta,\mathfrak{c},r)
-cylindrical neck region
\mathscr{N}=B^{*}(z,2r)\setminus B_{r_{x}}^{*}(\mathscr{C})
. First, we prove

Lemma 5.13
.

Let
x,y\in\mathscr{C}
with
s=d_{Z}(x,y)
. For any
\epsilon>0
, if
\delta\leq\delta(n,Y,\epsilon)
, then

|\mathfrak{t}(x)-\mathfrak{t}(y)|\leq\epsilon s^{2}.

Proof.

By
(n1)
in Definition
5.9
, we have

s=d_{Z}(x,y)\geq\mathfrak{c}^{2}(r_{x}+r_{y})\geq\mathfrak{c}^{2}\max\{r_{x},r_{y}\}.

By
(n3)
in Definition
5.9
, both
x
and
y
are
(k,\delta,s)
-cylindrical. If
\delta\leq\delta(n,Y,\epsilon)
, then by the rapid clearing out Lemma
5.2
,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{t}^{-1}\big([\mathfrak{t}(x)+\epsilon s^{2},\infty)\big)\bigcap B^{*}(x,\epsilon^{-1}s)=\emptyset.

Since
y\in B^{*}(x,\epsilon^{-1}s)\cap Z_{[\mathfrak{t}(x)-s^{2},\mathfrak{t}(x)+s^{2}]}
, we obtain
\mathfrak{t}(y)\leq\mathfrak{t}(x)+\epsilon s^{2}
. Similarly, we have
\mathfrak{t}(x)\leq\mathfrak{t}(y)+\epsilon s^{2}
. This finishes the proof.
∎

Since
\mathscr{C}
is nonempty and every point in
\mathscr{C}
is both
(k,\delta,r)
-cylindrical and
(\delta,r)
-selfsimilar, it follows from Proposition
3.11
(ii) and
[
FL25a
, Proposition 10.7]
that the following holds.

Lemma 5.14
.

For any
\epsilon>0
, if
\delta\leq\delta(n,Y,\epsilon)
, then there exists a limiting heat flow
\vec{u}=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{k})
, defined on
Z_{[\mathfrak{t}(z)-100\epsilon^{-2}r^{2},0]}
, such that for any
x\in\mathscr{C}
and
s\in[\epsilon r,\epsilon^{-1}r]
, we can find a matrix
T^{\prime}_{x,s}
with
\|T^{\prime}_{x,s}-\mathrm{Id}\|\leq\epsilon
such that
\vec{u}^{\prime}_{x,s}=T^{\prime}_{x,s}\left(\vec{u}-\vec{u}(x)\right)
is a
(k,\epsilon,s)
-splitting map at
x
.

For the rest of this subsection, we use
\vec{u}
to denote the map constructed in Lemma
5.14
. Next, we prove the Lipschitz property of
\vec{u}
.

Lemma 5.15
.

There exists a constant
C_{\mathrm{Lip}}=C_{\mathrm{Lip}}(n)>0
such that if
\delta\leq\delta(n,Y)
, then

\displaystyle|\vec{u}(x)-\vec{u}(y)|\leq C_{\mathrm{Lip}}d_{Z}(x,y),\quad\forall x,y\in\mathscr{C}.

Proof.

Passing to the limit in
[
FL25a
, Proposition 10.4]
, we may choose
s=s(n)
sufficiently large so that

\displaystyle|\vec{u}^{\prime}_{x,s}(y)|\leq C(n)d_{Z}(x,y),\quad\forall x,y\in\mathscr{C}.

By the definition of
\vec{u}_{x,s}
and Lemma
5.14
, it follows that

\displaystyle|\vec{u}(x)-\vec{u}(y)|\leq C(n)d_{Z}(x,y),

which completes the proof.
∎

We consider the following extra assumption:

(*_{D})

For any
x\in\mathscr{C}
and
r_{x}\leq s\leq r-d_{Z}(x,z)/2
, we have

\displaystyle D^{-1}s^{k}\leq\mu(B^{*}(x,s))\leq Ds^{k}.

Proposition 5.16
.

For any positive constants
D
and
\epsilon
, if
(*_{D})
holds and
\delta\leq\delta(n,Y,\mathfrak{c},D,\epsilon)
, then we can find
\mathscr{C}_{\epsilon}\subset\mathscr{C}\cap B^{*}(z,15r/8)
such that the following hold.

(i)

\mu\left(\mathscr{C}_{\epsilon}\right)\geq(1-\epsilon)\mu\left(\mathscr{C}\cap B^{*}(z,15r/8)\right)
.

(ii)

For any
x\in\mathscr{C}_{\epsilon}
, we have

\sum_{r_{x}\leq r_{i}=2^{-i}\leq 2^{-5}r}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.08334pt\hskip-2.08334pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\displaystyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-2.08334pt\hskip-2.77774pt\raisebox{-1.65277pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\displaystyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.77774pt\hskip 2.08334pt}\kern-7.49994pt}{\hbox{\hskip 2.08334pt\hskip-2.08334pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\textstyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-2.08334pt\hskip-2.77774pt\raisebox{-1.65277pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\textstyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.77774pt\hskip 2.08334pt}\kern-7.49994pt}{\hbox{\hskip 1.72743pt\hskip-1.72743pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\scriptstyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-1.72743pt\hskip-2.45831pt\raisebox{-1.15695pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.13998pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.13998pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.45831pt\hskip 1.72743pt}\kern-6.78813pt}{\hbox{\hskip 1.57988pt\hskip-1.57988pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-1.57988pt\hskip-2.29166pt\raisebox{-0.82639pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.09999pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.09999pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.29166pt\hskip 1.57988pt}\kern-6.49301pt}\int_{B^{*}(x,r_{i})}\left|\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{y}(r_{i}^{2}/40)-\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{y}(40r_{i}^{2})\right|^{1/2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)\leq\epsilon.

(iii)

For any
x\in\mathscr{C}_{\epsilon}
and
s\in[r_{x},r-d_{Z}(x,z)/2]
, there exists a matrix
T_{x,s}

with
\|T_{x,s}-\mathrm{Id}\|\leq\epsilon
such that
\vec{u}_{x,s}=T_{x,s}\left(\vec{u}-\vec{u}(x)\right)
is a
(k,\epsilon,s)
-splitting map at
x
. Here,
\vec{u}
is the map obtained in Lemma
5.14
.

(iv)

\vec{u}
is bi-Lipschitz on
\mathscr{C}_{\epsilon}
. More precisely, there exists a constant
c_{\mathrm{Lip}}=c_{\mathrm{Lip}}(n,Y)>0
such that for any
x,y\in\mathscr{C}_{\epsilon}
,

c_{\mathrm{Lip}}d_{Z}(x,y)\leq|\vec{u}(x)-\vec{u}(y)|\leq C_{\mathrm{Lip}}d_{Z}(x,y),

where
C_{\mathrm{Lip}}
is the same constant in Lemma
5.15
.

Proof.

For simplicity, we assume
r=1
. First, we prove

Claim 5.17
.

For
\delta^{\prime}>0
, if
\delta\leq\delta(n,Y,\mathfrak{c},D,\delta^{\prime})
, then

\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.08334pt\hskip-2.08334pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\displaystyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-2.08334pt\hskip-2.77774pt\raisebox{-1.65277pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\displaystyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.77774pt\hskip 2.08334pt}\kern-7.49994pt}{\hbox{\hskip 2.08334pt\hskip-2.08334pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\textstyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-2.08334pt\hskip-2.77774pt\raisebox{-1.65277pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\textstyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.77774pt\hskip 2.08334pt}\kern-7.49994pt}{\hbox{\hskip 1.72743pt\hskip-1.72743pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\scriptstyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-1.72743pt\hskip-2.45831pt\raisebox{-1.15695pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.13998pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.13998pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.45831pt\hskip 1.72743pt}\kern-6.78813pt}{\hbox{\hskip 1.57988pt\hskip-1.57988pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-1.57988pt\hskip-2.29166pt\raisebox{-0.82639pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.09999pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.09999pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.29166pt\hskip 1.57988pt}\kern-6.49301pt}\int_{B^{*}(z,15/8)}\sum_{r_{x}\leq r_{i}\leq 2^{-5}}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.08334pt}\kern-7.49994pt}{\hbox{\hskip 1.57988pt}\kern-6.49301pt}\int_{B^{*}(x,r_{i})}\left|\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{y}(r_{i}^{2}/40)-\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{y}(40r_{i}^{2})\right|^{1/2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\leq\delta^{\prime}.

We define
W_{x}(s)=\left\lvert\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{x}(s/40)-\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{x}(40s)\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}
for

and
W_{x}(s)=0
for

, where

. Then it is clear that

\displaystyle\sum_{r_{x}\leq r_{i}\leq 2^{-5}}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2^{-5}}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip (5.13)

Indeed, for any
y\in B^{*}(x,r_{i})
, if
r_{i}\geq r_{x}
, it follows from the
\mathfrak{c}^{-2}
-Lipschitz property of the radius function that

r_{y}\leq r_{x}+\mathfrak{c}^{-2}d_{Z}(x,y)\leq C_{0}r_{i}.

From this, it is clear that (
5.13
) holds. Moreover, if
r_{i}\leq\frac{r_{x}}{2C_{0}}
, we have for any
y\in B^{*}(x,r_{i})
,

r_{y}\geq r_{x}-\mathfrak{c}^{-2}d_{Z}(x,y)>r_{x}/2\geq C_{0}r_{i},

which implies
W_{y}(r_{i}^{2})=0
. From this fact, we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{r_{i}\leq\frac{r_{x}}{2C_{0}}}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.08334pt\hskip-2.08334pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\displaystyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-2.08334pt\hskip-2.77774pt\raisebox{-1.65277pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\displaystyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.77774pt\hskip 2.08334pt}\kern-7.49994pt}{\hbox{\hskip 2.08334pt\hskip-2.08334pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\textstyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-2.08334pt\hskip-2.77774pt\raisebox{-1.65277pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\textstyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.77774pt\hskip 2.08334pt}\kern-7.49994pt}{\hbox{\hskip 1.72743pt\hskip-1.72743pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\scriptstyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-1.72743pt\hskip-2.45831pt\raisebox{-1.15695pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.13998pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.13998pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.45831pt\hskip 1.72743pt}\kern-6.78813pt}{\hbox{\hskip 1.57988pt\hskip-1.57988pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-1.57988pt\hskip-2.29166pt\raisebox{-0.82639pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.09999pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.09999pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.29166pt\hskip 1.57988pt}\kern-6.49301pt}\int_{B^{*}(x,r_{i})}W_{y}(r_{i}^{2})\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)=0.

1.57988pt}\kern-6.49301pt}\int_{B^{*}(x,r_{i})}W_{y}(r_{i}^{2})\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)\right)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.08334pt\hskip-2.08334pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\displaystyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-2.08334pt\hskip-2.77774pt\raisebox{-1.65277pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\displaystyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.77774pt\hskip 2.08334pt}\kern-7.49994pt}{\hbox{\hskip 2.08334pt\hskip-2.08334pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\textstyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-2.08334pt\hskip-2.77774pt\raisebox{-1.65277pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\textstyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.77774pt\hskip 2.08334pt}\kern-7.49994pt}{\hbox{\hskip 1.72743pt\hskip-1.72743pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\scriptstyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-1.72743pt\hskip-2.45831pt\raisebox{-1.15695pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.13998pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.13998pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.45831pt\hskip 1.72743pt}\kern-6.78813pt}{\hbox{\hskip 1.57988pt\hskip-1.57988pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-1.57988pt\hskip-2.29166pt\raisebox{-0.82639pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.09999pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.09999pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.29166pt\hskip 1.57988pt}\kern-6.49301pt}\int_{B^{*}(z,15/8)}\sum_{r_{x}\leq r_{i}\leq 2^{-5}}\frac{1}{\mu(B^{*}(x,r_{i}))}\int_{B^{*}(x,r_{i})}W_{y}(r_{i}^{2})\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)+C(\mathfrak{c})\delta^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq
