\displaystyle D\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.08334pt\hskip-2.08334pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\displaystyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-2.08334pt\hskip-2.77774pt\raisebox{-1.65277pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\displaystyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.77774pt\hskip 2.08334pt}\kern-7.49994pt}{\hbox{\hskip 2.08334pt\hskip-2.08334pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\textstyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-2.08334pt\hskip-2.77774pt\raisebox{-1.65277pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\textstyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.77774pt\hskip 2.08334pt}\kern-7.49994pt}{\hbox{\hskip 1.72743pt\hskip-1.72743pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\scriptstyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-1.72743pt\hskip-2.45831pt\raisebox{-1.15695pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.13998pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.13998pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.45831pt\hskip 1.72743pt}\kern-6.78813pt}{\hbox{\hskip 1.57988pt\hskip-1.57988pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-1.57988pt\hskip-2.29166pt\raisebox{-0.82639pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.09999pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.09999pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.29166pt\hskip 1.57988pt}\kern-6.49301pt}\int_{B^{*}(z,15/8)}\sum_{r_{i}\leq 2^{-5}}r_{i}^{-k}\int_{B^{*}(x,r_{i})}W_{y}(r_{i}^{2})\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)+C(\mathfrak{c})\delta^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle D^{2}\int_{B^{*}(z,31/16)}\int_{B^{*}(z,31/16)}\sum_{r_{i}\leq 2^{-5}}r_{i}^{-k}\chi_{\{d_{Z}(x,y)\leq r_{i}\}}W_{y}(r_{i}^{2})\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)+C(\mathfrak{c})\delta^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle D^{2}\int_{B^{*}(z,31/16)}\int_{B^{*}(z,31/16)}\sum_{r_{i}\leq 2^{-5}}r_{i}^{-k}\chi_{\{d_{Z}(x,y)\leq r_{i}\}}W_{y}(r_{i}^{2})\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)+C(\mathfrak{c})\delta^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle D^{2}\int_{B^{*}(z,31/16)}\sum_{r_{i}\leq 2^{-5}}r_{i}^{-k}\mu(B^{*}(y,r_{i}))W_{y}(r_{i}^{2})\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)+C(\mathfrak{c})\delta^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle D^{3}\int_{B^{*}(z,31/16)}\sum_{r_{i}\leq 2^{-5}}W_{y}(r_{i}^{2})\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)+C(\mathfrak{c})\delta^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle D^{3}\int_{B^{*}(z,31/16)}\sum_{C_{0}^{-1}r_{y}\leq r_{i}\leq 2^{-5}}\left|\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{y}(r_{i}^{2}/40)-\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{y}(40r_{i}^{2})\right|^{1/2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)+C(\mathfrak{c})\delta^{\frac{1}{2}},

(5.15)

where we used (
5.14
) and
(*_{D})
. Note that in the seventh line, we also used Fubini’s theorem. From Proposition
5.1
and
(n2)
of Definition
5.9
, we conclude that for any
y\in\mathscr{C}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{C_{0}^{-1}r_{y}\leq r_{i}\leq 2^{-5}}|\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{y}(r_{i}^{2}/40)-\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{y}(40r_{i}^{2})|^{1/2}\leq\Psi(\delta).

(5.16)

Combining with (
5.3
), this finishes the proof of Claim
5.17
.

Claim 5.18
.

For
\epsilon>0
, if
\delta^{\prime\prime}\leq\delta^{\prime\prime}(n,Y,D,\epsilon)
and for some
x\in\mathscr{C}\bigcap B^{*}(z,15/8)
with

\sum_{r_{x}\leq r_{i}\leq 2^{-5}}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.08334pt\hskip-2.08334pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\displaystyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-2.08334pt\hskip-2.77774pt\raisebox{-1.65277pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\displaystyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.77774pt\hskip 2.08334pt}\kern-7.49994pt}{\hbox{\hskip 2.08334pt\hskip-2.08334pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\textstyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-2.08334pt\hskip-2.77774pt\raisebox{-1.65277pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\textstyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.77774pt\hskip 2.08334pt}\kern-7.49994pt}{\hbox{\hskip 1.72743pt\hskip-1.72743pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\scriptstyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-1.72743pt\hskip-2.45831pt\raisebox{-1.15695pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.13998pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.13998pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.45831pt\hskip 1.72743pt}\kern-6.78813pt}{\hbox{\hskip 1.57988pt\hskip-1.57988pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-1.57988pt\hskip-2.29166pt\raisebox{-0.82639pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.09999pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.09999pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.29166pt\hskip 1.57988pt}\kern-6.49301pt}\int_{B^{*}(x,r_{i})}\left|\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{y}(r_{i}^{2}/40)-\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{y}(40r_{i}^{2})\right|^{1/2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)\leq\delta^{\prime\prime},

(5.17)

then for any
r_{x}\leq s\leq 2^{-5}
, there exists a matrix
T_{x,s}

with
\|T_{x,s}-\mathrm{Id}\|\leq\epsilon
such that
\vec{u}_{x,s}=T_{x,s}\left(\vec{u}-\vec{u}(x)\right)
is a
(k,\epsilon,s)
-splitting map at
x
.

We set
\alpha=c_{1}D^{-2}
, where
c_{1}=c_{1}(n,Y)>0
is a constant to be determined. For any
r_{i}=2^{-i}
with
\alpha^{-1}r_{x}\leq r_{i}\leq 2^{-5}
, we can find subset
\mathscr{C}_{r_{i},x}\subset\mathscr{C}\cap B^{*}(x,r_{i})
such that

\displaystyle\mu(\mathscr{C}_{r_{i},x})\geq\frac{1}{2}\mu\left(\mathscr{C}\cap B^{*}(x,r_{i})\right)\geq\frac{r_{i}^{k}}{2D}

and for any
w\in\mathscr{C}_{r_{i},x}
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{w}(r_{i}^{2}/40)-\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{w}(40r_{i}^{2})\right|^{1/2}\leq 2\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.08334pt\hskip-2.08334pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\displaystyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-2.08334pt\hskip-2.77774pt\raisebox{-1.65277pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\displaystyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.77774pt\hskip 2.08334pt}\kern-7.49994pt}{\hbox{\hskip 2.08334pt\hskip-2.08334pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\textstyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-2.08334pt\hskip-2.77774pt\raisebox{-1.65277pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\textstyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.77774pt\hskip 2.08334pt}\kern-7.49994pt}{\hbox{\hskip 1.72743pt\hskip-1.72743pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\scriptstyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-1.72743pt\hskip-2.45831pt\raisebox{-1.15695pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.13998pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.13998pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.45831pt\hskip 1.72743pt}\kern-6.78813pt}{\hbox{\hskip 1.57988pt\hskip-1.57988pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-1.57988pt\hskip-2.29166pt\raisebox{-0.82639pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.09999pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.09999pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.29166pt\hskip 1.57988pt}\kern-6.49301pt}\int_{B^{*}(x,r_{i})}\left|\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{y}(r_{i}^{2}/40)-\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{y}(40r_{i}^{2})\right|^{1/2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y).

(5.18)

From Lemma
5.13
, we know that for
w\in\mathscr{C}_{r_{i},x}
,
|\mathfrak{t}(w)-\mathfrak{t}(x)|\leq\Psi(\delta)r_{i}^{2}
. By Definition
3.2
, any point
w\in\mathscr{C}_{r_{i},x}
is
(\delta,s)
-selfsimilar for
r_{w}\leq s\leq 1
.

We claim that there must exist strongly
(k,\alpha,\delta,r_{i})
-independent points in
\mathscr{C}_{r_{i},x}
at
x
. Indeed, suppose otherwise. Then by Lemma
4.18
, we can find
x_{i}\in\mathscr{C}_{r_{i},x}
for
1\leq i\leq N
with
N\leq C(n,Y)\alpha^{1-k}
and

\displaystyle\mathscr{C}_{r_{i},x}\subset\bigcup_{i=1}^{N}B^{*}(x_{i},\alpha r_{i}).

Then by assumption
(*_{D})
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{r_{i}^{k}}{2D}\leq\mu(\mathscr{C}_{r_{i},x})\leq\sum_{i}^{N}\mu\big(B^{*}(x_{i},\alpha r_{i})\big)\leq ND(\alpha r_{i})^{k}\leq C(n,Y)D\alpha r_{i}^{k}.

By the definition of
\alpha
, we obtain a contradiction if
c_{1}
is chosen to be small.

Let
\{y_{j}\}_{1\leq j\leq k}\subset\mathscr{C}_{r_{i},x}
be strongly
(k,\alpha,\delta,r_{i})
-independent points at
x
. Then by (
5.18
), we have

\displaystyle\left|\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{y_{j}}(r_{i}^{2}/40)-\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{y_{j}}(40r_{i}^{2})\right|^{1/2}\leq 2\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.08334pt\hskip-2.08334pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\displaystyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-2.08334pt\hskip-2.77774pt\raisebox{-1.65277pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\displaystyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.77774pt\hskip 2.08334pt}\kern-7.49994pt}{\hbox{\hskip 2.08334pt\hskip-2.08334pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\textstyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-2.08334pt\hskip-2.77774pt\raisebox{-1.65277pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\textstyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.77774pt\hskip 2.08334pt}\kern-7.49994pt}{\hbox{\hskip 1.72743pt\hskip-1.72743pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\scriptstyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-1.72743pt\hskip-2.45831pt\raisebox{-1.15695pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.13998pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.13998pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.45831pt\hskip 1.72743pt}\kern-6.78813pt}{\hbox{\hskip 1.57988pt\hskip-1.57988pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-1.57988pt\hskip-2.29166pt\raisebox{-0.82639pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.09999pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.09999pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.29166pt\hskip 1.57988pt}\kern-6.49301pt}\int_{B^{*}(x,r_{i})}\left|\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{y}(r_{i}^{2}/40)-\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{y}(40r_{i}^{2})\right|^{1/2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y),

which implies

\displaystyle\mathfrak{S}_{r_{i}}^{k,\alpha,\delta}(x)\leq 2k\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.08334pt\hskip-2.08334pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\displaystyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-2.08334pt\hskip-2.77774pt\raisebox{-1.65277pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\displaystyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.77774pt\hskip 2.08334pt}\kern-7.49994pt}{\hbox{\hskip 2.08334pt\hskip-2.08334pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\textstyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-2.08334pt\hskip-2.77774pt\raisebox{-1.65277pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\textstyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.77774pt\hskip 2.08334pt}\kern-7.49994pt}{\hbox{\hskip 1.72743pt\hskip-1.72743pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\scriptstyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-1.72743pt\hskip-2.45831pt\raisebox{-1.15695pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.13998pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.13998pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.45831pt\hskip 1.72743pt}\kern-6.78813pt}{\hbox{\hskip 1.57988pt\hskip-1.57988pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-1.57988pt\hskip-2.29166pt\raisebox{-0.82639pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.09999pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.09999pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.29166pt\hskip 1.57988pt}\kern-6.49301pt}\int_{B^{*}(x,r_{i})}\left\lvert\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{y}(r_{i}^{2}/40)-\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{y}(40r_{i}^{2})\right\rvert^{1/2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)+\left\lvert\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{x}(r_{i}^{2}/40)-\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{x}(40r_{i}^{2})\right\rvert^{1/2}.

Combining this with (
5.16
) and (
5.17
), we have

\displaystyle\sum_{(2\alpha)^{-1}r_{x}\leq r_{i}\leq 2^{-5}}\mathfrak{S}_{r_{i}}^{k,\alpha,\delta}(x)\leq C(n)\delta^{\prime\prime}.

Thus, Claim
5.18
follows from Proposition
3.11
and Theorem
4.16
.

Now, we define
\mathscr{C}_{\epsilon}
as:

\displaystyle\mathscr{C}_{\epsilon}:=\left\{x\in\mathscr{C}\cap B^{*}(z,15/8)\big|\sum_{r_{x}\leq r_{i}\leq 2^{-5}}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 2.08334pt\hskip-2.08334pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\displaystyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-2.08334pt\hskip-2.77774pt\raisebox{-1.65277pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\displaystyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.77774pt\hskip 2.08334pt}\kern-7.49994pt}{\hbox{\hskip 2.08334pt\hskip-2.08334pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\textstyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-2.08334pt\hskip-2.77774pt\raisebox{-1.65277pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\textstyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.77774pt\hskip 2.08334pt}\kern-7.49994pt}{\hbox{\hskip 1.72743pt\hskip-1.72743pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\scriptstyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-1.72743pt\hskip-2.45831pt\raisebox{-1.15695pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.13998pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.13998pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.45831pt\hskip 1.72743pt}\kern-6.78813pt}{\hbox{\hskip 1.57988pt\hskip-1.57988pt\hbox{$\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\phantom{\int}$}$}\hskip-1.57988pt\hskip-2.29166pt\raisebox{-0.82639pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.09999pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.09999pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}$}}\hskip-2.29166pt\hskip 1.57988pt}\kern-6.49301pt}\int_{B^{*}(x,r_{i})}\left|\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{y}(r_{i}^{2}/40)-\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{y}(40r_{i}^{2})\right|^{1/2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)<\delta^{\prime\prime}\right\}.

If
\delta^{\prime\prime}\ll\epsilon
and
\delta^{\prime}\ll\delta^{\prime\prime}
, then statement (ii) holds. Moreover, by Claim
5.17
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mu\left(\mathscr{C}_{\epsilon}\right)\geq(1-\epsilon)\mu\left(\mathscr{C}\cap B^{*}(z,15/8)\right).

which implies statement (i). Furthermore, statement (iii) follows from Claim
5.18
.

Next, we prove the bi-Lipschitz property of
\vec{u}
on the set
\mathscr{C}_{\epsilon}
. Note that the upper bound has already been proved by Lemma
5.15
, so we focus on the lower bound.

Given any
x,y\in\mathscr{C}_{\epsilon}
, define
s:=d_{Z}(x,y)\geq\mathfrak{c}^{2}\max\{r_{x},r_{y}\}
. By
(n3)
of Definition
5.9
and Lemma
5.13
, we have
|\mathfrak{t}(x)-\mathfrak{t}(y)|\leq\Psi(\delta)s^{2}
. Moreover,
x
is
(k,\delta,s)
-cylindrical. Also, by Claim
5.18
, the map
\vec{u}_{x,s}=T_{x,s}\left(\vec{u}-\vec{u}(x)\right)
is a
(k,\Psi(\delta),s)
-splitting map at
x
.

By Definition
2.29
and Proposition
5.3
(iii),
\vec{u}_{x,s}
is close to the standard coordinate functions on
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{k}
, and
y
is close to a point in
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{k}_{0}
, provided that
\delta
is sufficiently small. Applying a limiting argument and Lemma
2.26
, we obtain

\displaystyle s\leq C(n,Y)|\vec{u}_{x,s}(y)|.

Using the definition of
\vec{u}_{x,s}
, this implies

\displaystyle s\leq C(n,Y)|\vec{u}(x)-\vec{u}(y)|.

This establishes the bi-Lipschitz property of
\vec{u}
on
\mathscr{C}_{\epsilon}
and completes the proof of statement (iv), thereby concluding the proof of the proposition.
∎

To establish the Ahlfors regularity of the measure
\mu
, we first prove

Lemma 5.19
.

Under the assumption
(*_{D})
, if
\mathfrak{c}\leq\mathfrak{c}(n)
and
\delta\leq\delta(n,Y,\mathfrak{c},D)
, then for any
x\in\mathscr{C}
and
r_{x}\leq s\leq r-d_{Z}(x,z)/2
, the following holds:

C^{-1}_{0}(n,Y,\mathfrak{c})s^{k}\leq\mu(B^{*}(x,s))\leq C_{0}(n,Y,\mathfrak{c})s^{k}.

Proof.

Note that by Definition
5.9
,
\mathscr{N}^{\prime}=\mathscr{N}\cap B^{*}(x,2s)
is also a
(k,\delta,\mathfrak{c},s)
-neck region with center
\mathscr{C}^{\prime}=\mathscr{C}\cap B^{*}(x,2s)
and the same radius function.

We first prove the upper bound. By Proposition
5.16
, there exists
\mathscr{C}^{\prime\prime}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\prime}\cap B^{*}(x,15s/8)
so that

\displaystyle\mu\left(\mathscr{C}^{\prime\prime}\right)\geq\mu\left(\mathscr{C}^{\prime}\bigcap B^{*}(x,15s/8)\right)/2.

(5.19)

We write
\mathscr{C}^{\prime\prime}=\mathscr{C}^{\prime\prime}_{+}\bigcup\mathscr{C}^{\prime\prime}_{0}
, where
\mathscr{C}^{\prime\prime}_{+}=\mathscr{C}^{\prime\prime}\bigcap\mathscr{C}_{+}
and
\mathscr{C}^{\prime\prime}_{0}=\mathscr{C}^{\prime\prime}\bigcap\mathscr{C}_{0}
.

By Definition
5.9
and Proposition
5.16
(iv),
\left\{B\left(\vec{u}(y),c_{\mathrm{Lip}}\mathfrak{c}^{2}r_{y}\right)\right\}_{y\in\mathscr{C}^{\prime\prime}}
are mutually disjoint. Moreover, by Lemma
5.15
, we have

\displaystyle|\vec{u}(x)-\vec{u}(y)|\leq C_{\mathrm{Lip}}d_{Z}(x,y)\leq C(n)s

for any
y\in\mathscr{C}^{\prime\prime}
. Thus, we estimate

\displaystyle\mu(\mathscr{C}^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in\mathscr{C}^{\prime\prime}_{+}}r_{y}^{k}+\mathscr{H}^{k}\left(\mathscr{C}^{\prime\prime}_{0}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C(n,Y)\mathfrak{c}^{-2k}\sum_{y\in\mathscr{C}_{1/2}}\left\lvert B(\vec{u}(y),c_{\mathrm{Lip}}\mathfrak{c}^{2}r_{y})\right\rvert+C(n,Y)\left\lvert\vec{u}(\mathscr{C}^{\prime\prime}_{0})\right\rvert\leq C(n,Y,\mathfrak{c})s^{k},

where we use
|\cdot|
to denote the volume in
\mathbb{R}^{k}
. Combining with (
5.19
), we obtain

\displaystyle\mu\left(B^{*}(x,s)\right)\leq\mu\left(B^{*}(x,15s/8)\bigcap\mathscr{C}\right)\leq 2\mu\left(\mathscr{C}^{\prime\prime}\right)\leq C(n,Y,\mathfrak{c})s^{k}.

Next, we prove the lower bound. By Lemma
5.14
and Theorem
A.11
, for a small constant
\epsilon>0
to be determined later, if
\delta\leq\delta(n,Y,\epsilon)
, then for every point
y\in\mathscr{C}\bigcap B^{*}(x,s)
and every scale
s^{\prime}\in[\mathfrak{c}^{2}r_{y},2s]
, there exists a lower triangular
k\times k
matrix
A_{y,s^{\prime}}
such that
A_{y,s^{\prime}}(\vec{u}-\vec{u}(y))
is a
(k,\epsilon,s^{\prime})
-splitting map at
y
. Without loss of generality, we assume
\vec{u}(x)=\vec{0}^{k}
,
A_{x,s}=\mathrm{Id}
and
\|A_{y,s}-\mathrm{Id}\|\leq\epsilon
for any
y\in\mathscr{C}\bigcap B^{*}(x,s)
. Moreover,
A_{y,s^{\prime}}
satisfies conclusions (ii) and (iii) in Theorem
A.11
.

Note that since
y
is
(k,\delta,s^{\prime})
-cylindrical for any
s^{\prime}\in[\mathfrak{c}^{2}r_{y},s]
, it follows that
y
is not
(k+1,\eta,s^{\prime})
-splitting for some constant
\eta=\eta(n,Y)
. Hence, the conditions of Theorem
A.11
are readily verified.

Next, we claim:

B(\vec{0}^{k},\epsilon_{0}s/2)\subset\bigcup_{y\in\mathscr{C}\bigcap B^{*}(x,s)}\left\{A_{y,\mathfrak{c}^{-5}r_{y}}^{-1}\left(\bar{B}(\vec{u}(y),\mathfrak{c}^{-5}r_{y})\right)\right\},

(5.20)

where
A_{y,s}^{-1}\left(\bar{B}(\vec{u}(y),s)\right):=\vec{u}(y)+A_{y,s}^{-1}\bar{B}(\vec{0}^{k},s)
.

Once (
5.20
) is proved, it follows that

\displaystyle 0<C^{-1}(n,Y)s^{k}\leq\left|B(\vec{0}^{k},\epsilon_{0}s/2)\right|

\displaystyle\leq C(k)\mathfrak{c}^{-5k}\sum_{y\in\mathscr{C}_{+}\bigcap\bar{B}^{*}(y,s)}\det\left(A_{y,\mathfrak{c}^{-5}r_{y}}^{-1}\right)r_{y}^{k}+\left\lvert\vec{u}\big(\mathscr{C}_{0}\bigcap B^{*}(x,s)\big)\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C(n)\mathfrak{c}^{-5k}\mu\left(B^{*}(x,s)\right),

where we used the fact that
\det(A_{y,\mathfrak{c}^{-5}r_{y}})\geq 2^{-k}
and
\vec{u}
is
C(n)
-Lipschitz.

To prove (
5.20
), we argue by contradiction. Assume that there exists
z^{\prime}\in B(\vec{0}^{k},\epsilon_{0}s/2)
not covered by
\bigcup_{y\in\mathscr{C}\bigcap B^{*}(x,s)}\left\{A_{y,\mathfrak{c}^{-5}r_{y}}^{-1}\left(\bar{B}(\vec{u}(y),\mathfrak{c}^{-5}r_{y})\right)\right\}
. Then for any
y\in\mathscr{C}\bigcap B^{*}(x,s)
, set
s_{y}
to be the minimal number
s^{\prime}\geq\mathfrak{c}^{-5}r_{y}
such that
z^{\prime}\in A_{y,s^{\prime}}^{-1}\left(\bar{B}(\vec{u}(y),s^{\prime})\right)
. Then define
\bar{s}=\min_{y\in\mathscr{C}\bigcap B^{*}(x,s)}s_{y}
.

We fix a small parameter
\epsilon^{\prime}>0
to be determined later. Note that by Proposition
5.4
, if
\epsilon\leq\epsilon(n,Y,\epsilon^{\prime})
, then we can find
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}_{x,s}\bigcap B^{*}(x,3s/4)
such that
|\vec{u}(x^{\prime})-z^{\prime}|\leq\epsilon^{\prime}s
. Then using
(n4)
in Definition
5.9
and
C(n)
-Lipschitz property of
\vec{u}
, we can find
x^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}\bigcap B^{*}(x,s)
such that

\displaystyle|\vec{u}(x^{\prime\prime})-z^{\prime}|\leq\epsilon^{\prime}s+C(n)\mathfrak{c}s.

(5.21)

Since
\vec{u}
and
A_{x^{\prime\prime},s}(\vec{u}-\vec{u}(x^{\prime\prime}))
are both
(k,\epsilon,s)
-splitting maps at
x
and
x^{\prime\prime}
, respectively, and
|\mathfrak{t}(x^{\prime\prime})-\mathfrak{t}(x)|\leq\Psi(\delta)s
by Lemma
5.13
, it follows from the argument of Lemma
A.2
that

\displaystyle\rVert A_{x^{\prime\prime},s}-\mathrm{Id}\rVert\leq\epsilon^{\prime},

Combining this with (
5.21
), we obtain

\displaystyle\left\lvert A_{x^{\prime\prime},s}\left(\vec{u}(x^{\prime\prime})-z^{\prime}\right)\right\rvert\leq 2\epsilon^{\prime}s+2C(n)\mathfrak{c}s.

If we choose
\mathfrak{c}\leq\mathfrak{c}(n)
and
\epsilon^{\prime}
to be sufficiently small, then we have

\displaystyle\bar{s}\leq s_{x^{\prime\prime}}\leq 2(\epsilon^{\prime}s+C(n)\mathfrak{c}s)<\frac{s}{100}.

(5.22)

Choose
\bar{y}\in\mathscr{C}\bigcap B^{*}(x,s)
such that
\bar{s}=s_{\bar{y}}
. Then we must have
\bar{s}>\mathfrak{c}^{-5}r_{\bar{y}}
and
z^{\prime}\in A_{\bar{y},2\bar{s}}^{-1}\left(\bar{B}(\vec{u}(\bar{y}),2\bar{s})\right)
. Thus,

\displaystyle A_{\bar{y},2\bar{s}}z^{\prime}\in\bar{B}\left(A_{\bar{y},2\bar{s}}\vec{u}(\bar{y}),2\bar{s}\right).

Since
A_{\bar{y},2\bar{s}}(\vec{u}-\vec{u}(\bar{y}))
is a
(k,\epsilon,2\bar{s})
-splitting map at
\bar{y}
and
\bar{y}
is
(k,\epsilon,2\bar{s})
-cylindrical, it follows from Proposition
5.4
that there exists
y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}_{\bar{y},2\bar{s}}\cap B^{*}(\bar{y},2\bar{s})
such that

\displaystyle\left\lvert A_{\bar{y},2\bar{s}}\left(\vec{u}(y^{\prime})-z^{\prime}\right)\right\rvert\leq\epsilon^{\prime}\bar{s},

(5.23)

provided that
\epsilon\leq\epsilon(n,Y,\epsilon^{\prime})
.

In particular, by Theorem
A.11
(ii), this implies that

\displaystyle\left\lvert\vec{u}(y^{\prime})-z^{\prime}\right\rvert\leq\epsilon^{\prime}\left(\frac{s}{\bar{s}}\right)^{C(n)\epsilon}\bar{s}\leq\frac{\epsilon_{0}s}{100},

(5.24)

where we used (
5.22
), provided that
\epsilon\leq\epsilon(n,Y)
is small.

Since
\vec{u}
is a
(k,\epsilon,s)
-splitting map at
x
, it follows from (
5.24
) and a limiting argument that
y^{\prime}\in B^{*}(x,3s/4)
. Moreover, from
(n4)
in Definition
5.9
, we can find
y^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}\bigcap B^{*}(\bar{y},2\bar{s})
so that
d_{Z}(y^{\prime},y^{\prime\prime})\leq 2\mathfrak{c}\bar{s}
, which gives that
y^{\prime\prime}\in B^{*}(x,(3/4+\mathfrak{c})s)\subset B^{*}(x,s)
.

Since
A_{\bar{y},2\bar{s}}(\vec{u}-\vec{u}(\bar{y}))
is
C_{\mathrm{Lip}}
-Lipschitz by Lemma
5.15
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\left\lvert A_{\bar{y},2\bar{s}}\left(\vec{u}(y^{\prime})-\vec{u}(y^{\prime\prime})\right)\right\rvert\leq 2C(n)\mathfrak{c}\bar{s}.

(5.25)

Combining (
5.23
) and (
5.25
), it implies that

\displaystyle\left\lvert A_{\bar{y},2\bar{s}}\left(\vec{u}(y^{\prime\prime})-z^{\prime}\right)\right\rvert\leq(2C(n)\mathfrak{c}+\epsilon^{\prime})\bar{s}.

(5.26)

Since
A_{\bar{y},2\bar{s}}(\vec{u}-\vec{u}(\bar{y}))
and
A_{y^{\prime\prime},2\bar{s}}(\vec{u}-\vec{u}(y^{\prime\prime}))
are both
(k,\epsilon,2\bar{s})
-splitting map at
\bar{y}
and
y^{\prime\prime}
, respectively, and
|\mathfrak{t}(y^{\prime\prime})-\mathfrak{t}(\bar{y})|\leq\Psi(\delta)\bar{s}
by Lemma
5.13
, it follows from the argument of Lemma
A.2
that

\displaystyle\rVert A_{\bar{y},2\bar{s}}\circ A_{y^{\prime\prime},2\bar{s}}^{-1}-\mathrm{Id}\rVert\leq\epsilon^{\prime}.

Combining this with (
5.26
), it follows that

\displaystyle\left\lvert A_{y^{\prime\prime},2\bar{s}}\left(\vec{u}(y^{\prime\prime})-z^{\prime}\right)\right\rvert\leq 2(2C(n)\mathfrak{c}+\epsilon^{\prime})\bar{s}.

(5.27)

On the other hand, it follows from Lemma
A.2
(or equation (
A.2
)), we have

\displaystyle\rVert A_{y^{\prime\prime},2\bar{s}}^{-1}\circ A_{y^{\prime\prime},\bar{s}/2}-\mathrm{Id}\rVert\leq C(n)\epsilon,

which, when combined with (
5.27
), implies

\displaystyle\left\lvert A_{y^{\prime\prime},\bar{s}/2}\left(\vec{u}(y^{\prime\prime})-z^{\prime}\right)\right\rvert\leq 3(2C(n)\mathfrak{c}+\epsilon^{\prime})\bar{s}.

(5.28)

Thus, if
\mathfrak{c}\leq\mathfrak{c}(n)
and
\epsilon\leq\epsilon(n,Y)
, we would have
3(2C(n)\mathfrak{c}+\epsilon^{\prime})<1/2
. However, (
5.28
) then implies that
z^{\prime}\in A_{y^{\prime\prime},\bar{s}/2}^{-1}\left(B(\vec{u}(y^{\prime\prime}),\bar{s}/2)\right)
, which contradicts the definition of
\bar{s}
.

In sum, we have proved (
5.20
), thereby completing the proof of the theorem.
∎

Next, we prove the Ahlfors regularity of
\mu
under the extra assumption that
\inf_{x\in\mathscr{C}}r_{x}>0
.

Lemma 5.20
.

Suppose
\inf_{x\in\mathscr{C}}r_{x}>0
. If
\mathfrak{c}\leq\mathfrak{c}(n)
and
\delta\leq\delta(n,Y,\mathfrak{c})
, then for any
x\in\mathscr{C}
and
r_{x}\leq s\leq r-d_{Z}(x,z)/2
, the following holds:

C^{-1}(n,Y,\mathfrak{c})s^{k}\leq\mu\big(B^{*}(x,s)\big)\leq C(n,Y,\mathfrak{c})s^{k}.

Proof.

We say that the property
(A_{j})
holds with a constant
D
if any
(k,\delta,\mathfrak{c},r)
-cylindrical neck region
\mathscr{N}
with
\delta\leq\delta(n,Y,\mathfrak{c})
and
\inf_{y\in\mathscr{C}}r_{y}\geq 2^{-j}r
satisfies
(*_{D})
.

First, note that by Proposition
2.25
, there are at most
L_{1}=L_{1}(n,Y,\mathfrak{c})
mutually disjoint balls of size
\mathfrak{c}^{2}2^{-5}r
contained in a ball of size
2r
. Thus, it follows from Definition
5.12
that the property
(A_{5})
holds with a constant
D_{1}=D_{1}(n,Y,\mathfrak{c})
.

We set
D_{0}:=C_{0}(n,Y,\mathfrak{c})
, where
C_{0}
is the constant from Lemma
5.19
. Then, we define
D_{2}=D_{0}+D_{1}
. We set
D_{3}=100^{k}\mathfrak{c}^{-2k}D_{2}L_{1}
and require
\delta\leq\delta(n,Y,\mathfrak{c},D_{3})
as in Lemma
5.19
.

Assume that the property
(A_{j_{0}})
holds with
D_{2}
with
j_{0}\geq 2
. Given a
(k,\delta,\mathfrak{c},r)
-cylindrical neck region
\mathscr{N}\subset B^{*}(z,2r)
with
\inf_{y\in\mathscr{C}}r_{y}\geq 2^{-j_{0}-1}r
, we consider
x\in\mathscr{C}
and
r_{x}\leq s\leq r-d_{Z}(x,z)/2
.

If
s\leq r/2
, then
\inf_{y\in\mathscr{C}}r_{y}\geq 2^{-j_{0}-1}r\geq 2^{-j_{0}}s
. By our induction assumption, we have

\displaystyle D_{2}^{-1}s^{k}\leq\mu\left(B^{*}(x,s)\right)\leq D_{2}s^{k}.

(5.29)

If
s>r/2
, then
B^{*}(x,s)\subset B^{*}(z,3r/2)
. If
r_{x}\leq 2^{-4}r
, then by the inductive assumption,

\displaystyle\mu\left(B^{*}(x,s)\right)\geq\mu\left(B^{*}(x,2^{-4}r)\right)\geq 16^{-k}D_{2}^{-1}r^{k}\geq 16^{-k}D_{2}^{-1}s^{k}.

If
r_{x}>2^{-4}r
, then by Definition
5.12
,

\displaystyle\mu\left(B^{*}(x,s)\right)\geq r_{x}^{k}\geq 16^{-k}r^{k}\geq 16^{-k}s^{k}.

Thus, in either case,

\displaystyle\mu\left(B^{*}(x,s)\right)\geq 16^{-k}D_{2}^{-1}s^{k}.

(5.30)

On the other hand, let
\mathscr{C}^{\prime}:=\{y_{i}\}_{1\leq N}
to be the subset of
B^{*}(z,3r/2)\bigcap\mathscr{C}
such that
r_{y_{i}}\geq 2^{-4}r
. Clearly,
N\leq L_{1}
. Moreover,
r_{y_{i}}\leq r_{x}+\mathfrak{c}^{-2}d_{Z}(x,y_{i})\leq(1+3\mathfrak{c}^{-2})r
.

Next, choose a maximal
2^{-4}r
-separated set
\{x_{j}\}
of
\left(B^{*}(z,3r/2)\bigcap\mathscr{C}\right)\setminus\mathscr{C}^{\prime}
. For each
j
, the inductive assumption gives

\displaystyle\mu\left(B^{*}(x_{j},2^{-4}r)\right)\leq 16^{-k}D_{2}r^{k}.

Therefore, by Definition
5.12
,

\displaystyle\mu\left(B^{*}(x,s)\right)\leq L_{1}\left((1+3\mathfrak{c}^{-2})^{k}+16^{-k}D_{2}\right)r^{k}\leq L_{1}2^{k}\left((1+3\mathfrak{c}^{-2})^{k}+16^{-k}D_{2}\right)s^{k}.

(5.31)

Combining (
5.30
) and (
5.31
), we conclude that the property
(A_{j_{0}+1})
holds with the constant
D_{3}
. Hence, we may apply Lemma
5.19
to deduce that for any
x\in\mathscr{C}
and
r_{x}\leq s\leq r-d_{Z}(x,z)/2
, we have

\displaystyle D_{0}^{-1}s^{k}\leq\mu\left(B^{*}(x,s)\right)\leq D_{0}s^{k}.

Since
D_{0}\leq D_{2}
, it follows that
(A_{j_{0}+1})
holds with constant
D_{2}
. By induction, we conclude that for all
j\geq 5
, the property
(A_{j})
holds with constant
D_{2}
. This completes the proof.
∎

Next, we prove the Ahlfors regularity for the
(k,\delta,\mathfrak{c},r)
-cylindrical neck region constructed in Proposition
5.11
.

Theorem 5.21

(Ahlfors regularity)
.

Given
\epsilon>0
, if
\mathfrak{c}\leq\mathfrak{c}(n)
and
\delta\leq\delta(n,Y,\mathfrak{c},\epsilon)
, then for the
(k,\delta,\mathfrak{c},r_{0})
-cylindrical neck region
\mathscr{N}=B^{*}(x_{0},2r_{0})\setminus B_{r_{x}}^{*}(\mathscr{C})
constructed in Proposition
5.11
, any
x\in\mathscr{C}
and
r_{x}\leq s\leq r_{0}-d_{Z}(x,x_{0})/2
, we have

\displaystyle C^{-1}(n,Y,\mathfrak{c})s^{k}\leq\mu(B^{*}(x,s))\leq C(n,Y,\mathfrak{c})s^{k}.

(5.32)

Moreover, we can find a countable collection of
\mathscr{H}^{k}
-measurable subsets
E_{i}\subset\mathscr{C}_{0}
such that
\mathscr{H}^{k}(\mathscr{C}_{0}\setminus\bigcup_{i}E_{i})=0
and for each
i
, there exists a bi-Lipschitz map
u_{i}:E_{i}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
, where
\mathbb{R}^{k}
is equipped with the standard Euclidean distance, such that

\displaystyle\sqrt{|\mathfrak{t}(x)-\mathfrak{t}(y)|}\leq\epsilon d_{Z}(x,y),\quad\forall x,y\in E_{i}.

Proof.

From the construction in the proof of Proposition
5.11
, there exist a sequence of
(k,\delta,\mathfrak{c},r_{0})
-cylindrical neck region
\mathscr{N}^{l}
(see (
5.8
)) with center
\mathscr{C}^{l}
and radius function
r_{x}^{l}
such that

(i)

r^{l}_{x}\to r_{x}
uniformly,

(ii)

\mathscr{N}^{l}
converge to
\mathscr{N}
in the Hausdorff distance, and

(iii)

\inf_{x\in\mathscr{C}^{l}}r^{l}_{x}\geq\gamma^{l}r_{0}
,

where
\gamma=\mathfrak{c}^{5}
. Moreover, it follows from (
5.12
) that for any
l\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\mathscr{C}_{0}\subset B^{*}_{2\gamma^{l}r_{0}}(\mathcal{G}^{l}).

(5.33)

Let
\mu^{l}
be the packing measure with respect to
\mathscr{N}^{l}
. We assume that
\mu^{\infty}
is the limit of
\mu^{l}
in the weak sense. It is clear that
\mu^{\infty}=\mu
on
\mathscr{C}_{+}
.

Set
r_{l}:=2\gamma^{l}r_{0}
. For any
x\in\mathscr{C}_{0}
and
s\in(0,r_{0}-d(x_{0},x)/2)
, we have

\displaystyle r_{l}^{k-n-2}\left\lvert B^{*}_{r_{l}}\left(\mathscr{C}_{0}\cap B^{*}(x,s)\right)\right\rvert\leq C(n,Y,\mathfrak{c})\mu^{l}\left(B^{*}_{2r_{l}}\left(B^{*}(x,s)\cap\mathscr{C}_{0}\right)\right)

(5.34)

provided that
r_{l}<s
. To see this, let
\{B^{*}(x^{l}_{i},r_{l}),\,x^{l}_{i}\in\mathcal{G}^{l}\cap B^{*}_{r_{l}}\left(B^{*}(x,s)\cap\mathscr{C}_{0}\right)\}_{1\leq i\leq N}
be a covering of
B^{*}(x,s)\cap\mathscr{C}_{0}
. Note that by (
5.33
), this covering is possible.

By Lemma
5.20
,
\mu(B^{*}(x^{l}_{i},r_{l}))\geq c(n,Y,\mathfrak{c})r_{l}^{k}>0
, since the radius function is
r_{l}
at
x_{i}^{l}
. Since
\{B^{*}(x_{i}^{l},\mathfrak{c}^{2}r_{l}/2)\}
are disjoint, it follows from Proposition
2.25
that any point belongs to at most
C(n,Y,\mathfrak{c})
balls in
\{B^{*}(x_{i}^{l},r_{l}/2)\}
. Consequently, we conclude that the cardinality
N
satisfies

\displaystyle N\leq C(n,Y,\mathfrak{c})r_{l}^{-k}\mu^{l}\left(B^{*}_{2r_{l}}\left(B^{*}(x,s)\cap\mathscr{C}_{0}\right)\right).

By Proposition
2.25
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left\lvert B^{*}_{r_{l}}\left(\mathscr{C}_{0}\cap B^{*}(x,s)\right)\right\rvert\leq N\max_{i}\left\lvert B^{*}(x^{l}_{i},2r_{l})\right\rvert\leq C(n,Y,\mathfrak{c})\mu_{l}\left(B^{*}_{2r_{l}}\left(B^{*}(x,s)\cap\mathscr{C}_{0}\right)\right)r_{l}^{n+2-k},

which yields (
5.34
).

Similarly, since
\{B^{*}(x_{i}^{l},\mathfrak{c}^{2}r_{l})\}
are disjoint, we have

\displaystyle N\leq\frac{\left\lvert B^{*}_{2r_{l}}\left(B^{*}(x,s)\cap\mathscr{C}_{0}\right)\right\rvert}{\min_{i}\left\lvert B^{*}(x^{l}_{i},\mathfrak{c}^{2}r_{l})\right\rvert}.

By Proposition
2.25
, we obtain

\displaystyle N\leq C(n,Y,\mathfrak{c})r_{l}^{-n-2}\left\lvert B^{*}_{2r_{l}}\left(B^{*}(x,s)\cap\mathscr{C}_{0}\right)\right\rvert.

Since
\mu^{l}(B^{*}(x^{l}_{i},2r_{l}))\leq C(n,Y,\mathfrak{c})r_{l}^{k}
by Lemma
5.20
, we have

\displaystyle\mu^{l}\left(B^{*}_{r_{l}}\left(B^{*}(x,s)\cap\mathscr{C}_{0}\right)\right)\leq C(n,Y,\mathfrak{c})Nr_{l}^{k}\leq C(n,Y,\mathfrak{c})\left\lvert B^{*}_{2r_{l}}\left(B^{*}(x,s)\cap\mathscr{C}_{0}\right)\right\rvert r_{l}^{k-n-2}.

(5.35)

Taking
l\to\infty
, we get the following estimate for Minkowski content
\mathscr{M}^{k}
from (
5.34
) and (
5.35
) (cf.
[
FL25a
, Definition 8.9]
):

\displaystyle C^{-1}(n,Y,\mathfrak{c})\mathscr{M}^{k}\left(B^{*}(x,s)\cap\mathscr{C}_{0}\right)\leq\mu^{\infty}\left(B^{*}(x,s)\cap\mathscr{C}_{0}\right)\leq C(n,Y,\mathfrak{c})\mathscr{M}^{k}\left(B^{*}(x,s)\cap\mathscr{C}_{0}\right).

(5.36)

From this, we have

\displaystyle\mu\left(B^{*}(x,s)\cap\mathscr{C}_{0}\right)=\mathscr{H}^{k}\left(B^{*}(x,s)\cap\mathscr{C}_{0}\right)\leq C(k)\mathscr{M}^{k}\left(B^{*}(x,s)\cap\mathscr{C}_{0}\right)\leq C(n,Y,\mathfrak{c})\mu^{\infty}\left(B^{*}(x,s)\cap\mathscr{C}_{0}\right).

Consequently, we have proved

\displaystyle\mu\leq C(n,Y,\mathfrak{c})\mu^{\infty}.

(5.37)

Next, we prove that
\mathscr{C}_{0}
is
k
-rectifiable with respect to
d_{Z}
. Fix
\epsilon>0
,
x\in\mathscr{C}_{0}
,
s\leq r_{0}-d_{Z}(x,x_{0})/2
and
\delta\leq\delta(n,Y,\epsilon)
. We define the neck region
\mathscr{N}^{l}_{x,s}:=B^{*}(x,2s)\setminus B^{*}_{r_{y}^{l}}(\mathscr{C}^{l}_{x,s})
with center
\mathscr{C}^{l}_{x,s}:=\mathscr{C}^{l}\cap B^{*}(x,2s)
. Note that by Lemma
5.20
, the corresponding packing measure, denoted by
\mu^{l}_{x,s}
, satisfies the Ahlfors regularity. Thus, by Proposition
5.16
, we can find
(k,\delta,s)
-splitting map
\vec{u}^{l}_{x,s}
and
\mathscr{C}^{l}_{x,s,\epsilon}\subset\mathscr{C}^{l}_{x,s}
such that
\vec{u}^{l}_{x,s}:\mathscr{C}^{l}_{x,s,\epsilon}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
is
C(n,Y)
-bi-Lipschitz and

\displaystyle\mu^{l}_{x,s}\left(B^{*}(x,s)\setminus\mathscr{C}^{l}_{x,s,\epsilon}\right)<\epsilon s^{k}.

(5.38)

Next, we set

\displaystyle\mu_{x,s}:=\lim_{l\to\infty}\mu^{l}_{x,s},\quad\mathscr{C}_{x,s,\epsilon}:=\lim_{l\to\infty}\mathscr{C}^{l}_{x,s,\epsilon},\quad\vec{u}_{x,s}:=\lim_{l\to\infty}\vec{u}^{l}_{x,s}.

Here, the first limit refers to convergence in the weak sense, while the second refers to Hausdorff convergence. From (
5.37
) and (
5.38
), we have

\displaystyle\mu\left(B^{*}(x,s)\setminus\mathscr{C}_{x,s,\epsilon}\right)\leq C(n,Y,\mathfrak{c})\mu_{x,s}\left(B^{*}(x,s)\setminus\mathscr{C}_{x,s,\epsilon}\right)\leq C(n,Y,\mathfrak{c})\epsilon s^{k}.

(5.39)

Moreover,
\vec{u}_{x,s}
is a
C(n,Y)
-bi-Lipschitz map on
\mathscr{C}_{x,s,\epsilon}
. Set
E_{x,s,\epsilon}:=\mathscr{C}_{0}\bigcap\mathscr{C}_{x,s,\epsilon}
. Since the Ricci flow limit space
Z
is separable (see Theorem
2.11
), we can choose a countable dense subset
\{x_{i}\}
of
\mathscr{C}_{0}
and define

\displaystyle E:=\bigcup_{s\in\mathbb{Q}\bigcap\left(0,r_{0}-d_{Z}(x_{0},x_{i})/2\right),x_{i}}E_{x_{i},s,\epsilon}.

By (
5.39
) and a standard geometric measure theory argument, we obtain that
\mathscr{H}^{k}(\mathscr{C}_{0}\setminus E)=0
. Indeed, if
\mathscr{H}^{k}(\mathscr{C}_{0}\setminus E)>0
, then there exists a
k
-density point
y\in\mathscr{C}_{0}\setminus E
(see
[
Sim83
, Theorem 3.6]
). In other words, we have

\displaystyle\limsup_{s\to 0}\frac{\mathscr{H}^{k}\left(B^{*}(y,s)\cap(\mathscr{C}_{0}\setminus E)\right)}{s^{k}}\geq c(k)>0.

In particular, we can find a small
s\in(0,r_{0}-d_{Z}(x_{0},y))
such that
\mathscr{H}^{k}\left(B^{*}(y,s)\cap(\mathscr{C}_{0}\setminus E)\right)\geq c(k)s^{k}/2
. Then, there exist
x_{i}
close to
y
and
s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Q}\bigcap\left(0,r_{0}-d_{Z}(x_{0},x_{i})/2\right)
close to
s
such that

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}^{k}\left(B^{*}(x_{i},s^{\prime})\cap(\mathscr{C}_{0}\setminus E)\right)\geq c(k)(s^{\prime})^{k}/2,

which contradicts (
5.38
). Consequently, we have
\mathscr{H}^{k}(\mathscr{C}_{0}\setminus E)=0
and hence
\mathscr{C}_{0}
is rectifiable.

Next, we show that

\displaystyle\mu^{\infty}\leq C(n,Y,\mathfrak{c})\mu.

(5.40)

Indeed, by (
5.36
),

\displaystyle\mu^{\infty}\left(B^{*}(x,s)\cap\mathscr{C}_{0}\right)\leq C(n,Y,\mathfrak{c})\mathscr{H}^{k}\left(B^{*}(x,s)\cap\mathscr{C}_{0}\right)=C(n,Y,\mathfrak{c})\mu\left(B^{*}(x,s)\cap\mathscr{C}_{0}\right),

since the Hausdorff measure and the Minkowski content are equivalent on the
k
-rectifiable set
\mathscr{C}_{0}
(see
[
Fed69
, Theorem 3.2.29]
). Combining (
5.40
) with Lemma
5.20
, we obtain the Ahlfors regularity (
5.32
) for
\mu
.

With (
5.32
) established, the last statement of the theorem follows directly from Lemma
5.2
and Proposition
5.16
. This completes the proof.
∎

We now prove the main theorem of the subsection.

Theorem 5.22
.

For any
k\in\{0,1,\ldots,n-2\}
, the set
\mathcal{S}^{k}_{\mathrm{c}}
is horizontally parabolic
k
-rectifiable with respect to
d_{Z}
.

Proof.

The case
k\in\{1,\ldots,n-2\}
follows directly from Corollary
4.24
(c), Proposition
5.11
, Theorem
5.21
, and a standard countable covering argument. The case
k=0
is simpler: any point
z\in\mathcal{S}^{0}_{\mathrm{c}}
must be isolated, since every tangent flow at
z
is the standard sphere
S^{n}
.
∎

We end this subsection with the following result.

Corollary 5.23
.

Each connected component of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
is contained in a time slice.

Proof.

It follows from Proposition
5.11
and a standard covering argument that, for some sufficiently small
\delta>0
,

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\setminus\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{c}}^{1}\right)\subset\bigcup_{i}\left(\mathscr{C}_{i,0}\bigcap B^{*}(x_{i},r_{i})\right),

where
\mathscr{N}_{i}=B^{*}(x_{i},2r_{i})\setminus B^{*}_{r_{x}}(\mathscr{C}_{i})
is a
(2,\delta,r_{i})
-neck region with
x_{i}\in\mathcal{S}_{c}^{2}
. Moreover, by the definition of the cylindrical neck region, we have
\mathscr{C}_{i,0}\bigcap B^{*}(x_{i},2r_{i})\subset\mathcal{S}_{c}^{2}
.

We claim that

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}^{1}\left(\mathfrak{t}\big(\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\setminus\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{c}}^{1}\big)\right)=0,

(5.41)

where
\mathscr{H}^{1}
denotes the
1
-dimensional Hausdorff measure on
\mathbb{R}
. To prove (
5.41
), it suffices to show that for each
i
:

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}^{1}\left(\mathfrak{t}\left(\mathscr{C}_{i,0}\bigcap B^{*}(x_{i},r_{i})\right)\right)=0.

(5.42)

Let
S:=\mathscr{C}_{i,0}\bigcap\overline{B^{*}(x_{i},r_{i})}
, which is a closed subset of
\mathcal{S}^{2}_{c}
. By Theorem
5.21
, we have

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}^{2}(S)\leq\mathscr{H}^{2}\left(\mathscr{C}_{i,0}\bigcap B^{*}(x_{i},3r_{i}/2)\right)\leq C(n,Y)r_{i}^{2}<\infty.

Since
S
is compact, Lemma
5.10
implies that for any
\epsilon>0
, there exists
r_{\epsilon}>0
such that each
x\in S
is uniformly
(2,\epsilon,r_{\epsilon})
-cylindrical. In particular, by Lemma
5.2
, we have

\displaystyle|\mathfrak{t}(x)-\mathfrak{t}(y)|\leq\Psi(\epsilon)d_{Z}^{2}(x,y).

(5.43)

for any
x,y\in S
with
d_{Z}(x,y)\leq r_{\epsilon}
.

By the definition of Hausdorff measure, there exists a countable cover
\{B^{*}(y_{j},s_{j})\}
of
S
with
s_{j}<r_{\epsilon}/2
such that

\displaystyle\sum_{j}s_{j}^{2}\leq C(n,Y)r_{i}^{2}.

(5.44)

Then, from (
5.43
), for any
x,y\in B^{*}(y_{j},s_{j})
, we obtain

\displaystyle|\mathfrak{t}(x)-\mathfrak{t}(y)|\leq\Psi(\epsilon)d_{Z}^{2}(x,y)\leq\Psi(\epsilon)s_{j}^{2}.

Thus, it implies

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}^{1}\left(\mathfrak{t}(B^{*}(y_{j},s_{j})\bigcap S)\right)\leq\Psi(\epsilon)s_{j}^{2}

and summing over all
j
, it follows from (
5.44
) that

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}^{1}\left(\mathfrak{t}(S)\right)\leq\Psi(\epsilon)r_{i}^{2}.

Since
\epsilon
is arbitrary, we conclude that
\mathscr{H}^{1}\left(\mathfrak{t}(S)\right)=0
, which proves (
5.42
), and hence (
5.41
).

On the other hand, it follows from Corollary
4.24
(c) that
\mathscr{H}^{2}(\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{c}}^{1})=0
. Since the time-function
\mathfrak{t}
is
2
-Hölder, a similar argument yields
\mathscr{H}^{1}\left(\mathfrak{t}(\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{c}}^{1})\right)=0
. Combining this with (
5.41
), we obtain

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}^{1}\left(\mathfrak{t}\big(\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\big)\right)=0.

(5.45)

It now follows from (
5.45
) that the image under
\mathfrak{t}
of any connected component of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
must be a single point. This completes the proof.
∎

5.4
Rectifiability of quotient cylindrical singularities

In this subsection, we consider the quotient cylindrical singularities.

As before, we set

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{m}_{-1}=(\bar{M},\bar{g},\bar{f})=\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\times S^{n-m},g_{E}\times g_{S^{n-m}},\frac{|\vec{x}|^{2}}{4}+\frac{n-m}{2}+\Theta_{n-m}\right).

For any finite group
\Gamma\leqslant\mathrm{Iso}(\mathcal{C}^{m}_{-1})
acting freely on
\bar{M}
, we set
\pi:\bar{M}\to\bar{M}/\Gamma
to be the natural quotient map and define

\displaystyle{\@index{\@indexphrase{$\mathcal{C}^{m}(\Gamma)$}}}\mathcal{C}^{m}(\Gamma)_{-1}=(\bar{M}_{\Gamma},\bar{g}_{\Gamma},\bar{f}_{\Gamma}),

(5.46)

where
\bar{M}_{\Gamma}=\bar{M}/\Gamma
,
\bar{g}_{\Gamma}
is the quotient metric of
\bar{g}
, and
\bar{f}_{\Gamma}
is defined so that

\displaystyle\pi^{*}\bar{f}_{\Gamma}=\bar{f}-\log|\Gamma|.

Also, we define

\displaystyle{\@index{\@indexphrase{$\Theta_{n-m}(\Gamma)$}}}\Theta_{n-m}(\Gamma):=\Theta_{n-m}-\log|\Gamma|.

Let
\mathcal{C}^{m}_{k}(\Gamma)_{-1}
be a quotient cylinder as in (
5.46
) so that
\mathcal{C}^{m}(\Gamma)_{-1}
splits off an
\mathbb{R}^{k}
exactly. We define
\mathcal{C}^{m}_{k}(\Gamma)=(\bar{M},(\bar{g}_{\Gamma}(t))_{t<0},(\bar{f}_{\Gamma}(t))_{t<0})
to be the associated Ricci flow, where
t=0
corresponds to the singular time, and the potential function is given by

\displaystyle\pi^{*}\bar{f}_{\Gamma}(t)=\frac{|\vec{x}|^{2}}{4|t|}+\frac{n-m}{4}+\Theta_{n-m}(\Gamma).

Let
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{m}_{k}(\Gamma)
be the completion of
\mathcal{C}^{m}_{k}(\Gamma)
, equipped with the spacetime distance
d_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
, defined with respect a spacetime distance constant
\epsilon_{0}=\epsilon_{0}(n,Y)
. We also define the base point
p^{*}
as the limit of
(\bar{p},t)
as
t\nearrow 0
with respect to
d_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
, where
\bar{p}\in\mathcal{C}^{m}_{k}(\Gamma)_{-1}
is a minimum point of
\bar{f}_{\Gamma}(-1)
. For any
t<0
, we have

\displaystyle\nu_{p^{*};t}=(4\pi|t|)^{-\frac{n}{2}}e^{-\bar{f}_{\Gamma}(t)}\,\mathrm{d}V_{\bar{g}_{\Gamma}(t)}.

Next, we consider a general noncollapsed Ricci flow limit space
(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})
over
\mathbb{I}
, obtained as the limit of a sequence in
\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)
. Then, we have the following definition similar to Definition
2.27
.

Definition 5.24
.

A point
z\in Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
is called
a quotient cylindrical point regarding
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{m}_{k}(\Gamma)
if a tangent flow at
z
is
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{m}_{k}(\Gamma)
for some
k
,
m
and
\Gamma
.

It follows from
[
FL25b
, Theorem 8.11]
that at any quotient cylindrical point, the tangent flow is unique. Thus, we have the following definition similar to Definition
2.28
.

Definition 5.25
.

Let
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{qc}}^{k}(m,\Gamma)
be the subset of
Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
consisting of all singular points at which one tangent flow is isometric to
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{m}_{k}(\Gamma)
. Moreover, we define

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{qc}}^{k}=\bigcup_{m,\Gamma,0\leq j\leq k}\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{qc}}^{j}(m,\Gamma).

It is clear that
\mathcal{S}_{{\mathrm{c}}}^{k}\subset\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{qc}}^{k}\subset\mathcal{S}^{k}
.

Next, we consider the quantitative version.

Definition 5.26
.

A point
z\in Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
is called
(k,\epsilon,r)
-quotient cylindrical regarding
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{m}_{k}(\Gamma)
if
\mathfrak{t}(z)-\epsilon^{-1}r^{2}\in\mathbb{I}^{-}
and

\displaystyle(Z,r^{-1}d_{Z},z,r^{-2}(\mathfrak{t}-\mathfrak{t}(z)))\quad\text{is $\epsilon$-close to}\quad(\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{m}_{k}(\Gamma),d^{*}_{\mathcal{C}},p^{*},\mathfrak{t})\quad\text{over}\quad[-\epsilon^{-1},\epsilon^{-1}].

Let
\tilde{\phi}
be an
\epsilon
-map map from Definition
2.13
, which is defined from
B^{*}(p^{*},\epsilon^{-1})\cap\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{m}_{k}(\Gamma)_{[-\epsilon^{-1},\epsilon^{-1}]}
to
Z_{[\mathfrak{t}(z)-\epsilon^{-1}r^{2},\mathfrak{t}(z)+\epsilon^{-1}r^{2}]}
, where
B^{*}(p^{*},\epsilon^{-1})
is the metric ball in
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{m}_{k}(\Gamma)
with respect to
d_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
. Then, we define
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{z,r}:=\tilde{\phi}\left(B^{*}(p^{*},\epsilon^{-1})\cap\mathrm{spine}(\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{m}_{k}(\Gamma))\right),{\@index{\@indexphrase{$\mathcal{L}_{z,r}$}}}

and say that
z
is
(k,\epsilon,r)
-quotient cylindrical regarding
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{m}_{k}(\Gamma)

with respect to
\mathcal{L}_{z,r}
. In general, we say that
z
is
(k,\epsilon,r)
-quotient cylindrical if it is
(k,\epsilon,r)
-quotient cylindrical regarding some
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{m}_{k}(\Gamma)
.

By using the Lojasiewicz inequality in
[
FL25b
, Theorem 8.9]
, we can argue in the same way as Proposition
5.1
to prove the following summability result.

Proposition 5.27
.

For any
0<\epsilon\leq\epsilon(n,Y)
,
\alpha\in(1/4,1)
, and
\delta\leq\delta(n,Y,\epsilon,\alpha)
, the following holds. Suppose

\displaystyle\left|\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{z}(s_{1})-\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{z}(s_{2})\right|<\delta,

for
0<s_{1}<s_{2}
, and for any
s\in[s_{1},s_{2}]
,
z
is
(k,\delta,\sqrt{s})
-quotient cylindrical regarding
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{m}_{k}(\Gamma)
. Then

\displaystyle\sum_{s_{1}\leq r_{j}=2^{-j}\leq s_{2}}\left|\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{z}(r_{j})-\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{z}(r_{j-1})\right|^{\alpha}<\epsilon.

Similar to Proposition
5.8
, we also have

Proposition 5.28
.

Suppose
z\in Z
is
(k,\delta,r)
-quotient cylindrical regarding
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{m}_{k}(\Gamma)
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{z}(\delta\bar{r}^{2})-\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{z}(\delta^{-1}r^{2})<\delta
for some
0<\bar{r}<r
. For any
\epsilon>0
, if
\delta\leq\delta(n,Y,\epsilon)
, then
z
is
(k,\epsilon,s)
-quotient cylindrical regarding
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{m}_{k}(\Gamma)
for any
s\in[\bar{r},r]
. In particular, if
z\in\mathcal{S}^{k}_{\mathrm{qc}}\setminus\mathcal{S}^{k-1}_{\mathrm{qc}}
is
(k,\delta,r)
-quotient cylindrical regarding
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{m}_{k}(\Gamma)
and
\delta\leq\delta(n,Y,\epsilon)
, then
z
is
uniformly
(k,\epsilon,r)
-quotient cylindrical regarding
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{m}_{k}(\Gamma)
, meaning that
z
is
(k,\epsilon,s)
-quotient cylindrical regarding
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{m}_{k}(\Gamma)
for any
s\in(0,r]
.

Next, we define the quotient cylindrical neck region and its packing measure as in Definitions
5.9
and
5.12
.

Definition 5.29

(Quotient cylindrical neck region)
.

Given
\delta>0
,
\mathfrak{c}\in(0,10^{-10n})
,
r>0
and
z\in Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
with
\mathfrak{t}(z)-2\delta^{-1}r^{2}\in\mathbb{I}^{-}
, we call a subset
\mathscr{N}\subset B^{*}(z,2r)
a
(k,\delta,\mathfrak{c},r)
-quotient cylindrical neck region regarding
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{m}_{k}(\Gamma)
if
\mathscr{N}=B^{*}(z,2r)\setminus B_{r_{x}}^{*}(\mathscr{C})
, where
\mathscr{C}\subset B^{*}(z,2r)
is a nonempty closed subset with
r_{x}:\mathscr{C}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
, satisfies:

(n1)
for any
x,y\in\mathscr{C}
,
d_{Z}(x,y)\geq\mathfrak{c}^{2}(r_{x}+r_{y});

(n2)
for all
x\in\mathscr{C}
,

\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{x}(\delta r^{2}_{x})-\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{x}(\delta^{-1}r^{2})<\delta;

(n3)
for each
x\in\mathscr{C}
and
\mathfrak{c}^{2}r_{x}\leq s\leq 2r
,
x
is
(k,\delta,r)
-quotient cylindrical regarding
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{m}_{k}(\Gamma)
with respect to
\mathcal{L}_{x,s};

(n4)
for each
x\in\mathscr{C}
and
\mathfrak{c}^{-5}r_{x}\leq s\leq r-d_{Z}(x,z)/2
, we have
\mathcal{L}_{x,s}\cap B^{*}(x,s)\subset B^{*}_{\mathfrak{c}s}(\mathscr{C})
and
\mathscr{C}\bigcap B^{*}(x,s)\subset B^{*}_{\mathfrak{c}s}(\mathcal{L}_{x,s})
.

Furthermore, we define the
packing measure

\mu
as

\mu:=\sum_{x\in\mathscr{C}^{+}}r_{x}^{k}\delta_{x}+\mathscr{H}^{k}|_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}.

Analogous to Proposition
5.11
, we obtain the following construction of the neck region. Note that in this setting, one must consider the entropy
\Theta_{n-m}(\Gamma)
instead.

Proposition 5.30
.

Given
\delta>0
and
\mathfrak{c}\in(0,10^{-10n})
and
x_{0}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{qc}}^{k}(m,\Gamma)
, we can find a constant
r_{0}>0
and
\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{C}_{0}\bigcup\mathscr{C}_{+}\subset B^{*}(x_{0},2r_{0})
with
r_{x}:\mathscr{C}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
which satisfies
r_{x}>0
on
\mathscr{C}_{+}
,
r_{x}=0
on
\mathscr{C}_{0}
, such that the following hold:

(i)

\mathscr{N}=B^{*}(x_{0},2r_{0})\setminus B^{*}_{r_{x}}(\mathscr{C})
is a
(k,\delta,\mathfrak{c},r_{0})
-quotient cylindrical neck region regarding
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{m}_{k}(\Gamma)
.

(ii)

\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{qc}}^{k}(m,\Gamma)\bigcap B^{*}(x_{0},r_{0})\subset\mathscr{C}_{0}
.

Now, one can proceed as in the previous subsection to establish the following result. The only difference lies in the proof of the analog of Proposition
5.16
(iv), where a version of Lemma
2.26
is required for
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{m}_{k}(\Gamma)
and points in the spine of
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{m}_{k}(\Gamma)
. This can, however, be readily obtained from
[
FL25a
, Lemma 8.5]
.

Proposition 5.31

(Ahlfors regularity)
.

Given
\epsilon>0
, if
\mathfrak{c}\leq\mathfrak{c}(n)
and
\delta\leq\delta(n,Y,\mathfrak{c},\epsilon)
, then for the
(k,\delta,r_{0})
-quotient cylindrical neck region
\mathscr{N}=B^{*}(z,2r_{0})\setminus B_{r_{x}}^{*}(\mathscr{C})
constructed in Proposition
5.30
, there exists a constant
C=C(n,Y,\mathfrak{c})>1
such that for any
x\in\mathscr{C}
and
r_{x}\leq s\leq r_{0}-d_{Z}(x,x_{0})/2
,

\displaystyle C^{-1}s^{k}\leq\mu(B^{*}(x,s))\leq Cs^{k}.

Moreover, we can find a countable collection of
\mathscr{H}^{k}
-measurable subsets
E_{i}\subset\mathscr{C}_{0}
such that
\mathscr{H}^{k}(\mathscr{C}_{0}\setminus\bigcup_{i}E_{i})=0
and for each
i
, there exists a bi-Lipschitz map
u_{i}:E_{i}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
, where
\mathbb{R}^{k}
is equipped with the standard Euclidean distance, such that

\displaystyle\sqrt{|\mathfrak{t}(x)-\mathfrak{t}(y)|}\leq\epsilon d_{Z}(x,y),\quad\forall x,y\in E_{i}.

Since
Z
has entropy bounded below by
-Y
, there are at most
C(n,Y)
conjugacy classes for the group
\Gamma
. Therefore, by combining Propositions
5.30
and
5.31
, we obtain the following:

Theorem 5.32
.

For any
k\in\{0,1,\ldots,n-2\}
,
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{qc}}^{k}
is horizontally parabolic
k
-rectifiable with respect to
d_{Z}
-distance.

Similar to Corollary
5.23
, we also have

Corollary 5.33
.

Each connected component of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{qc}}^{2}
is contained in a time slice.

6
Analysis of singularities in dimension four

In this section, we prove a neck decomposition theorem for noncollapsed Ricci flow limit spaces in dimension four. As an application, we establish the rectifiability and derive a sharp volume estimate for the singular set. Also, we derive the
L^{1}
-curvature bounds for four-dimensional closed Ricci flows.

Throughout this section, we fix a noncollapsed Ricci flow limit space
(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})
, which is obtained as a pointed Gromov-Hausdorff limit of a sequence in
\mathcal{M}(4,Y,T)
. As before, we set

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}:=[-0.98T,0]\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{I}^{-}:=(-0.98T,0].

Moreover, the regular part is given by a Ricci flow spacetime
(\mathcal{R},\mathfrak{t},\partial_{\mathfrak{t}},g^{Z})
. For simplicity, we use
B^{*}(x,r)
instead of
B_{Z}^{*}(x,r)
for the metric balls in
Z
with respect to
d_{Z}
.

6.1
Overview of four-dimensional singularities

Recall that the singular set
\mathcal{S}\subset Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
has the following stratification:

\mathcal{S}^{0}\subset\mathcal{S}^{1}\subset\mathcal{S}^{2}=\mathcal{S}.

Given a point
z\in\mathcal{S}
, we consider the following cases.

Case A
:
z\in\mathcal{S}^{2}\setminus\mathcal{S}^{1}
.

In this case, there exists a tangent flow
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
at
z
, which is
2
-symmetric, but not
3
-symmetric. Let
(\mathcal{R}^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime},\partial_{\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}},g^{Z^{\prime}}_{t})
denote its regular part. Then
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0)}
has vanishing Ricci curvature or splits off an
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. By the classification of all
3
-dimensional Ricci shrinkers (see
[
Ham93
,
Per02
,
Nab10
,
NW08
,
CCZ08
]
), we conclude that

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{-1},g^{Z^{\prime}}_{-1}\right)=S^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2},\,\,\mathbb{RP}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\text{ or }\mathbb{R}^{4}/\Gamma.

Thus,
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
is isometric to
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{2}
,
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{2}_{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{2})
or
\mathbb{R}^{4}/\Gamma\times\mathbb{R}
. In the first two cases, the tangent flow at
z
is unique. We therefore decompose

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{2}\setminus\mathcal{S}^{1}=\left(\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\mathrm{qc}}\setminus\mathcal{S}^{1}_{\mathrm{qc}}\right)\sqcup\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\mathrm{F}},

where
\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\mathrm{F}}\subset\mathcal{S}^{2}
denotes the set of points for which at least one tangent flow is isometric to
\mathbb{R}^{4}/\Gamma\times\mathbb{R}
.

Case B
:
z\in\mathcal{S}^{1}\setminus\mathcal{S}^{0}
.

In this case, there exists a tangent flow
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
at
z
, which is
1
-symmetric, but not
2
-symmetric. Arguing as in Case A, we obtain

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{-1},g^{Z^{\prime}}_{-1}\right)=S^{3}/\Gamma\times\mathbb{R}\text{ or }(S^{2}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\mathbb{R})\times\mathbb{R}.

Hence,
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
is isometric to
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{1}_{1}(\Gamma)
or
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{2}_{1}(\mathbb{Z}_{2})
. In particular, any tangent flow at
z
is unique, and we conclude that

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{1}\setminus\mathcal{S}^{0}=\mathcal{S}^{1}_{\mathrm{qc}}\setminus\mathcal{S}^{0}_{\mathrm{qc}}.

Case C
:
z\in\mathcal{S}^{0}
.

In this case, any tangent flow
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
at
z
is either a quasi-static cone, so that
Z^{\prime}_{(-\infty,t_{a}]}
is isometric to
\mathbb{R}^{4}/\Gamma\times(-\infty,t_{a}]
for some arrival time
t_{a}\in[0,\infty)
, or else its regular part
\left(\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{-1},g^{Z^{\prime}}_{-1}\right)
has nonvanishing Ricci curvature and does not split off any line.

We claim that in the former case, one must have
t_{a}=0
. Indeed, suppose
t_{a}>0
and
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
arises as the
\hat{C}^{\infty}
-limit of a sequence

\displaystyle\left(Z,r_{i}^{-1}d_{Z},z,r_{i}^{-2}(\mathfrak{t}-\mathfrak{t}(z))\right),\quad r_{i}\to 0.

Then we can choose another sequence
s_{i}\to 0
so that

\displaystyle\left(Z,(r_{i}s_{i})^{-1}d_{Z},z,(r_{i}s_{i})^{-2}(\mathfrak{t}-\mathfrak{t}(z))\right)

converges to
\mathbb{R}^{4}/\Gamma\times\mathbb{R}
, contradicting the definition of
\mathcal{S}^{0}
.

Next, we prove

Proposition 6.1
.

\mathcal{S}^{0}
is a countable set.

Proof.

Recall that we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{0}=\bigcup_{\epsilon\in\mathbb{Q}\cap(0,1)}\bigcap_{0<r<\epsilon}\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,0}_{r,\epsilon}.

To finish the proof, it suffices to show that
S_{\epsilon}:=\bigcap_{0<r<\epsilon}\mathcal{S}^{\epsilon,0}_{r,\epsilon}
is countable for any
\epsilon>0
.

For any
z\in Z
, we define the entropy at
z
by

\displaystyle{\@index{\@indexphrase{$\mathcal{N}_{z}(0)$}}}\mathcal{N}_{z}(0):=\lim_{\tau\searrow 0}\mathcal{N}_{z}(\tau).

This limit exists because
\mathcal{N}_{z}(\tau)
is nonpositive and nonincreasing with respect to
\tau
.

Claim 6.2
.

For any
z\in S_{\epsilon}
, there exists a constant
\delta>0
such that there is no point
x\in S_{\epsilon}\bigcap B^{*}(z,\delta)
satisfying
\left\lvert\mathcal{N}_{x}(0)-\mathcal{N}_{z}(0)\right\rvert<\delta
.

Suppose, for contradiction, that the claim fails. Then there exists a sequence
x_{j}\in S_{\epsilon}
such that
x_{j}\to z
and
\mathcal{N}_{x_{j}}(0)\to\mathcal{N}_{z}(0)
. Define
r_{j}:=d_{Z}(x_{j},z)
, and let
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
be a subsequential limit of
\left(Z,r_{j}^{-1}d_{Z},z,r_{j}^{-2}(\mathfrak{t}-\mathfrak{t}(z))\right)
. It is clear from
[
FL25a
, Lemma 7.2]
that
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
is a Ricci shrinker space, and

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{z^{\prime}}(\tau)=\mathcal{N}_{z}(0),\quad\text{for all}\quad\tau>0.

(6.1)

We assume
x_{j}
converge to
x^{\prime}\in Z^{\prime}
so that
d_{Z^{\prime}}(z^{\prime},x^{\prime})=1
. For any
\tau>0
, it follows from
[
FL25a
, Lemma 7.2]
again and our assumption that

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{x^{\prime}}(\tau)=\lim_{j\to\infty}\mathcal{N}_{x_{j}}(\tau r_{j}^{2})\leq\lim_{j\to\infty}\mathcal{N}_{x_{j}}(0)=\mathcal{N}_{z}(0).

Therefore, by
[
FL25a
, Lemma D.3]
and (
6.1
), we deduce that
x^{\prime}
lies on the spine of
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
. By
[
FL25a
, Propositions D.5, D.8]
,
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
is
1
-splitting, a static cone, or a quasi-static cone. Since
z\in S_{\epsilon}
, it must be a quasi-static cone. In particular,
\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}(x^{\prime})\neq 0
.

If
\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}(x^{\prime})>0
, then for
s_{j}:=\sqrt{|\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}(x^{\prime})|}r_{j}\epsilon
and large
j
,

\displaystyle\left(Z,s_{j}^{-1}d_{Z},z,s_{j}^{-2}(\mathfrak{t}-\mathfrak{t}(z))\right)

is
\epsilon
-close to
\mathbb{R}^{4}/\Gamma\times\mathbb{R}
, contradicting the assumption that
z\in S_{\epsilon}
. Similarly, if
\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}(x^{\prime})<0
, then for large
j
and
s_{j}
as above,

\displaystyle\left(Z,s_{j}^{-1}d_{Z},x_{j},s_{j}^{-2}(\mathfrak{t}-\mathfrak{t}(x_{j}))\right)

is
\epsilon
-close to
\mathbb{R}^{4}/\Gamma\times\mathbb{R}
, again contradicting
x_{j}\in S_{\epsilon}
. Hence, Claim
6.2
follows.

Now, define a map
I:S_{\epsilon}\to Z\times\mathbb{R}
by

\displaystyle I(z)=\left(z,\mathcal{N}_{z}(0)\right).

It is clear that
I
is injective. By Claim
6.2
, for each
z_{0}\in S_{\epsilon}
, there exists
\delta_{0}>0
such that

\displaystyle\mathrm{image}(I)\bigcap B_{P}\left(I(z_{0}),\delta_{0}\right)=\{I(z_{0})\},

where
B_{p}
denotes the metric ball in
Z\times\mathbb{R}
with respect to the product metric
d_{Z}\times d_{E}
. Since
Z
is separable by
[
FL25a
, Theorem 1.3]
, so is
Z\times\mathbb{R}
. Hence,
\mathrm{image}(I)
is countable, and therefore
S_{\epsilon}
is also countable.

This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 6.3
.

By the same argument, the conclusion that
\mathcal{S}^{0}
is countable holds for noncollapsed Ricci flow limit spaces in all dimensions.

6.2
Flat neck regions

In this subsection, we consider the model space:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{a}(\Gamma)=\mathbb{R}^{4}/\Gamma\times(-\infty,a],

where
a\in[0,\infty]
is a constant, and
\Gamma\leqslant\mathrm{O}(4)
is a nontrivial finite group acting freely on
S^{3}
. Since
Z
has entropy bounded below by
-Y
, there are at most
C(Y)
such
\Gamma
, up to conjugacy, in
\mathrm{O}(4)
. Note that
\mathcal{F}^{a}(\Gamma)
is equipped with the spacetime distance
d^{*}_{\mathcal{F}}
, defined with respect to a spacetime distance constant
\epsilon_{0}=\epsilon_{0}(4,Y)=\epsilon_{0}(Y)
. We use
B^{*}_{\mathcal{F}^{a}(\Gamma)}(\cdot,\cdot)
to denote the
d^{*}
-balls in
\mathcal{F}^{a}(\Gamma)
. We also define the base point
p^{*}=([\vec{0}^{4}],0)
, where
[\vec{0}^{4}]
is the image of
\vec{0}^{4}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
to
\mathbb{R}^{4}/\Gamma
.

Next, we consider the quantitative closeness.

Definition 6.4
.

A point
z\in Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
is called
(\epsilon,r)
-close
to
\mathcal{F}^{a}(\Gamma)
if
\mathfrak{t}(z)-\epsilon^{-1}r^{2}\in\mathbb{I}^{-}
and

\displaystyle(Z,r^{-1}d_{Z},z,r^{-2}(\mathfrak{t}-\mathfrak{t}(z)))\quad\text{is $\epsilon$-close to}\quad\left(\mathcal{F}^{a}(\Gamma),d^{*}_{\mathcal{F}},p^{*},\mathfrak{t}\right)\quad\text{over}\quad[-\epsilon^{-1},\min\{\epsilon^{-1},a\}].

Let
\tilde{\phi}
be an
\epsilon
-map from Definition
2.13
, which is from
B_{\mathcal{F}^{a}(\Gamma)}^{*}(p^{*},\epsilon^{-1})\bigcap\mathcal{F}^{a}(\Gamma)_{[-\epsilon^{-1},\min\{\epsilon^{-1},a\}]}
to
Z_{[\mathfrak{t}(x)-\epsilon^{-1}r^{2},\mathfrak{t}(x)+\min\{\epsilon^{-1},a\}r^{2}]}
. Then, we define
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{x,r}:=\tilde{\phi}\left([\vec{0}^{4}]\times[-\epsilon^{-1},\min\{\epsilon^{-1},a\}]\right),

and we say that
x
is
(\epsilon,r)
-close to
\mathcal{F}^{a}(\Gamma)

with respect to
\mathcal{L}_{x,r}
.

Parallel to cylindrical or quotient cylindrical neck regions, we introduce the notion of
flat neck regions
, which, together with quotient cylindrical neck regions, plays a central role in the decomposition of Ricci flow limit spaces in dimension
4
.

Definition 6.5

(Flat neck region)
.

Given constants
\delta>0
,
\mathfrak{c}\in(0,10^{-40})
,
r>0
and
z\in Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
with
\mathfrak{t}(z)-2\delta^{-1}r^{2}\in\mathbb{I}^{-}
, we call a subset
\mathscr{N}\subset B^{*}(z,2r)
a
(\delta,\mathfrak{c},r)
-flat neck region regarding
\mathcal{F}^{0}(\Gamma)
if
\mathscr{N}=B^{*}(z,2r)\setminus B_{r_{x}}^{*}(\mathscr{C})
, where
\mathscr{C}\subset B^{*}(z,2r)
is a nonempty closed subset with
r_{x}:\mathscr{C}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
, satisfies
:

(n1)
for any
x,y\in\mathscr{C}
,
d_{Z}(x,y)\geq\mathfrak{c}^{2}(r_{x}+r_{y});

(n2)
for all
x\in\mathscr{C}
,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{x}(\delta r^{2}_{x})-\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{x}(\delta^{-1}r^{2})<\delta;

(n3)
for each
x\in\mathscr{C}
and
\mathfrak{c}^{2}r_{x}\leq s\leq 2r
,
x
is
(\delta,s)
-close to
\mathcal{F}^{0}(\Gamma)
with respect to
\mathcal{L}_{x,s};

(n4)
for each
x\in\mathscr{C}
and
\mathfrak{c}^{-5}r_{x}\leq s\leq r-d_{Z}(x,z)/2
, we have
\mathcal{L}_{x,s}\bigcap B^{*,-}(x,s)\subset B^{*}_{\mathfrak{c}s}(\mathscr{C})
and
\mathscr{C}\bigcap B^{*,-}(x,s)\subset B^{*}_{\mathfrak{c}s}(\mathcal{L}_{x,s})
, where

\displaystyle B^{*,-}(x,s):=B^{*}(x,s)\bigcap\{y\in Z\mid\mathfrak{t}(y)\leq\mathfrak{t}(x)\}.

As before,
\mathscr{C}
is called the
center
of the flat neck region, and
r_{x}
is referred to as the
radius function
. We decompose
\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{C}_{0}\bigcup\mathscr{C}_{+}
, where
r_{x}>0
on
\mathscr{C}_{+}
and
r_{x}=0
on
\mathscr{C}_{0}
. In addition, the corresponding
packing measure
is defined as

\displaystyle{\@index{\@indexphrase{packing measure}}}\mu:=\sum_{x\in\mathscr{C}_{+}}r_{x}^{2}\delta_{x}+\mathscr{H}^{2}|_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}.

Next, we prove

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
\mathscr{N}\subset B^{*}(z,2r)
be a
(\delta,\mathfrak{c},r)
-flat neck region regarding
\mathcal{F}^{0}(\Gamma)
. If
\delta\leq\delta(Y)
, then for any
x\in\mathscr{C}
and
s\in(0,2r]
,

\displaystyle 0<c_{0}(Y)d_{Z}^{2}(x,y)\leq|\mathfrak{t}(x)-\mathfrak{t}(y)|\leq d_{Z}^{2}(x,y)

for any
y\in B^{*,-}(x,s)\cap\mathscr{C}
. In particular,
\mathscr{C}_{0}
is vertically parabolic
2
-rectifiable.

Proof.

The second inequality is obvious, so we only prove the first one.

We set
s_{0}=d_{Z}(x,y)
. Then it is clear that
s\geq s_{0}\geq\mathfrak{c}^{2}(r_{x}+r_{y})
. Then by
(n3)
of Definition
6.5
, we know that
x
is
(\delta,s_{0})
-close to
\mathcal{F}^{0}(\Gamma)
with respect to
\mathcal{L}_{x,s_{0}}
.

Since our model space is the static cone
\mathbb{R}^{4}/\Gamma\times\mathbb{R}_{-}
and
\mathfrak{t}(x)\geq\mathfrak{t}(y)
, it follows that for any
\epsilon>0
, if
\delta\leq\delta(Y,\epsilon)
and
d_{Z}(y,\mathcal{L}_{x,s_{0}})\geq\epsilon s_{0}
, then
y
is
(3,\epsilon,\epsilon^{2}s_{0})
-symmetric. However, this would imply—by condition
(n2)
of Definition
6.5
—that
y
is
(3,2\epsilon,s_{0})
-symmetric, leading to a contradiction.

Thus, if
\delta\leq\delta(Y,\epsilon)
, we can find
y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}_{x,s_{0}}
so that

\displaystyle d_{Z}(y,y^{\prime})\leq\epsilon s_{0},

which implies

\displaystyle|\mathfrak{t}(y)-\mathfrak{t}(y^{\prime})|\leq\epsilon^{2}s^{2}_{0}.

On the other hand, it follows from
[
FL25a
, Lemma 7.24]
that if
\delta
is sufficiently small,

\displaystyle|\mathfrak{t}(x)-\mathfrak{t}(y^{\prime})|\geq c(Y)d^{2}_{Z}(x,y^{\prime})>0.

Combining the above inequalities, we obtain

\displaystyle|\mathfrak{t}(x)-\mathfrak{t}(y)|\geq|\mathfrak{t}(x)-\mathfrak{t}(y^{\prime})|-|\mathfrak{t}(y)-\mathfrak{t}(y^{\prime})|\geq c(Y)(1-\epsilon)^{2}s_{0}^{2}-\epsilon^{2}s^{2}_{0}\geq c(Y)s_{0}^{2}/2,

provided that
\epsilon\leq\epsilon(Y)
is small.

The last conclusion follows from a standard covering argument, which completes the proof.
∎

In the following, we will always assume
\delta\leq\delta(Y)
so that Lemma
6.6
holds.

Proposition 6.7

(Ahlfors regularity—flat case)
.

Let
\mathscr{N}\subset B^{*}(z,2r)
be a
(\delta,\mathfrak{c},r)
-flat neck region. If
\mathfrak{c}\leq\mathfrak{c}(Y)
, then for any
x\in\mathscr{C}
and
r_{x}\leq s\leq r-d_{Z}(x,z)/2
,

\displaystyle C^{-1}(Y,\mathfrak{c})s^{2}\leq\mu(B^{*,-}(x,s))\leq\mu(B^{*}(x,s))\leq C(Y,\mathfrak{c})s^{2}.

(6.2)

Proof.

Note that by Definition
5.9
,
\mathscr{N}^{\prime}=\mathscr{N}\cap B^{*}(x,2s)
is also a
(\delta,\mathfrak{c},s)
-neck region with center
\mathscr{C}^{\prime}=\mathscr{C}\cap B^{*}(x,2s)
and the same radius function. Thus, we only prove (
6.2
) for the case
x=z\in\mathscr{C}
and
s=r
.

We choose a maximal cover
\{B^{*}(x_{i},r/8)\}_{1\leq i\leq N}
of
B^{*}(z,r)\cap\mathscr{C}
such that
x_{i}\in B^{*}(z,r)\cap\mathscr{C}
and
\{B^{*}(x_{i},r/16)\}
are pairwise disjoint. By Proposition
2.25
,
N\leq C(Y)
.

On
\mathbb{R}
, we set the parabolic ball
P(a,s^{\prime}):=\{b\in\mathbb{R}\mid d_{P}(b,a):=\sqrt{|b-a|}\leq s^{\prime}\}
. For each ball
B^{*}(x_{i},r/8)
, Lemma
6.6
applies. Thus, we have

\displaystyle\mu(B^{*}(x_{i},r/8))

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in B^{*}(x_{i},r/8)\cap\mathscr{C}_{+}}r_{y}^{2}+\mathscr{H}^{2}\left(B^{*}(x_{i},r/8)\cap\mathscr{C}_{0}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C(Y)\mathfrak{c}^{-2}\sum_{B^{*}(x_{i},r/8)\cap\mathscr{C}_{+}}\mathscr{H}_{P}^{2}\left(P(\mathfrak{t}(y),c(Y)\mathfrak{c}^{2}r_{y})\right)+C(Y)\mathscr{H}_{P}^{2}\left(\mathfrak{t}\left(B^{*}(x_{i},r/8)\cap\mathscr{C}_{0}\right)\right)\leq C(Y,\mathfrak{c})r^{2},

where we choose a small
c(Y)
so that
\{P(\mathfrak{t}(y),c(Y)\mathfrak{c}^{2}r_{y})\}_{y\in B^{*}(x_{i},r/8)\cap\mathscr{C}}\subset\mathbb{R}
are mutually disjoint. Moreover, we use
\mathscr{H}_{P}^{2}
to denote the
2
-Hausdorff measure on
\mathbb{R}
with respect to the parabolic distance
d_{P}
. Thus, we obtain
\mu(B^{*}(z,r))\leq C(Y,\mathfrak{c})r^{2}
.

On the other hand, we set
P^{-}(a,s^{\prime}):=\{b\in P(a,s^{\prime})\mid b\leq a\}
and claim that

\displaystyle P^{-}(\mathfrak{t}(z),c_{1}(Y)r)\subset\bigcup_{y\in B^{*,-}(z,r)\cap\mathscr{C}}\overline{P^{-}(\mathfrak{t}(y),\mathfrak{c}^{-5}r_{y})},

(6.3)

where
c_{1}:=\sqrt{c_{0}}/100
, and
c_{0}=c_{0}(Y)
is the constant from Lemma
6.6
.

Once (
6.3
) is established, it follows that

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle<C^{-1}(Y)r^{2}\leq\mathscr{H}_{P}^{2}\left(P^{-}(\mathfrak{t}(z),c_{1}r)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{y\in B^{*,-}(z,r)\cap\mathscr{C}_{+}}\mathscr{H}_{P}^{2}\left(\overline{P^{-}(\mathfrak{t}(y),\mathfrak{c}^{-5}r_{y})}\right)+\mathscr{H}_{P}^{2}\left(\mathfrak{t}\left(\mathscr{C}_{0}\bigcap B^{*,-}(z,r)\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathfrak{c}^{-10}\sum_{y\in B^{*,-}(z,r)\cap\mathscr{C}_{+}}r_{y}^{2}+\mathscr{H}^{2}\left(\mathscr{C}_{0}\bigcap B^{*,-}(z,r)\right)\leq\mathfrak{c}^{-10}\mu\left(B^{*,-}(z,r)\right).

Assume now, for contradiction, that (
6.3
) fails. Then there exists

\displaystyle a\in P^{-}(\mathfrak{t}(z),c_{1}(Y)r)\setminus\bigcup_{y\in B^{*,-}(z,r)\cap\mathscr{C}}\overline{P^{-}(\mathfrak{t}(y),\mathfrak{c}^{-5}r_{y})}.

For any
x\in\mathscr{C}\bigcap B^{*,-}(z,r)\bigcap Z_{[a,\mathfrak{t}(z)]}
, define
s_{x}=\sqrt{\mathfrak{t}(x)-a}
and set
\bar{s}:=\min_{x\in\mathscr{C}\bigcap B^{*}(z,r)\bigcap Z_{[a,\mathfrak{t}(z)]}}s_{x}>0
. By our choice of
c_{1}
and Lemma
6.6
, we can find
z_{0}\in\mathscr{C}\bigcap B^{*}(z,r/4)\bigcap Z_{[a,\mathfrak{t}(z)]}
with
\bar{s}=s_{z_{0}}
. In particular, we have
a+\bar{s}^{2}=\mathfrak{t}(z_{0})\leq\mathfrak{t}(z)
.

Since
\bar{s}\geq\mathfrak{c}^{-5}r_{z_{0}}
, we consider
\mathcal{L}_{z_{0},\bar{s}}
. Given that our model space is
\mathbb{R}^{4}/\Gamma\times\mathbb{R}_{-}
, we can find
z_{1}\in B^{*,-}(z_{0},\bar{s})\cap\mathcal{L}_{z_{0},\bar{s}}
so that
0<\mathfrak{t}(z_{1})-a<(1-c(Y))\bar{s}^{2}
. By
(n4)
of Definition
6.5
, there exists
z_{2}\in\mathscr{C}
so that
d_{Z}(z_{1},z_{2})\leq\mathfrak{c}\bar{s}
. If
\mathfrak{c}<\mathfrak{c}(Y)
, we have
z_{2}\in B^{*,-}(z,r)\cap\mathscr{C}
. Moreover,

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathfrak{t}(z_{2})-\mathfrak{t}(z_{1})\right\rvert\leq d^{2}_{Z}(z_{1},z_{2})\leq\mathfrak{c}^{2}\bar{s}^{2},

which implies that
0<\mathfrak{t}(z_{2})-a<(1-c(Y)+\mathfrak{c}^{2})\bar{s}^{2}<\bar{s}^{2}
—a contradiction.

Hence, we conclude that claim (
6.3
) must hold, completing the proof.
∎

6.3
Neck decomposition theorem

We begin this subsection by proving the following result:

Proposition 6.8
.

Suppose
z\in Z
is
(\delta,r)
-close to
\mathcal{F}^{a}(\Gamma)
with respect to
\mathcal{L}_{z,r}
. Then the following conclusions hold.

(i)

If
\delta\leq\delta(Y,\epsilon)
, there exists a constant
W
such that for any
x\in\mathcal{L}_{z,r}\cap B^{*}(z,\epsilon^{-1}r)
and
\tau\in[\epsilon r^{2},\epsilon^{-1}r^{2}]
,

\displaystyle\left\lvert\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{x}(\tau)-W\right\rvert<\epsilon.

(ii)

If
\delta\leq\delta(Y,\epsilon,\eta)
, then any point in
B^{*}_{\delta r}(\mathcal{L}_{z,r})\cap B^{*}(z,\epsilon^{-1}r)
is not
(3,\eta,s)
-symmetric for any
s\in[\epsilon r,\epsilon^{-1}r]
.

(iii)

If
\delta\leq\delta(Y,\epsilon)
and
x\in B^{*}_{\delta r}(\mathcal{L}_{z,r})\cap B^{*}(z,\epsilon^{-1}r)
is
(\delta,s)
-close to
\mathcal{F}^{0}(\Gamma^{\prime})
for some
s\in[\epsilon r,\epsilon^{-1}r]
, then
\Gamma^{\prime}=\Gamma
and

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{H}}\left(\mathcal{L}_{x,s}\cap B^{*}(x,\epsilon^{-1}s),\mathcal{L}_{z,r}\cap B^{*,-}(x,\epsilon^{-1}s)\right)<\epsilon s.

Proof.

We only prove (i), as (ii) and (iii) can be proved similarly by a limiting argument.

Without loss of generality, we assume that
\mathfrak{t}(z)=0
and
r=1
. Suppose the conclusion (l) fails. Then we can find a sequence of Ricci flow limit spaces
(Z^{l},d_{Z^{l}},\mathfrak{t}_{l})
, which is obtained as the limit of a sequence in
\mathcal{M}(4,Y,T_{l})
, and
z_{l}\in Z^{l}
such that
z_{l}
is
(l^{-2},1)
-close to
\mathcal{F}^{a_{l}}(\Gamma_{l})
with respect to
\mathcal{L}_{z_{l},1}
, but the conclusion fails.

After passing to a diagonal subsequence, we may assume that
a_{l}\to a
, each
(Z^{l},d_{Z^{l}},\mathfrak{t}_{l})
is given by a closed Ricci flow, and
\Gamma_{l}=\Gamma
for all
l
. By our assumption, we have

\displaystyle(Z^{l},d_{Z^{l}},z_{l},\mathfrak{t}_{l})\xrightarrow[l\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\hat{C}^{\infty}\hskip 8.19447pt}\left(\mathcal{F}^{a}(\Gamma),d^{*}_{\mathcal{F}},p^{*},\mathfrak{t}\right).

Then, it is clear that
\mathcal{L}_{z_{l},1}
converge to the spine
[\vec{0}^{4}]\times(-\infty,a]
on which
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}
is constant.

Thus, we obtain a contradiction by Proposition
4.3
and monotonicity if
l
is sufficiently large.
∎

The main result of this subsection is the following neck decomposition theorem in dimension
4
.

Theorem 6.9

(Neck decomposition theorem)
.

For any constants
\delta>0
,
\eta>0
and
k\in\{1,2\}
, if
\zeta\leq\zeta(Y,\delta,\eta)
, then the following holds.

Given
z_{0}\in Z
with
\mathfrak{t}(z_{0})-2\zeta^{-2}r_{0}^{2}\in\mathbb{I}^{-}
, we have the decomposition
:

\displaystyle B^{*}(z_{0},r_{0})\subset\bigcup_{a}\big(\mathscr{N}^{\prime}_{a}\bigcap B^{*}(x_{a},r_{a})\big)\bigcup\bigcup_{b}B^{*}(x_{b},r_{b})\bigcup S^{k,\delta,\eta},

\displaystyle S^{k,\delta,\eta}\subset\bigcup_{a}\big(\mathscr{C}_{0,a}\bigcap B^{*}(x_{a},r_{a})\big)\bigcup\tilde{S}^{k,\delta,\eta},

with the following properties
:

(a)

For each
a
,
\mathscr{N}_{a}=B^{*}(x_{a},2r_{a})\setminus B^{*}_{r_{x}}(\mathscr{C}_{a})
is either a
(k,\delta,\mathfrak{c},r_{a})
-quotient cylindrical neck region or a
(\delta,\mathfrak{c},r_{a})
-flat neck region, where
\mathfrak{c}=\mathfrak{c}(Y)
if
k=2
and
\mathfrak{c}=\mathfrak{c}(Y,\eta)
if
k=1
. In the former case, we set
\mathscr{N}_{a}^{\prime}=\mathscr{N}_{a};
in the latter,
\mathscr{N}_{a}^{\prime}
denotes the modified
\delta
-region associated with
\mathscr{N}_{a}
(see Definition
6.18
).

(b)

For each
b
, there exists a point in
B^{*}(x_{b},2r_{b})
which is
(k+1,\eta,r_{b})
-symmetric.

(c)

The following content estimates hold
:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\sum_{a}r_{a}^{2}+\sum_{b}r_{b}^{2}+\mathscr{H}^{2}(S^{2,\delta,\eta})\leq C(Y)r_{0}^{2}\quad&\text{if}\quad k=2,\\
\sum_{a}r_{a}+\sum_{b}r_{b}+\mathscr{H}^{1}(S^{1,\delta,\eta})\leq C(Y,\eta)r_{0}\quad&\text{if}\quad k=1.\end{cases}

(d)

\mathscr{H}^{k}(\tilde{S}^{k,\delta,\eta})=0
.

Throughout the proof, we fix
z_{0}\in Z
and
r_{0}>0
such that
\mathfrak{t}(z_{0})-2\zeta^{-2}r_{0}^{2}\in\mathbb{I}^{-}
. We will only consider balls
B^{*}(x,r)
with
x\in B^{*}(z_{0},4r_{0})
and
r\leq r_{0}-d_{Z}(x,z_{0})/4
.

Definition 6.10

(Pinching set)
.

We define

\displaystyle\bar{W}:=\inf_{z\in B^{*}(z_{0},4r_{0})}\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{z}(\zeta^{-2}r_{0}^{2}).

(6.4)

and introduce the pinching set

{\@index{\@indexphrase{$\mathcal{P}_{\zeta,r}$}}}\mathcal{P}_{\zeta,r}:=\{y\in B^{*}(z_{0},4r_{0})\mid\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{y}(\zeta^{2}r^{2})\leq\bar{W}+\zeta\}.

For any
x\in B^{*}(z_{0},4r_{0})
with
r\leq r_{0}-d_{Z}(x,z_{0})/4
, we define the localized pinching set:

{\@index{\@indexphrase{$\mathcal{P}_{\zeta,r}(x)$}}}\mathcal{P}_{\zeta,r}(x):=\mathcal{P}_{\zeta,r}\bigcap B^{*}(x,2.5r),

and set

{\@index{\@indexphrase{$t_{p}(x,r)$}}}t_{p}(x,r):=\sup_{y\in\mathcal{P}_{\zeta,r}(x)}\mathfrak{t}(y).

(6.5)

Definition 6.11

(Different types of balls)
.

For any constants
\delta>0
,
\eta>0
,
\mathfrak{c}\in(0,10^{-40})
and
\beta>0
, we define the following different types of balls
B^{*}(x,r)
with
x\in B^{*}(z_{0},4r_{0})
and
r\leq r_{0}-d_{Z}(x,z_{0})/4
.

(a)

A ball
B^{*}(x_{a},r_{a})
is called an
a
-ball if
\mathscr{N}_{a}=B^{*}(x_{a},2r_{a})\setminus B^{*}_{r_{x}}(\mathscr{C}_{a})
is either a
(k,\delta,\mathfrak{c},r_{a})
-quotient cylindrical neck region or a
(\delta,\mathfrak{c},r_{a})
-flat neck region.

(b)

A ball
B^{*}(x_{b},r_{b})
is called a
b
-ball if there exists a
(k+1,\eta,r_{b})
-symmetric point in
B^{*}(x_{b},2r_{b})
.

(c)

A ball
B^{*}(x_{c},r_{c})
is called a
c
-ball if it is not a
b
-ball and satisfies
\mathcal{P}_{\zeta,r_{c}}(x_{c})\cap B^{*}(x_{c},r_{c})\neq\emptyset
and

\displaystyle\left\lvert B_{3\beta r_{c}}^{*}\left(\mathcal{P}_{\zeta,r_{c}}(x_{c})\right)\right\rvert>D\beta^{7-k}r_{c}^{6},

(6.6)

where
D=D(Y)
is a positive constant to be determined in Lemma
6.12
.

(d)

A ball
B^{*}(x_{d},r_{d})
is called a
d
-ball if it is not a
b
-ball and satisfies
\mathcal{P}_{\zeta,r_{d}}(x_{d})\cap B^{*}(x_{d},r_{d})\neq\emptyset
and

\displaystyle 0<\left\lvert B_{3\beta r_{d}}^{*}\left(\mathcal{P}_{\zeta,r_{d}}(x_{d})\right)\right\rvert\leq D\beta^{7-k}r_{d}^{6}.

(6.7)

(e)

A ball
B^{*}(x_{e},r_{e})
is called an
e
-ball if
\mathcal{P}_{\zeta,r_{e}}(x_{e})\cap B^{*}(x_{e},r_{e})=\emptyset
.

By our definition, every ball
B^{*}(x,r)
with
x\in B^{*}(z_{0},4r_{0})
and
r\leq r_{0}-d_{Z}(x,z_{0})/4
must belong to one of the categories:
b
-ball,
c
-ball,
d
-ball, or
e
-ball. However, it is possible that
B^{*}(x,r)
is both a
b
-ball and an
e
-ball.

For a
c
-ball, we first prove

Lemma 6.12
.

Given
\eta>0
,
\beta>0
,
k\in\{1,2\}
and
\epsilon>0
, if
\zeta\leq\zeta(Y,\eta,\epsilon,\beta)
and
B^{*}(x,r)
is a
c
-ball, then any point
y_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{\zeta,r}(x)
is either
(k,\epsilon,r)
-quotient cylindrical or
(\epsilon,r)
-close to
\mathcal{F}^{a}(\Gamma)
for some
a\geq 0
and
\Gamma\leqslant\mathrm{O}(4)
.

Proof.

Let
S:=\{y_{i}\}_{0\leq i\leq N}
be a maximal
\beta r
-separated subset of
\mathcal{P}_{\zeta,r}(x)
containing
y_{0}
. Since
B^{*}(x,r)
is not a
b
-ball, arguing as in the proof of Lemma
4.21
, we conclude that if
N\geq C(Y)\beta^{1-k}
, then
y_{0}
is either
(k,\epsilon,r)
-symmetric—which must in turn be
(k,\epsilon,r)
-quotient cylindrical—or
y_{0}
is
(\epsilon,r)
-close to
\mathcal{F}^{a}(\Gamma)
, provided that
\zeta\leq\zeta(Y,\eta,\epsilon,\beta)
.

If instead
N\leq C(Y)\beta^{1-k}
, then
\mathcal{P}_{\zeta,r}(x)
can be covered by at most
C(Y)\beta^{1-k}
balls of radius
\beta r
. Combining this with Proposition
2.25
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left\lvert B_{3\beta r}^{*}\left(\mathcal{P}_{\zeta,r}(x)\right)\right\rvert\leq D(Y)\beta^{7-k}r^{6}.

By (
6.6
), this leads to a contradiction.

Therefore, the proof is complete.
∎

In the setting of Lemma
6.12
, if there exists a point
y\in\mathcal{P}_{\zeta,r}(x)
that is
(k,\epsilon,r)
-quotient cylindrical regarding
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{m}_{k}(\Gamma)
, then, by rigidity, any other point
y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{\zeta,r}(x)
is also
(k,\epsilon,r)
-quotient cylindrical regarding
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{m}_{k}(\Gamma)
.

On the other hand, if one point
y\in\mathcal{P}_{\zeta,r}(x)
is
(k,\epsilon)
-close to
\mathcal{F}^{a}(\Gamma)
, then any other point
y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{\zeta,r}(x)
is
(k,\epsilon)
-close to
\mathcal{F}^{a^{\prime}}(\Gamma)
. In this case, we define the following time-function:

\displaystyle{\@index{\@indexphrase{$t_{s}(x,r)$}}}t_{s}(x,r):=\sup\left\{ar^{2}+\mathfrak{t}(y)\mid y\in\mathcal{P}_{\zeta,r}(x)\cap B^{*}(x,r)\text{ is $(\epsilon,r)$-close to }\mathcal{F}^{a}(\Gamma)\right\}.

(6.8)

It is clear that if
\zeta\leq\zeta(Y,\eta,\epsilon)
, we always have

\displaystyle t_{s}(x,r)\geq t_{p}(x,r),

(6.9)

where
t_{p}(x,r)
is the function in (
6.5
).

By Lemma
6.12
and the subsequent discussion, each
c
-ball is modeled either on a quotient cylinder or on a flat cone. We refer to the former as a
type-I

c
-ball and to the latter as a
type-II

c
-ball.

We now further decompose
c
-balls into neck regions according to their types. For type-I
c
-balls, the decomposition is similar to that in Proposition
5.11
.

Proposition 6.13

(Decomposition of type-I
c
-balls)
.

For
z\in B^{*}(z_{0},4r_{0})
and
s\leq r_{0}-d_{Z}(z,z_{0})/4
, let
B^{*}(z,s)
be a type-I
c
-ball. For any constants
\delta>0
,
\eta>0
,
\mathfrak{c}\in(0,10^{-40})
and
\beta>0
, if
\zeta\leq\zeta(Y,\delta,\eta,\mathfrak{c},\beta)
, then we have the decomposition

B^{*}(z,2s)\subset\left(\mathscr{C}_{0}\bigcup\mathscr{N}\right)\bigcup\bigcup_{b}B^{*}(x_{b},r_{b})\bigcup\bigcup_{d}B^{*}(x_{d},r_{d})\bigcup\bigcup_{e}B^{*}(x_{e},r_{e})

satisfying

(i)

for each
b
,
B^{*}(x_{b},r_{b})
is a
b
-ball with
r_{b}\leq\mathfrak{c}^{5}s;

(ii)

for each
d
,
B^{*}(x_{d},r_{d})
is a
d
-ball with
r_{d}\leq\mathfrak{c}^{5}s;

(iii)

for each
e
,
B^{*}(x_{e},r_{e})
is an
e
-ball with
r_{e}\leq\mathfrak{c}^{5}s;

(iv)

\displaystyle\mathscr{N}:=B^{*}(z,2s)\setminus\left(\mathscr{C}_{0}\bigcup\bigcup_{b}B^{*}(x_{b},r_{b})\bigcup\bigcup_{d}B^{*}(x_{d},r_{d})\bigcup\bigcup_{e}B^{*}(x_{e},r_{e})\right)
is a
(k,\delta,\mathfrak{c},s)
-quotient neck region regarding some
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{m}_{k}(\Gamma);

(v)

the following content estimate holds:

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}^{k}\left(\mathscr{C}_{0}\bigcap B^{*}(z,3s/2)\right)+\sum_{x_{b}\in B^{*}(z,3s/2)}r_{b}^{k}+\sum_{x_{d}\in B^{*}(z,3s/2)}r_{d}^{k}+\sum_{x_{e}\in B^{*}(z,3s/2)}r_{e}^{k}\leq C(Y,\mathfrak{c})s^{k}.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we assume
s=1
. In the proof, we choose a small parameter
\epsilon\ll\delta
and assume that Lemma
6.12
holds for constants
\epsilon
,
\eta
,
\beta
and
\zeta
by default. Moreover, we may assume
\delta\ll\mathfrak{c}
and define
\gamma:=\mathfrak{c}^{5}
.

Since
B^{*}(z,1)
is a type-I
c
-ball, it follows from Lemma
6.12
that there exists
q\in\mathcal{P}_{\zeta,1}(z)\cap B^{*}(z,1)
such that
q
is
(k,\epsilon,1)
-quotient cylindrical regarding
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{m}_{k}(\Gamma)
with respect to
\mathcal{L}_{q,1}
. We define

\displaystyle L^{1}:=\mathcal{L}_{q,1}\bigcap B^{*}(z,2).

Choose a maximal
2\mathfrak{c}^{2}\gamma
-separated set
\{x_{f^{1}}\}\subset L^{1}
. In particular,
\{B^{*}(x_{f^{1}},\mathfrak{c}^{2}\gamma)\}
are pairwise disjoint and

\displaystyle L^{1}\subset\bigcup_{f^{1}}B^{*}(x_{f^{1}},2\mathfrak{c}^{2}\gamma).

According to the type of
B^{*}(x_{f^{1}},\gamma)
, we write

\displaystyle L^{1}\subset\bigcup_{b^{1}}B^{*}(x_{b^{1}},\gamma)\bigcup\bigcup_{c^{1}}B^{*}(x_{c^{1}},\gamma)\bigcup\bigcup_{d^{1}}B^{*}(x_{d^{1}},\gamma)\bigcup\bigcup_{e^{1}}B^{*}(x_{e^{1}},\gamma).

We set

\displaystyle\mathscr{N}^{1}:=B^{*}(z,2)\setminus\left(\bigcup_{b^{1}}B^{*}(x_{b^{1}},\gamma)\bigcup\bigcup_{c^{1}}B^{*}(x_{c^{1}},\gamma)\bigcup\bigcup_{d^{1}}B^{*}(x_{d^{1}},\gamma)\bigcup\bigcup_{e^{1}}B^{*}(x_{e^{1}},\gamma)\right).

Moreover, we define
\mathscr{C}^{1}:=\{x_{b^{1}},x_{c^{1}},x_{d^{1}},x_{e^{1}}\}
with radius
r_{x}:\equiv\gamma
.

Claim 6.14
.

If
\epsilon\leq\epsilon(Y,\delta)
and
\zeta\leq\zeta(Y,\delta,\eta,\epsilon,\mathfrak{c},\beta)
, then
\mathscr{N}^{1}
is a
(k,\delta,\mathfrak{c},1)
-quotient cylindrical neck region regarding
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{m}_{k}(\Gamma)
.

Indeed, property
(n1)
follows from the construction. For
(n2)
, since
q\in\mathcal{P}_{\zeta,1}(z)
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{q}(\tau)-\bar{W}\right|\leq\delta^{4},\quad\forall\tau\in\left[(\delta\gamma)^{2},\delta^{-2}\right],

if
\zeta
is sufficiently small. Moreover, it follows from the generalization of Proposition
5.3
in the quotient cylindrical case that if
\epsilon
is small, then for any
x\in\mathcal{L}_{q,1}\bigcap B^{*}(z,2)
,

\displaystyle\left|\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{x}(\tau)-\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{q}(\tau)\right|\leq\delta^{4},\quad\forall\tau\in\left[(\delta\gamma)^{2},\delta^{-2}\right].

Combining the above two inequalities, we have for any
x\in\mathcal{L}_{q,1}\bigcap B^{*}(z,2)
,

\displaystyle\left|\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{x}(\tau)-\bar{W}\right|\leq\delta^{3},\quad\forall\tau\in\left[(\delta\gamma)^{2},\delta^{-2}\right],

(6.10)

which establishes
(n2)
. Moreover,
(n3)
and
(n4)
follow from our construction. This completes the proof of Claim
6.14
.

Each
c
-ball
B^{*}(x_{c^{1}},\gamma)
must be of type-I, since otherwise the model space at scale
\gamma
is a flat cone, which contradicts (
6.10
) by Proposition
5.28
. Thus, we can repeat the above process. Specifically, for each
c
-ball
B^{*}(x_{c^{1}},\gamma)
, we can find
